The two-dimensional one-component plasma is hyperuniform
Thomas Leblé

Abstract
We prove that at all positive temperatures in the bulk of a classical two-dimensional one-component plasma (also called Coulomb or log-gas, or jellium) the variance of the number of particles in large disks grows (strictly) more slowly than the area. In other words the system is hyperuniform.



1
Introduction

Let
N\geq 1
be an integer, let
\Sigma_{N}
be the disk of center
0
and radius
\sqrt{\frac{N}{\pi}}
, let
\mathrm{X}_{N}:=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
denote a
N
-tuple of points in
\Sigma_{N}
and let
\mathbf{X}_{N}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}
be the associated atomic measure. We let
\mathbf{f}_{N}
be the signed “fluctuation” measure on
\Sigma_{N}
defined by
\mathbf{f}_{N}:=\mathbf{X}_{N}-\mathbf{m}_{0}
, where
\mathbf{m}_{0}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\Sigma_{N}
. We think of
\mathbf{X}_{N}
as a collection of
point particles
in
\Sigma_{N}
and of
\mathbf{m}_{0}
as the
background measure
. We define the
logarithmic interaction energy

\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})
as:

\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N}):=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{(x,y)\in\Sigma_{N}\times\Sigma_{N},x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y).

(1.1)

Let
\beta
be a positive real number that will be fixed throughout the paper. We define a probability density
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
on the space of
N
-tuples of points in
\Sigma_{N}
by setting:

\mathtt{d}\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}(\mathrm{X}_{N}):=\frac{1}{\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N},

(1.2)

with a normalizing constant
\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}:=\int_{\Sigma_{N}\times\dots\times\Sigma_{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}
called the
partition function
.
Here and below,
\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on the Cartesian product
\Sigma_{N}\times\dots\times\Sigma_{N}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{N}
. The probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
is the
canonical Gibbs measure of the two-dimensional one-component plasma
(2DOCP) at
inverse temperature

\beta
. We denote the expectation under
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
by
\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}
.

Let
\Omega
some (Borel measurable) subset of
\Sigma_{N}
. We denote by
\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)
the number of points of
\mathbf{X}_{N}
in
\Omega
and by
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)
the
discrepancy
(of
\mathbf{X}_{N}
) in
\Omega
, defined by:

\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega):=\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)-\mathbf{m}_{0}(\Omega).

(1.3)

By construction we have
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Sigma_{N})=0
, which corresponds to the fact that the system is globally neutral. However it cannot be perfectly locally neutral, and
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)
is meant to measure
charge fluctuations
in
\Omega
. The
number variance
in
\Omega
is defined as the variance under
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
of
\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)
, or equivalently of
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)
.

For all
x
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
R>0
, we let
\mathfrak{D}(x,R)
be the disk of center
x
and radius
R
.

1.1
Main result

Hyperuniformity of a system is defined by the fact that:

[
Tor18
, Section 1]

(…) the number variance of particles within a spherical observation window of radius
R
grows more slowly than the window volume in the large-
R
limit.

Our main conclusion is the following:

Theorem

(The 2DOCP is hyperuniform)
.

For each
N\geq 1
, let
x=x(N)
be a point in the bulk of
\Sigma_{N}
and let
R=R(N)
be such that
R(N)\to\infty
. Then the number variance in
\mathfrak{D}(x,R)
is
o(R^{2})
as
N\to\infty
.

We give a more precise statement in Section
1.5
, explaining what we mean by “the bulk of
\Sigma_{N}
” and specifying a quantitative upper bound on the number variance of the form
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R^{2}}{\log^{c}(R)}\right)
for some
c>0
.

Hyperuniformity of the 2DOCP
at all positive temperatures
is a forty year old prediction
1
1

1

Those results are rigorous to the extent that authors make use of so-called “clustering assumptions”, i.e. they assume properties of the two-point correlation function at large distances in order to derive certain identities (called “sum rules”) which, among other things, imply hyperuniformity. As explained in
[
Mar88
]
: “
The results obtained in this way are exact (i.e. do not follow from approximations), but not all of them are rigorously proven, in so far as some reasonable properties (e.g. the type of decay of the correlations) are assumed to hold a priori.
”. Unfortunately, obtaining mathematically rigorous statements about the large-distance properties of the 2DOCP’s two-point correlation function (for
\beta\neq 2
) is extremely challenging.

from statistical physics, see
[
MY80
,
Leb83
,
Mar88
,
JLM93
,
LWL00
]
(which use a different terminology), or
[
Tor18
]
again:
“OCP fluid phases at [all] temperatures must always be hyperuniform”
. In fact, the full physical prediction says not only that the number variance is negligible with respect to the area of the disk, but that it should even be
comparable to the perimeter
(
\mathcal{O}(R)
and not only
o(R^{2})
). We give here the first mathematical proof of hyperuniformity at all temperatures, however our upper bound on the number variance remains far from the conjectured sharp estimate.

1.2
The 2DOCP

The two-dimensional
one-component plasma
is a well-studied model of statistical physics. Besides the papers cited above, let us refer to
[
For10
, Chapter 15]
for a presentation of exact and approximate results. When defining the energy
\mathsf{F}_{N}
in (
1.1
) and the canonical Gibbs measure
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
in (
1.2
), we think of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
as the positions of point particles in
\Sigma_{N}
which all carry the same electric charge
+1
, and which are immersed in a uniform neutralizing background of constant density on
\Sigma_{N}
. The logarithmic potential is then the electrostatic interaction potential in dimension
2
. Instead of directly placing a background measure, one sometimes imposes an harmonic confining potential. This would not change our analysis, see Appendix
A
for a discussion.

Mathematical aspects of Coulomb gases

As mathematical objects, Coulomb gases (under various forms: one-, two- or three-dimensional, with one or two components…) have attracted much interest. Concerning the 2DOCP itself, topics that have been investigated in the last years alone are very diverse and include: lower bounds on the minimal distance between points
[
Ame18
]
, concentration inequalities for the empirical measure of the particles
[
CHM18
,
GZ19a
]
, upper bounds for the local density of points
[
LRY19
]
, generalizations to Riemannian manifolds
[
Ber19
]
, Wegner’s and clustering estimates
[
Tho22
]
… to quote only a few among many others. We refer to
[
Ser18
]
for an overview of motivations, ranging from constructive approximation to the study of the Quantum Hall Effect, via random matrix theory. The long-range and singular nature of the pairwise potential raises considerable analytic challenges.

In
[
LS18
,
BBNY19
,
Ser20
]
it was shown that the 2DOCP exhibits strong forms of rigidity at all scales and for all values of the temperature as far as fluctuations of
smooth
linear statistics are concerned. Regarding fluctuations of charges, i.e. the statistics of an indicator function,
[
AS21
]
(see also
[
Leb17
,
BBNY17
]
for weaker controls) imply that the discrepancy within a disk of radius
R
is
\mathcal{O}(R)
with high probability. Our goal here is improve such bounds to
o(R)
and to thus prove hyperuniformity.

The Ginibre ensemble

When the inverse temperature parameter
\beta
is equal to
2
, the probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
admits an interpretation as the joint law of the complex eigenvalues of a
N\times N
non-Hermitian random matrix, known as the (finite) Ginibre ensemble, after
[
Gin65
]
. The model belongs to the class of
determinantal processes
(see e.g.
[
HKPV06
, Section 4.3.7]
) and is amenable to exact computations. In that specific case, the standard deviation of the number of points in a disk of radius
R
is known: it scales like
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
(in accordance with predictions from physics), see
[
Shi06
]
and
[
OS08
]
. Thus for
\beta=2
our result is not new, and our bound is far from the optimal one.

The hierarchical model

In
[
Cha19
]
, S. Chatterjee studied Coulomb gases in dimensions
\{1,2,3\}
(see
[
GS20
]
for an extension to higher dimensions), giving sharp estimates on the number variance up to logarithmic corrections. His model is a
hierarchical Coulomb gas
, where the physical space is decomposed into a tree-like structure following an old recipe of Dyson. The hierarchical model has two built-in properties: conditional independence of sub-systems and a self-similar nature. A large part of the present analysis is devoted to finding approximate analogues of those features for the “true” (non-hierarchical) model.

1.3
Hyperuniformity

The term “hyperuniform(ity)” has been coined in the theoretical chemistry literature by S. Torquato (see
[
TS03
,
Tor18
]
for surveys), an alternative terminology due to J. Lebowitz is “superhomogeneous/ity”. As we already mentioned, a system is hyperuniform when the number variance in a large ball is asymptotically negligible with respect to the volume of said ball. Of course the definition of hyperuniformity needs an adaptation for finite systems, as one should take both the size of the system and of the “spherical window” to infinity. We refer to
[
Cos21
]
for a mathematical presentation of hyperuniformity and a survey of related results.

Examples of hyperuniform systems

For non-interacting random particles (forming a Bernoulli point process or a Poisson point process with constant intensity) the number variance grows exactly like the volume, hence hyperuniformity can be thought of as the property of systems that are “much more rigid” than independent particles regarding discrepancy at large scales. Examples of two-dimensional objects that are proven to be hyperuniform include: the Ginibre ensemble (as mentioned above) and its generalizations (see
[
CE19
]
for a recent result in connection with the Quantum Hall Effect), averaged and perturbed lattices (see
[
GS75
]
), the zeroes of random polynomials with i.i.d Gaussian coefficients (see
[
FH99
,
SZ08
]
)…

In some cases (see
[
Tor18
, Section 5.3.2]
) the number variance grows as the perimeter (in fact that was initially chosen as the definition of hyperuniformity in
[
TS03
]
but is now called “class I hyperuniformity”), which is the slowest possible growth (see
[
Bec87
]
). The 2DOCP is predicted to be class I hyperuniform at all temperatures, with good tails on the probability of large charge fluctuations, as we explain next.

The Jancovici-Lebowitz-Manificat law

In
[
JLM93
]
, Jancovici, Lebowitz and Manificat made precise predictions concerning the probability of observing large charge fluctuations within the 2DOCP (and its three-dimensional version). Their statement is significantly more precise
2
2

2

Tail estimates as in (
1.4
) readily imply the strongest type of hyperuniformity (Type I).
than hyperuniformity, as they argue that for all
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
:

\mathbb{P}\left[\text{Discrepancy of size }R^{\alpha}\text{ in a disk of radius }R\right]\sim\exp(-R^{\varphi(\alpha)})\quad\textbf{(“JLM law”)}

(1.4)

where the rate
\varphi(\alpha)>0
is an explicit piecewise affine function of
\alpha
. This was later checked for
\beta=2
through explicit computations, see
[
Shi06
,
FL21
]
, while the general case is open. Remarkably, although the original prediction of
[
JLM93
]
deals with Coulomb gases, it was first verified in
[
NSV08
]
for a different model, namely the zeros of the Gaussian Entire Function (see
[
GN18
]
for a survey of related results).

Fekete points and the low temperature regime

A situation of interest that we do not consider here is the case of energy minimizers, which formally corresponds to taking
\beta=+\infty
i.e. a zero temperature. A long-standing conjecture posits that minimizers of
\mathsf{F}_{N}
form a lattice as
N\to\infty
and in that regard Fekete points should exhibit excellent rigidity properties at finite
N
. For studies of such questions we refer to
[
AOC12
,
Ame17
]
and, in a different line of work, to
[
NS15
, Thm. 3]
. See also
[
AR22
,
MR22
]
or the
\beta
-dependent statements of
[
AS21
]
for a more general “low-temperature” regime where
\beta
is large but not necessarily infinite.

1.4
Open questions

Besides the obvious natural goal of obtaining the sharp number variance estimate as well as proving the aforementioned “JLM laws”, let us mention three other open directions of research.

The 3DOCP.

The claims of
[
JLM93
]
are made in dimension
2

and

3
, moreover sharp hyperuniformity estimates were obtained for the
hierarchical
model in dimension
3
in
[
Cha19
]
, it is thus natural to ask whether one can prove hyperuniformity for the 3DOCP.
Unfortunately, in dimension
3
there is no (known) value of
\beta
for which the model would be integrable and predictions could be tested. Some understanding of fluctuations of smooth linear statistics was recently obtained in
[
Ser20
]
but with considerable new difficulties compared to the two-dimensional case.

Riesz interactions.

The 2DOCP can be seen as a member of one-parameter family of two-dimensional systems called
Riesz gases
for which the interaction potential is taken as a certain power
|x-y|^{-\mathsf{s}}
of the distance (the case
\mathsf{s}=0
corresponds by convention to the logarithmic interaction). Rigidity properties of those systems depend on the value of
\mathsf{s}
as it governs both the level of repulsiveness at
0
and, most importantly, the long- or short-range nature of the interaction. Rough bounds on the discrepancies were given in
[
LS17
, Lemma 3.2]
in the infinite-volume limit, which say that the number variance in a disk of radius
R
grows at most as
R^{2+\mathsf{s}}
. We believe the sharp estimate to be
R^{1+\mathsf{s}}
and thus that two-dimensional Riesz gases should remain hyperuniform for
\mathsf{s}<1
. For one-dimensional systems, such questions were recently answered in
[
Bou21
]
.

High temperature regime.

There has been recent interest in studying the “high-temperature” regime where
\beta\to 0
as
N\to\infty
for Coulomb gases (and other related systems), see e.g.
[
AB19
,
Lam21
]
in the two-dimensional setting. Those results have put forward that the inverse temperature regime where
\beta\sim\frac{c}{N}
is the threshold that distinguishes between “Ginibre-like” (
c\to+\infty
) and “Poisson-like” (
c\to 0
) properties. It is thus tempting to ask whether the 2DOCP stays hyperuniform as long as
\beta N\to\infty
, and whether one can observe an interesting transition along
\beta=\frac{c}{N}
, as
c
moves down from
+\infty
to
0
.

1.5
Precise statement of the result, proof strategy and tools

Theorem 1
.

Let
\delta>0
be fixed. For all
N
and
R
large enough (both depending on
\beta
and
\delta
), for all
x
in
\Sigma_{N}
such that
x
is “in the bulk” in the following sense:

\dist(\mathfrak{D}(x,R),\partial\Sigma_{N})\geq\delta\sqrt{N},

(1.5)

we have:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\left\{\left|\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}(x,R))\right|\geq R(\log R)^{-0.3}\right\}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\log^{1.5}R\right).

(1.6)

Since good exponential tails for
\mathsf{Dis}
at values higher than
R
are already known (see e.g.
[
AS21
, Thm 1]
), this new sub-algebraic tail valid for values of the discrepancy between
R\log^{-0.3}R
and
R
imply that:

\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}(x,R))]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\mathsf{Dis}^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}(x,R))\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R^{2}}{\log^{0.6}R}\right)=o(R^{2}),

(1.7)

and the 2DOCP is thus indeed
hyperuniform
. The proof will be given in Section
7
.

General strategy

Our main source of inspiration is
[
NSV08
, Section 4]
, in which Nazarov-Sodin-Volberg prove JLM-like estimates on the probability of having large “charge” fluctuations in the disk
\mathfrak{D}(0,R)

for zeros of the Gaussian Entire Function
. Their argument can be roughly summarized as follows: fix
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and assume there is a discrepancy of size
R^{\alpha}
in the disk
\mathfrak{D}(0,R)
.

1.

Show that the discrepancy can be captured along the boundary
\partial\mathfrak{D}(0,R)
.

2.

Split that boundary into
\approx R
pieces of size
\approx 1
. Take
M
large and apply a basic pigeonhole argument: there exists a family of
\approx\frac{R}{M}
pieces that capture a discrepancy of size at least
\frac{R^{\alpha}}{M}
and which are “well-separated” (distances between pieces are multiples of
M
).

3.

Then comes the main probabilistic work:

(a)

Show that these well-separated pieces are
approximately independent
.

(b)

Show that the discrepancy on each piece (of size
\approx 1
) is typically
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

(c)

Show that the discrepancy on each piece is centered.

4.

Apply Bernstein’s concentration inequality: if
\{D_{i}\}_{i}
is a family of
\approx\frac{R}{M}
independent centered random variables of size
\mathcal{O}(1)
then:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sum_{i}D_{i}\geq R^{\alpha}\right\}\right)\lesssim\exp\left(-\frac{R^{2\alpha-1}}{M}\right).

This gives them
sharp
bounds on the number variance
and
the correct tail probabilities. Whilst not aiming so high, we follow here a similar strategy, with much effort to translate it to the context of the 2DOCP (besides point (2) and (4), which are easy).

Electric approach, sub-systems

The technical core is the the “electric approach” to Coulomb gases as developed by S. Serfaty and co-authors. In that regard, our main imports are:

•

The general spirit of controlling fluctuations through the electric energy (see Section
2.3
).

•

Local laws (up to microscopic scale) as in
[
AS21
]
(see Proposition
2.4
and Proposition
5.8
).

•

Optimal bounds for fluctuations of smooth linear statistics as in
[
LS18
,
Ser20
,
BBNY19
]
. Point (1), for example follows fairly easily from such bounds.

•

“Smallness of the anisotropy”, for which we refer to
[
LS18
,
Ser20
]
(see also
[
BBNY19
]
).

In addition, we put forward the role of so-called “sub-systems”, which arise as restrictions of the full system to some region and which we view as two-dimensional Coulomb systems in their own right, possibly with a small global non-neutrality, and (most importantly) feeling the effect of some harmonic exterior potential. As such, this object is not new - it has been sometimes called a “conditional” or “local” measure in the literature (see e.g.
[
BBNY19
,
BEY14
]
). We provide here a thorough study of its behavior, through global and local laws, and show that with high probability the sub-systems, although under the influence of an external potential, retain most of the rigidity properties of the full system - as is the case “for free” in the hierarchical model studied in
[
Cha19
]
. This requires a precise study of the external potential “felt” by a sub-system and of the perturbation of the equilibrium measure that it induces within the sub-system (for which we rely on the analysis of
[
BBNY17
, Section 2.]
, with some modifications). The proof of local laws given in
[
AS21
,
Leb17
]
extends to sub-systems. This allows one for example to treat Point (3) (b), which seem unsurprising, but which requires to consider objects of size
\sim 1
, hence to control the system
down to the smallest scales
. Such local laws are also crucial for the approximate translation-invariance argument which we present now.

Approximate translation-invariance

Step (3) (c) is void in the context of
[
NSV08
]
because the underlying point process is infinite and translation-invariant (which implies that linear statistics are centered). However, it turns out to be a major roadblock when adapting the proof to a Coulomb gas context. Indeed, in sub-systems (or even in the full system if one does not impose artificial boundary conditions) there is absolutely no obvious translation-invariance. We introduce an “approximate translation-invariance” result, valid for both the full system and sub-systems, which is crucial for Step (3) (c). The point being that shifting a function (or more precisely, averaging over shifts) acts as a mollification and enables us to compare the expectation of a discrepancy, or of any non-smooth linear statistic, to that of a smoother one.

There is a series of results in mathematical statistical mechanics
à la
H.-O. Georgii devoted to prove translation invariance of infinite volume Gibbs measures in contexts where stationarity is not built-in, see e.g.
[
Geo99
,
Ric07
]
, following earlier works by Fröhlich-Pfister
[
FP81
,
FP86
]
. The basic idea is to construct suitable “localized translations” in the form of diffeomorphisms acting as a given translation in a large box while leaving the majority of the system unchanged, and to control the effect on the energy of such changes of variables. Following the wisdom found in a remark of
[
Sim14
, Chap. 3, Sec. III.7]
, we construct a localized translation that varies very slowly (in terms of its
H^{1}
norm), and a careful revisit of a computation done in
[
Ser20
]
, together with Serfaty’s “smallness of anisotropy” trick, allows us to conclude that this localized translation can be chosen to have an arbitrarily small effect on the energy.

Approximate independence

Finally, one needs to find some kind of independence between the sub-systems. We introduce a new “approximate independence” argument (which we believe to be of interest on its own) for sub-systems that are well-separated, conditionally on the
number of points
in each of them. The simple idea is that two domains
\Lambda_{i},\Lambda_{j}
with
\mathsf{n}_{i},\mathsf{n}_{j}
points contribute an interaction energy given to first order by:

-\left(\mathsf{n}_{i}-|\Lambda_{i}|\right)\left(\mathsf{n}_{j}-|\Lambda_{j}|\right)\log\dist(\Lambda_{i},\Lambda_{j}),

while the precise arrangements of the points inside each domain should matter only to a lower order.

Remark 1.1
.

In the rest of the paper we often use results found in
[
AS21
]
and
[
Ser20
]
. It is worth noting that although both papers deal (among other things) with the same 2DOCP model, they do not use the same scaling convention. In
[
AS21
]
the authors work with the so-called “blown-up scaling” (as in the present paper), which is more common in the physics literature and for which lengthscales range from
\sim 1
(the nearest-neighbor scale) to
\sim N^{1/2}
(the diameter of the system), whereas
[
Ser20
]
uses the random matrix theory convention where the local scale is
\sim N^{-1/2}
and the global scale is
\sim 1
. In particular, a length scale
\ell
in the present paper translates into
N^{1/2}\ell
in
[
Ser20
]
, and the corresponding modifications must be applied when quoting results.

2
Preliminary results

2.1
Some notation.

We denote indicator functions by
\mathsf{1}
. We denote by
|\Omega|
the Lebesgue measure of a measurable subset
\Omega
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
r>0
we let
\mathfrak{D}(x,r)
be the (closed) disk of center
x
and radius
r
. We let
\square(x,r)
be the square of center
x
and sidelength
r
(with sides parallel to the axes of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
). We recall the notation
\mathsf{Dis}
for discrepancies: if
\mathbf{X}
is a point configuration and
\Omega
is a measurable subset, we let
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\Omega):=\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X},\Omega)-|\Omega|
.

Size of derivatives.
If
M
is a matrix we let
\|M\|
be its Euclidean norm. If
\varphi
is a bounded map we write
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{0}}
for its sup-norm. If
\varphi
is differentiable, we let
\mathsf{D}\varphi
be its differential and introduce the following notation:

•

|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x)
is the size of
\mathsf{D}\varphi
at a given point
x
,
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x):=\|\mathsf{D}\varphi(x)\|
.

•

|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x)
is the size of
\mathsf{D}\varphi
around a given point
x
,
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x):=\sup_{|x^{\prime}-x|\leq 1}|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x^{\prime})

•

|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\Omega}
is the size of
\mathsf{D}\varphi
in a given domain
\Omega
,
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\Omega}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x)
.

•

Finally,
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1}}
is the sup-norm of
\mathsf{D}\varphi
.

We define similarly
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{k},\star}
,
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{k},\mathrm{loc}}
,
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{k},\Omega}
and
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{k}}
for
\mathsf{k}\geq 2
.

Point configurations.

For all Borel subsets
\Lambda
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we let
\mathrm{Conf}(\Lambda)
be the space of locally finite
3
3

3

In fact all of the point configurations considered in this paper are finite.
point configurations on
\Lambda
, endowed with the vague topology of Radon measures and the associated Borel
\sigma
-algebra. When
\Lambda
is not specified, we use the notation
\mathrm{Conf}
for point configurations on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We will denote by
\mathbf{X}\mapsto\mathbf{X}\cap\Lambda
the natural projection
\mathrm{Conf}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Conf}(\Lambda)
. We may write “
x\in\mathbf{X}
” to express the fact that
\mathbf{X}
has an atom at a given point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We say that a measurable function
\mathsf{G}
on
\mathrm{Conf}
is
\Lambda
-local when for all
\mathbf{X}
in
\mathrm{Conf}
we have
\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})=\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}\cap\Lambda)
. We say that a measurable subset (an “event”)
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathrm{Conf}
is
\Lambda
-local when its indicator function
\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}
is
\Lambda
-local.

Constants.
Unless specified otherwise,
\mathtt{C}
denotes a universal constant (which may change from line to line) and
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
a constant that depends only on
\beta
. We will use
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
for constants that may depend on
\beta
and the parameter
\delta
(as in (
1.5
)). We write
A\preceq B
or
A=\mathcal{O}(B)
if
|A|\leq\mathtt{C}|B|
.

Fluctuations.
If
\varphi
is a piece-wise continuous function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we define the
fluctuation of (the linear statistics associated to)
\varphi
as the following random variable:

\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]:=\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\varphi(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(x).

(2.1)

2.2
Electric fields

We recall that
-\log
satisfies
-\Delta(-\log)=2\pi\delta_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the sense of distributions. In particular for all smooth enough test functions
f
, the following identity holds:

f(x)=\frac{-1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-\log|x-y|\Delta f(y).

(2.2)

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
\mathbf{X}
be a point configuration in
\Lambda
.

Definition 2.1

(True electric potential and electric field)
.

We let
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}
(resp.
\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}
) be the
true electric potential
(resp.
true electric field
) generated by
\mathbf{X}
(in
\Lambda
), namely the map (resp. vector field) defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by:

\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}(x):=\int_{\Lambda}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right)(y),\quad\text{resp. }\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}(x)=\int_{\Lambda}-\nabla\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right)(y).

It is easy to check that
\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}
is in
\cap_{p\in[1,2)}\mathrm{L}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{2})
but fails to be in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
around each point charge, and that the following identity is satisfied on
\Lambda
in the sense of distributions:

-\Delta\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}=-\dive\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}=2\pi\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right).

Definition 2.2

(Compatible electric fields)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}
be a vector field in
\cap_{p\in[1,2)}\mathrm{L}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We say that
\mathrm{E}
is an
electric field compatible with

\mathbf{X}
on
\Lambda
whenever we have:
-\dive\mathrm{E}=2\pi\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right)
on
\Lambda
in the sense of distributions.

Obviously the true electric field is a compatible electric field, however it is not the only one as can be seen by adding any divergence-free smooth vector field on
\Lambda
to
\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}
.

In order to “take care” of the singularities, one often proceeds to a truncation of the fields near each point charge.

Definition 2.3

(Truncation and spreading out Dirac masses)
.

For
\eta>0
we let
\mathsf{f}_{\eta}
be the function:

\mathsf{f}_{\eta}(x):=\max\left(-\log\frac{|x|}{\eta},0\right)=\begin{cases}-\log|x|+\log|\eta|&\text{if }x\leq\eta\\
0&\text{if }x\geq\eta\end{cases}.

For each point
x
of
\mathbf{X}
, let
\eta(x)
be a non-negative real number. The data of
\vec{\eta}=\{\eta(x),x\in\mathbf{X}\}
is called a
truncation vector
. If
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}
is the true electric potential, we let
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}_{\vec{\eta}}
(resp.
\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}_{\vec{\eta}}
) be the (true)
truncated
electric potential (resp. field) given by:

\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}_{\vec{\eta}}:=\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}-\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}}\mathsf{f}_{\eta(x)}(\cdot-x),\quad\text{resp. }\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}_{\vec{\eta}}=\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}-\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}}\nabla\mathsf{f}_{\eta(x)}(\cdot-x).

We are thus effectively replacing
-\log|x-\cdot|
by
-\log\eta
near each point charge. Another way to think about
\mathsf{f}_{\eta}
is that we are truncating the singularities by
smearing out
the point charge
\delta_{x}

à la
Onsager. Indeed when computing the divergence of
\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}_{\vec{\eta}}
one finds that the atom at each point
x\in\mathbf{X}
has been replaced by a uniform measure of mass
1
on the circle of center
x
and radius
\eta(x)
. We refer to
[
AS21
, Section 2.2 & Appendix B.1]
or to
[
Ser20
, Sec 3.1]
for more details. The truncation procedure can be extended to define
\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}
, where
\mathrm{E}
is any electric field compatible with
\mathbf{X}
, by setting:

\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}:=\mathrm{E}-\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}}\nabla\mathsf{f}_{\eta(x)}(\cdot-x).

For every point
x
of
\mathbf{X}
we define the “nearest-neighbor” distance
\mathsf{r}(x)
as:

\mathsf{r}(x):=\frac{1}{4}\min\left(\min_{y\in\mathbf{X},y\neq x}|x-y|,1\right).

(2.3)

In particular
\mathsf{r}(x)
is always smaller than
1/4
. We let
\vec{\mathsf{r}}=(\mathsf{r}(x),x\in\mathbf{X})
be the associated truncation vector. We will sometimes use instead the vector
s\vec{\mathsf{r}}
with
s<1
.

2.3
Logarithmic energy and electric fields

The logarithmic interaction energy
\mathsf{F}_{N}
defined in (
1.1
) can be expressed in terms of the true electric field generated by
\mathbf{X}_{N}
(Definition
2.1
) as follows.

•

Taking a uniform truncation vector
\eta(x)=\eta>0
for all
x
in
\mathbf{X}_{N}
, we have the following equality in the limit as
\eta\to 0
:

\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\eta\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}_{N}}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}+N\log\eta\right).

(This quantity is “almost” non-decreasing as
\eta\to 0
, see e.g.
[
AS21
, Lemma B.1.]
.)

•

On the other hand, taking for each
x
a truncation
\eta(x)\leq\mathsf{r}(x)
, we get a non-asymptotic identity:

\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}_{N}}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}+\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}_{N}}\log\eta(x)\right)-\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}_{N}}\int_{\mathfrak{D}(x,\eta(x))}\mathsf{f}_{\eta(x)}(t-x)\mathtt{d}t.

(2.4)

The first formulation can be found in
[
SS12
,
RS16
]
and the second one in
[
LS18
]
or
[
AS21
, Lemma 2.2.]
. Of course, expressing electrostatic interactions in terms of the electric field and “smearing out” point charges are both old ideas. One sees that in the small truncation limit there is a compensation between the explosion of
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}_{N}}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}
and the very negative terms
\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}_{N}}\log\eta(x)
, hence the name “renormalized energy” given in
[
SS15
]
(this renormalization procedure appeared in
[
BBH94
]
and the idea of using nearest-neighbor distances was borrowed from
[
GP77
]
). Despite their apparent mutual cancellation, in general the “positive” and “negative” parts can both be compared to
\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})
as explained e.g. in
[
AS21
, Lemma B.2]
.

In the sequel we use the expression “electric energy” when referring to quantities of the type
\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}
where
\mathrm{E}
is an electric field,
\vec{\eta}
a truncation vector and
\Omega
is some subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and we write:

\mathrm{Ener}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega):=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}_{N}}_{\vec{\mathsf{r}}}|^{2},\quad\mathrm{Ener}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega):=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}_{N}}_{s\vec{\mathsf{r}}}|^{2}\ (\text{for }s\in(0,1)).

(2.5)

From the identity (
2.4
) we deduce that for
s<1
:

\mathrm{Ener}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)=\mathrm{Ener}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)-\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Omega)\log s,

(2.6)

For convenience, we will sometimes write
\mathsf{EnerPts}
for the sum of
\mathrm{Ener}
and
\mathsf{Pts}
in a given domain.

2.4
Global and local laws

The following properties of
\mathsf{F}_{N}
are well-known (see
[
AS21
, Lemma 3.7]
):

•

It is bounded below: there exists a universal constant
\mathtt{C}
such that for all
N\geq 2
and for all
\mathbf{X}_{N}
, we have
\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\geq-\mathtt{C}N
.

•

It is typically of order
N
in the sense that for some constant
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
depending only on
\beta
:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\right]\leq\exp\left(\mathtt{C}_{\beta}N\right).

(2.7)

We refer to (
2.7
) as a “global law”, controlling the system at macroscopic scale. The next proposition expresses the fact that the system is also well-behaved down to some large microscopic scale. Let us first introduce two important distances that will play a role in Proposition
2.4
and in the rest of the paper.

The smallest microscopic scale
\rho_{\beta}
.
We refer to a certain
minimal lengthscale
introduced in
[
AS21
]
and denoted by
\rho_{\beta}
. It is a positive constant that depends only on
\beta
, and corresponds to the length-scale above which good rigidity properties are proven.

Distance to the boundary.
For technical (and possibly physical) reasons, properties of the full-system are easier to understand when one looks “away from the edge”, namely at some non-trivial distance of the boundary
\partial\Sigma_{N}
. For
x
a point in
\Sigma_{N}
and
\ell>0
, we will say that “
(x,\ell)
satisfies (
2.8
)” when:

\dist(\square(x,\ell),\partial\Sigma_{N})\geq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}N^{1/4},

(2.8)

where
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
is some large enough constant introduced in
[
AS21
, (1.16)]
.

Let us note that in the statement of Theorem
1
we assume that
\dist(\mathfrak{D}(x,R),\partial\Sigma_{N})\geq\delta\sqrt{N}
which is clearly a stronger assumption, at least for
N
large enough depending only on
\delta,\beta
.

Proposition 2.4

(Local laws)
.

There exists some universal constant
\mathtt{C}
, and a “local laws” constant
C

LL

depending only on
\beta
such that the following holds. Let
x
be a point in
\Sigma_{N}
and
\ell
a lengthscale such that:

1.

\ell\geq\rho_{\beta}
(the length-scale
\ell
is larger than the “minimal” one)

2.

(x,\ell)
satisfy (
2.8
) (we are sufficiently “far from the edge”).

Then we control the electric energy in
\square(x,\ell)
in exponential moments:

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\mathrm{Ener}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N},\square(x,\ell)\right)\right)\right)\leq\hyperref@@ii[prop:local_laws]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{LL}}}\beta\ell^{2}.

(2.9)

Moreover, we have the following control on the number of points:

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{\mathtt{C}}\mathsf{Pts}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N},\square(x,\ell)\right)\right)\right)\leq\hyperref@@ii[prop:local_laws]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{LL}}}\beta\ell^{2},

(2.10)

together with a discrepancy bound:

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{\mathtt{C}}\frac{\mathsf{Dis}^{2}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N},\square(x,\ell)\right)}{\ell^{2}}\right)\right)\leq\hyperref@@ii[prop:local_laws]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{LL}}}\beta.

(2.11)

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

This is a subset of the statements in
[
AS21
, Theorem 1]
, see Section
B.1
for a technical discussion.
∎

As can be seen from Proposition
2.4
,
\mathrm{Ener}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\square(x,\ell)),\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\square(x,\ell))
and
\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\square(x,\ell))
are all expected to be of the same order as the area
\ell^{2}
.

Remark 2.5
.

For our purposes, we will repeatedly use the local laws under the following form: for
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
large enough, the probability of having more than
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\ell^{2}
points (or an energy higher than
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\ell^{2}
) in a given square
\square(x,\ell)
is smaller than
\exp\left(-\ell^{2}/\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\right)
.

One can replace squares by disks (or by any “reasonable” shape) in the previous statement, however it is worth observing that local laws do not directly yield interesting controls the energy (or number of points) on very thin strips, rectangles with diverging aspect ratio, boundaries of squares, thin annuli etc. In those situations, one must resort to splitting the region into squares, applying the local laws to each square and then using a union bound.

2.5
The electric energy controls fluctuations

The next lemma expresses how the electric energy controls linear statistics of Lipschitz functions.

Lemma 2.6

(Bounds on fluctuations - Lipschitz case)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a point configuration in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and let
\varphi
be a function in
\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with compact support. Let
\mathrm{E}
be any electric field compatible with
\mathbf{X}
on
\supp\varphi
in the sense of Definition
2.2
. Let
\Omega
be a domain containing a
1
-neighborhood of
\supp\nabla\varphi
. We have:

\left|\int\varphi(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right)(x)\right|\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\mathsf{r}}}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\Omega}\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X},\Omega)

(2.12)

Localized case

Assume that
\tilde{\Omega}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\Omega}_{m}
cover
\supp\nabla\varphi
, and that for each
i
the domain
\Omega_{i}
contains a
1
-neighborhood of
\tilde{\Omega}_{i}
, then we can replace the right-hand side of (
2.12
) by:

\sum_{k=1}^{m}|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\Omega_{i}}\times\left(\left(\int_{\Omega_{i}}|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\mathsf{r}}}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left|\Omega_{i}\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X},\Omega_{i})\right)

(2.13)

Remark 2.7
.

Controls of the type (
2.12
) have appeared under various forms in previous works see e.g.
[
SS15
, Lemma 5.1]
,
[
LS18
, Proposition 2.5]
or
[
AS21
, Lemma B.5]
, they are usually phrased as: “the electric energy controls the fluctuations”. The electric energy in a given domain
\Omega
is typically of order
|\Omega|
and so is the number of points in
\Omega
, cf. Proposition
2.4
, thus (
2.12
) bounds the typical fluctuations of
\varphi
by
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1}}|\supp\nabla\varphi|
whereas a naive
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
bound would rather give
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{0}}\times|\supp\varphi|
. Since our test functions often live on some large lengthscale
\ell
with
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1}}
comparable to
\ell^{-1}|\varphi|_{\mathsf{0}}
, there is indeed an improvement.

We give the proof of Lemma
2.6
in Section
B.2
. Compared to existing results, here we simply emphasize the role played by the support of the gradient (instead of the whole support of the test function), which yields more accurate estimates when
\varphi
is a sharp cut-off function. We will repeatedly use its “localized version” (
2.13
), whose proof is a straightforward of (
2.12
).

2.6
Finer bound on fluctuations for smooth test functions

For test functions with higher regularity the bound of Lemma
2.6
on fluctuations of linear statistics can be improved (see
[
LS18
,
BBNY19
,
Ser20
]
as well as
[
RV07
,
AHM11
]
for the
\beta=2
case). In particular if
\varphi_{\ell}(x):=\bar{\varphi}(x/\ell)
for a fixed reference smooth test function
\bar{\varphi}
, then
\varphi_{\ell}
lives at scale
\ell\gg 1
yet its fluctuations remain
bounded
as
\ell\to\infty
(with high probability). One can refer e.g. to
[
Ser20
, Thm. 1]
and
[
Ser20
, Cor. 2.1]
for such results.

In this paper we will occasionally need a more specific statement valid in the radially symmetric case.

Proposition 2.8

(Finer bound, the
C^{2}
radially symmetric case)
.

There exists a constant
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
depending only on
\beta
such that the following holds. Let
x
be a point in
\Sigma_{N}
and let
\varphi
be a test function which is radially symmetric around
x
, with compact support. Assume that
\varphi
is in
\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and let
\mathcal{A}
be an annulus containing (a
1
-neighborhood of) the support of
\Delta\varphi
. Let
s
be a real number satisfying:

|s|\leq\frac{\pi\beta}{4|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}}.

(2.14)

Then the exponential moments of the fluctuations of
\varphi
satisfy:

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(s\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]\right)\right]=\frac{s^{2}}{4\pi\beta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(s|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\left(\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A})\right)\right)\right].

(2.15)

Although it does not appear as such in the literature, Proposition
2.8
can be easily deduced from the tools of
[
Ser20
]
, we give a proof in Section
B.3
.

2.7
Wegner’s estimates

The recent paper
[
Tho22
]
provides upper bounds on the
k
-point correlation functions of Coulomb gases (in dimension
2
and higher). Among many other things, it states so-called “Wegner’s estimates” i.e. uniform controls of the form
\rho_{1}\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
, where
\rho_{1}
is the one-point correlation function. We will use
[
Tho22
, Theorem 1.6]
, which is valid even for
r\ll 1
(sub-microscopic scale):

Lemma 2.9
.

There exists
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
such that for all
x
in
\Sigma_{N}
and
for all
r>0
, if
(x,r)
satisfies (
2.8
) we have:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\left\{\mathsf{Pts}(\mathfrak{D}(x,r))\geq 1\right\}\right)\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}r^{2}.

(2.16)

3
Locating discrepancies near the boundary

Let
0\leq\varepsilon_{R}\leq 1
(to be chosen later). The goal of this section to show that any “large” discrepancy
\varepsilon_{R}R
within a disk of radius
R
can be found (with high probability)
near the boundary of the disk
.

3.1
Cornering the discrepancy in an annulus close to the boundary

Let
L\geq 100
be a lengthscale to be chosen later. Let
z
be a point and
R\geq\rho_{\beta}
such that
\mathfrak{D}(z,R)
satisfies (
2.8
). In this section we sometimes simply write
\mathfrak{D}_{r}
(for
r>0
) instead of
\mathfrak{D}(z,r)
. Assume that the disk
\mathfrak{D}(z,R)
contains
\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
too many
4
4

4

We mean
\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{R})\geq\pi R^{2}+\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
. points
points. Compare two idealized situations: in the first one, the excess of charges is spread uniformly over
\mathfrak{D}_{R}
, in the sense that
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r})
behaves like
\frac{r^{2}}{R^{2}}\times\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
for
r\leq R
. In the second situation, the excess is concentrated in a thin strip near
\partial\mathfrak{D}_{R}
and immediately compensated by a default on the other side of the boundary. According to
[
JLM93
]
, the physically realistic picture is the second one:
“Macroscopic electrostatics of conductors implies that, for a given value of [the discrepancy]
Q
, the dominant configurations are such that
Q
is concentrated in a layer on the inner side of the boundary of the disk, while a charge
-Q
accumulates in a layer on the outer side.”
. In order to give a corresponding mathematical statement, observe that the event

\bigcap_{R-2L\leq r\leq R-L}\left\{\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}(z,r))\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R\right\}

expresses that some excess of charges is found in the disks
\mathsf{D}_{r}
for a fairly wide region
r\in[R-2L,R-L]
and that the discrepancy is thus not “concentrated in a layer on the inner side of the boundary of the disk”.

Proposition 3.1
.

There exists a constant
\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}(\beta)
depending only on
\beta
such that the following holds. Let
s
be a real parameter. Assume that
R,L,\varepsilon_{R},s
satisfy:

\begin{cases}&\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{R}}\leq L\leq\frac{R}{10}\\
&0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}}\min\left(\frac{L^{3}}{R},L\varepsilon_{R}\right).\end{cases}

(3.1)

Then we have:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\bigcap_{R-2L\leq r\leq R-L}\left\{\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}(z,r))\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R\right\}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{s\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{4}\right).

(3.2)

We postpone the proof of Proposition
3.1
to Section
B.4
. It relies on Proposition
2.8
.

Next, we argue that if the complementary event occurs, then one can find an annulus of width
\approx L
near the boundary of
\mathfrak{D}_{R}
that carries a large discrepancy.

Lemma 3.2

(Finding discrepancy in an annulus)
.

If
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{R})\geq\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
and if, in contrast to the event considered in (
3.2
), there exists a radius
r
with
R-2L\leq r\leq R-L
such that:

\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r})<\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R

(3.3)

then there exists an integer
k
with
0\leq k\leq R^{2}
such that the discrepancy in the annulus
\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{R-2L+\frac{kL}{R^{2}}}
is larger than
\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
.

Proof.

Let
r
be such that (
3.3
) holds and let
0\leq k\leq R^{2}
be the integer such that:

r_{k}:=R-2L+\frac{kL}{R^{2}}\leq r<R-2L+\frac{(k+1)L}{R^{2}}.

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}})\leq\frac{3}{4}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
(a rough bound), indeed we have:

\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}})\leq\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r})+|\mathfrak{D}_{r}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}}|,

with equality if and only if there is no point in the annulus
\mathfrak{D}_{r}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}}
, whose area is of order
\frac{RL}{R^{2}}\leq 1
.

Since the total discrepancy in
\mathfrak{D}_{R}
is at least
\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
and the one inside
\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}}
is at most
\frac{3}{4}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
then the annulus
\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}}
must carry a discrepancy of size at least
\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

If there is a negative discrepancy i.e. a default of points instead of an excess, one proceeds the same way, except that in Proposition
3.1
we bound the probability of
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r})\leq\frac{-\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{2}
for all
R-2L\leq r\leq R-L
instead. The complementary event is that for some
r
with
R-2L\leq r\leq R-L
, we have
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r})>-\frac{-\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{2}
. Then in Lemma
3.2
instead of taking
r_{k}
a bit smaller than
r
we take it a bit larger and observe that the discrepancy cannot go down too much between
\mathfrak{D}_{r}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}}
. Hence
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}})\geq\frac{-3\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{4}
, so the annulus
\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{r_{k}}
carries a default of points at least
\frac{\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{4}
(in absolute value).

3.2
A well-separated family of boxes carrying the discrepancy

Let
r
be in
[R-2L,R-L]
.

Decomposition in boxes

We split the annulus
\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{r}
into smaller parts that we call
boxes
.

Definition 3.4

(Decomposition in boxes)
.

For
i\in\{0,\dots,\frac{R}{L}-1\}
, we let the
i
-th “box”
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
be the intersection of the annulus
\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{r}
with a certain angular sector of center
z
:

\mathfrak{B}_{i}:=\left(\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{r}\right)\cap\left\{z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C},\ i\leq\frac{\arg(z^{\prime}-z)}{2\pi}\times\frac{R}{L}\leq i+1\right\}.

The boundary of each such box is made of two line segments of equal length in
(L,2L)
and two concentric circular arcs which subtend the same angle at the center and whose arclengths are different but both in
\left(\frac{L}{2},L\right)
. The shape is symmetric with respect to the straight line joining the midpoints of both arcs. We sometimes call “a box of size
L
” any domain
\mathfrak{B}
that fits the previous description.

We let
\omega_{i}
be the center of mass of
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
, which only serves as a convenient reference point.

Introducing the parameters
M
and
T

Let
T
be a lengthscale, and
M
be an integer, both to be chosen at the end, such that:

T\geq 10L,\quad 100\leq M\leq\frac{R}{L},\quad T\leq\frac{ML}{100},\quad T^{2}\log T\leq ML.

(3.4)

(The fourth condition (which could be weakened), implies the third one but for clarity we write them all down.)

Lemma 3.5

(Some well-separated boxes carry the discrepancy)
.

Assume that the discrepancy in
\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{r}
is larger than
\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R
. Then there exists
l\in\{0,\dots,M-1\}
such that:

\sum_{i\equiv l\text{ mod }M}\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{B}_{i})\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{4M}.

(3.5)

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

It follows from a straightforward pigeonhole argument.
∎

From now on, we assume to have chosen such a
l\in\{0,\dots,M-1\}
and we write the corresponding boxes as
\mathfrak{B}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{N}}
, where
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R}{ML}\right)
is the number of box in each “well-separated” family. Moreover, we see each box
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
as being contained in a large disk
\Lambda_{i}:=\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T)
, where
T
is as above.

Let
\mathrm{d}_{ij}
be the distance between
\Lambda_{i}
and
\Lambda_{j}
.

Claim 3.6
.

We have for each fixed
i
:

\sum_{j,j\neq i}\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}_{ij}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log R}{ML}\right),\quad\sum_{j,j\neq i}\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}_{ij}^{2}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{M^{2}L^{2}}\right)

(3.6)

Proof.

Let us observe that between two “consecutive” boxes in the family
\{i\equiv l\text{ mod }M\}
considered in (
3.5
), there is a distance of order
ML
(since
ML\geq 100T
by assumption, this is also comparable to the distance between two consecutive
disks
). We can compare the sum to an harmonic sum (in the first case) or a converging Riemann series (in the second case).
∎

Plan for the next two sections

We now want to treat each box
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
as living in its own smaller version of a 2DOCP contained in
\Lambda_{i}
, which leads us to the next two sections.

Think of a “sub-system” as the random collection of particles contained in a given sub-domain
\Lambda\subset\Sigma_{N}
with a reasonable shape (e.g. a square or a disk). These particles feel the influence of each other, but also of the full system in
\Sigma_{N}
because the logarithmic interaction is long-range. Hence sub-systems are typically not isolated and not independent from each other.

1.

In Section
4
we observe that if we condition on the values of the discrepancies (or equivalently of the number of particles) in domains that are well-separated, then the corresponding sub-systems acquire a form of independence.

2.

In Section
5
we show that “typical” sub-systems, seen as slight generalizations of the 2DOCP model introduced earlier (in (
1.1
), (
1.2
)), retains most of the properties of the
full
system mentioned in Section
2
.

4
Approximate conditional independence for sub-systems

In this section, we consider a family
\{\Lambda_{i},1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}\}
of
\mathcal{N}\geq 2
disjoint disks in
\Sigma_{N}
(forming our “sub-systems”). We will eventually apply the results below to the
\Lambda_{i}
’s chosen in Section
3.2
, but the statements in the present section are general.

Let
\mathsf{Ext}
be the complement
\mathsf{Ext}:=\Sigma_{N}\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\Lambda_{i}
. Let
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
be the logarithmic potential generated by the system in
\mathsf{Ext}
, namely:

\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x):=\int_{\mathsf{Ext}}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y).

(4.1)

The potential
\mathsf{Ext}
is harmonic on all the
\Lambda_{i}
’s and depends only on the configuration in
\mathsf{Ext}
.

4.1
Decomposition of the interaction, conditional independence error

We let
\mathsf{Int}[\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}]
be the true logarithmic interaction between the sub-systems, namely:

\mathsf{Int}[\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}]:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathcal{N}}\iint_{\Lambda_{i}\times\Lambda_{j}}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y).

(4.2)

By expanding the double integral defining
\mathsf{F}_{N}
, we may write:

\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Ext}}(\mathbf{X}_{N}\cap\mathsf{Ext})+\mathsf{Int}[\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}]+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{N}\cap\Lambda_{i})+\int_{\Lambda_{i}}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(x)\right)

(4.3)

with
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Ext}},\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda_{i}}
defined the obvious way (see (
4.9
)). On the other hand, for each
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
, let
\mathsf{D}_{i}
be the discrepancy of
\mathbf{X}_{N}
in
\Lambda_{i}
, and let
\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]
be the approximation of
\mathsf{Int}[\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}]
given by:

\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathcal{N}}-\mathsf{D}_{i}\mathsf{D}_{j}\log|\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}|,

(4.4)

where
\omega_{i}
denotes the center of
\Lambda_{i}
. We define the quantity
\mathrm{ErrorCI}
as follows:

\mathrm{ErrorCI}\left[\mathbf{X}_{N}|\left(\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]:=\left|\mathsf{Int}[\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}]-\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]\right|,

(4.5)

and we use it below in order to measure a “conditional independence error”.

4.2
Bounds on the conditional independence error

For
1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathcal{N}
, define the distance
\mathrm{d}_{ij}
as
\mathrm{d}_{ij}:=\dist(\Lambda_{i},\Lambda_{j})
. Assume that for all
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
the disk
\Lambda_{i}
has radius
T
and that:

\max_{1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{n}_{i}\leq 10T^{2},\quad\min_{1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathcal{N}}\mathrm{d}_{ij}\geq 10T.

(4.6)

Lemma 4.1

(The size of
\mathrm{ErrorCI}
)
.

We have, if (
4.6
) holds:

\left|\mathrm{ErrorCI}\left[\mathbf{X}_{N}|\left(\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right|=\mathcal{O}(T^{5})\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathcal{N}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}_{ij}}.

(4.7)

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

For
x\in\Lambda_{i},y\in\Lambda_{j}
(with
i\neq j
) since the diameter of the squares is
\mathcal{O}(T)
and the mutual distances satisfy (
4.6
), a Taylor’s expansion yields:

\log|x-y|=\log|\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}|+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{T}{|\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}|}\right),

(4.8)

with a universal implicit constant. Integrating (
4.8
) against the fluctuation measures in
\Lambda_{i}
and
\Lambda_{j}
yields:

\left|\iint_{\Lambda_{i}\times\Lambda_{j}}\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y)-\mathsf{D}_{i}\mathsf{D}_{j}\log|\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}|\right|\leq(|\Lambda_{i}|+\mathsf{n}_{i})\cdot(|\Lambda_{j}|+\mathsf{n}_{j})\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{T}{\mathrm{d}_{ij}}\right).

Using the bound on
\mathsf{n}_{i},\mathsf{n}_{j}
given by (
4.6
) and summing over
i\neq j
, we get (
4.7
).
∎

Remark 4.2
.

It is possible to reduce the order of magnitude of
\mathrm{ErrorCI}
by expanding the interaction in a more precise way.

4.3
2DOCP’s with harmonic external field

In the following we consider two-dimensional one-component plasmas whose energy takes into account the effect of an external field
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
on each particle.

•

Let
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
(the
external field
on
\Lambda
) be a lower semi-continuous function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is harmonic on (the interior of)
\Lambda
. Let us emphasize that
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is harmonic hence very regular in the interior of
\Lambda
but we do not
a priori
control
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
or its derivatives near
\partial\Lambda
. The situation is thus different from the choice of an external weight/potential as frequently found in the literature on log-gases.

•

Let
\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}
denote a
\mathsf{n}
-tuple of points
\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{\mathsf{n}})
in
\Lambda
, let
\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\delta_{x_{i}}
be the associated atomic measure, and let
\mathbf{f}_{\Lambda}:=\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\mathtt{1}_{\Lambda}
be the signed fluctuation measure on
\Lambda
. Let
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}})
be the logarithmic interaction energy:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}):=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{(x,y)\in\Lambda\times\Lambda,x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{\Lambda}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{\Lambda}(y).

(4.9)

We define a probability density
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}
on the space of
\mathsf{n}
-tuples of points in
\Lambda
by setting:

\mathtt{d}\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}(\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}):=\frac{\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}})+\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{\Lambda}(x)\right)\right)}{\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}},

(4.10)

where
\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}
is the partition function, namely the normalizing constant:

\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}:=\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}})+\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{\Lambda}(x)\right)\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}},

(4.11)

and
\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}
. We may now state the main result of this section.

4.4
Approximate conditional independence

Proposition 4.3

(Approximate conditional independence)
.

•

For
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
, let
\mathsf{G}_{i}
be a measurable function on
\mathrm{Conf}
with
non-negative
real values and let
\mathcal{E}_{i}
be a measurable subset of
\mathrm{Conf}
. Assume that
\mathsf{G}_{i}
,
\mathcal{E}_{i}
are
\Lambda_{i}
-local in the sense of Section
2.1
.

•

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}
be a measurable subset of
\mathrm{Conf}
, assume that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}
is
\mathsf{Ext}
-local.

•

Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{N}
the following event:
\mathcal{E}_{N}:=\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}.

•

Finally, we say that a family
\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}}
of integers is “admissible” (we write “
\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}
” below) when there exists
\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}
such that
\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Lambda_{i})=\mathsf{n}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
.

We have:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\right]\leq\exp\left(2\beta\sup_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\mathrm{ErrorCI}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\\
\times\sup_{\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}},\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{i}\right].

(4.12)

Moreover, with the same assumptions, the following lower bound holds:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\right]\geq\exp\left(-2\beta\sup_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\mathrm{ErrorCI}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\times\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}(\mathcal{E}_{N})\\
\times\inf_{\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}},\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{i}\right].

(4.13)

Notice that one
\sup
is now an
\inf
, the error term
\sup\mathrm{ErrorCI}
now appears in the exponent with a minus sign, and there is an extra factor
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}(\mathcal{E}_{N})
.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Let us start by using the definition (
1.2
) of
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
and the decomposition (
4.3
) of the logarithmic interaction
\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathrm{X}_{N})
. We obtain:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\right]=\frac{1}{\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}}\int_{(\Sigma_{N})^{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Ext}}(\mathbf{X}_{N}\cap\mathsf{Ext})\right)\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{Int}[\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}]\right)\\
\times\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{N}\cap\Lambda_{i})+\int_{\Lambda_{i}}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(x)\right)\right)\times\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right]\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}.

Next:

•

We use a complete system of events by fixing the number
\mathsf{n}_{i}
of points in each
\Lambda_{i}
(and thus also the number
\mathsf{n}_{\mathrm{ext}}
of points in
\mathsf{Ext}
). The knowledge of
\mathsf{n}_{i}
is equivalent to fixing the discrepancy
\mathsf{D}_{i}
in
\Lambda_{i}
. Since we are working under the event
\mathcal{E}_{N}
, the
\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}
must be admissible as defined in the statement of Proposition
4.3
.

•

Up to a combinatorial factor, we may then decompose the
N
-tuple
\mathrm{X}_{N}
into a
\mathsf{n}_{\mathrm{ext}}
-tuple
\mathrm{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}
of points in
\mathsf{Ext}
, and
\mathrm{X}_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
), where each
\mathrm{X}_{i}
is a
\mathsf{n}_{i}
-tuple of points in
\Lambda_{i}
.

•

We decompose the Lebesgue measure
\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}
accordingly.

•

We write
\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}
and
\mathbf{X}_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
) for the associated atomic measures. We have the identities
\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}=\mathbf{X}_{N}\cap\mathsf{Ext}
and
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{N}\cap\Lambda_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
).

•

By our locality assumptions:
\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}(\mathbf{X}_{N})=\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\times\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{i})
.

•

We use our locality assumption on
\mathsf{G}_{i}
to write
\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})=\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{i})
.

•

We introduce the measures
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Ext}}:=\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\mathtt{1}_{\mathsf{Ext}}
and
\mathbf{f}_{\Lambda_{i}}:=\mathbf{X}_{i}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\mathtt{1}_{\Lambda_{i}}
.

•

Finally, using the definition (
4.5
) we may replace
\mathsf{Int}[\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}]
by
\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]
, up to an error quantified by
\mathrm{ErrorCI}
. The quantity
\exp\left(-\beta\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]\right)
can be taken outside the integrals because it only depends on the data of
\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}}
.

We obtain the following upper bound:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}}\sum_{\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}}\binom{N}{\mathsf{n}_{1}\dots\mathsf{n}_{\mathcal{N}}}\exp\left(-\beta\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]\right)\\
\times\exp\left(\beta\sup_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\mathrm{ErrorCI}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\times\int_{(\mathsf{Ext})^{\mathsf{n}_{\mathrm{ext}}}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Ext}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\\
\times\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\int_{(\Lambda_{i})^{\mathsf{n}_{i}}}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{i})+\int_{\Lambda_{i}}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{\Lambda_{i}}(x)\right)\right)\times\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{i}\right]\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}.

(4.14)

We may conveniently condense (
4.14
) using our notation. For
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
, in view of the definitions (
4.10
), (
4.11
), we write:

\int_{(\Lambda_{i})^{\mathsf{n}_{i}}}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{i})+\int_{\Lambda_{i}}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{\Lambda_{i}}(x)\right)\right)\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{i}\\
=\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]\times\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]\times\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}},

and we may thus re-write (
4.14
) as:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}}\sum_{\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}}\binom{N}{\mathsf{n}_{1}\dots\mathsf{n}_{\mathcal{N}}}\exp\left(-\beta\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]\right)\\
\times\exp\left(\beta\sup_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\mathrm{ErrorCI}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\times\int_{(\mathsf{Ext})^{\mathsf{n}_{\mathrm{ext}}}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Ext}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\\
\times\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]\times\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]\times\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\right]\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}.

(4.15)

In (
4.14
), (
4.15
), the converse inequalities hold up to adding a minus sign in front of
\mathrm{ErrorCI}
in the exponent (recall that the
\mathsf{G}_{i}
’s have non-negative values by assumption). Now, for all
\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}
and all admissible
\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}
, we have:

\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]\leq\sup_{\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{i}\right],

and the converse inequality holds up to trading the
\sup
for an
\inf
. We get:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\right]\leq\sup_{\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}},\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\mathrm{adm.}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]\times\exp\left(\beta\sup_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\mathrm{ErrorCI}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\\
\times\frac{1}{\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}}\sum_{\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}}\binom{N}{\mathsf{n}_{1}\dots\mathsf{n}_{\mathcal{N}}}\exp\left(-\beta\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]\right)\int_{(\mathsf{Ext})^{\mathsf{n}_{\mathrm{ext}}}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Ext}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\\
\times\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\int_{(\Lambda_{i})^{\mathsf{n}_{i}}}\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]\times\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\right]\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}^{\mathrm{ext}},

(4.16)

and the converse inequality holds up to trading the first
\sup
for an
\inf
and writing
-\sup\mathrm{ErrorCI}
instead of
\sup\mathrm{ErrorCI}
in the exponent. We now establish a bound on the partition function
\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}
in a similar fashion. By definition, we have:

\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}}&\leq\left(\int_{(\Sigma_{N})^{N}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}\right)^{-1}\\
\frac{1}{\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}}&=\left(\int_{(\Sigma_{N})^{N}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}\right)^{-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}(\mathcal{E}_{N}).\end{cases}

(4.17)

Arguing as above (taking all the
\mathsf{G}_{i}
’s equal to the constant
1
), we get:

\int_{(\Sigma_{N})^{N}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}\\
\leq\exp\left(\beta\sup_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\mathrm{ErrorCI}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\times\sum_{\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}}\binom{N}{\mathsf{n}_{1}\dots\mathsf{n}_{\mathcal{N}}}\exp\left(-\beta\widetilde{\mathsf{Int}}[\mathsf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{D}_{\mathcal{N}}]\right)\\
\times\int_{\mathsf{Ext}^{\mathsf{n}_{\mathrm{ext}}}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Ext}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}}(\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}})\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\int_{(\Lambda_{i})^{\mathsf{n}_{i}}}\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]\times\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\right]\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}^{\mathrm{ext}},

(4.18)

and the converse inequality holds with
-\sup\mathrm{ErrorCI}
instead of
\sup\mathrm{ErrorCI}
. Combining (
4.16
), (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) we obtain (
4.12
) (note that many terms cancel out in the ratio). We have also proven the converse inequality (
4.13
), with an extra factor
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}(\mathcal{E}_{N})
coming from (
4.17
).
∎

5
Generalized 2DOCP’s arising as sub-systems

Let
\Lambda
be a disk of center
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and radius
T
, and let
\mathsf{n}\geq 1
be an integer, corresponding to the number of points in
\Lambda
. In general we may have
\mathsf{n}\neq|\Lambda|
.

5.1
Good external potentials, good sub-systems

Let
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
be an external field as in Section
4.3
(we will eventually use this for the particular choice (
4.1
)). We introduce two definitions to pinpoint “good situations” for the generalized 2DOCP measure
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}
introduced in (
4.10
).

Definition 5.1

(Good potential)
.

We say that
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is a “
good external potential
on
\Lambda
with constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
” when the following holds:

1. Control up to the edge.

There exists a function
\tilde{\mathrm{V}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
on
\Lambda
satisfying:

\tilde{\mathrm{V}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\text{ if }\dist(x,\partial\Lambda)\geq 1,\quad\tilde{\mathrm{V}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\leq\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)+100\text{ for all }x\in\Lambda,

(5.1)

such that:

\left|\tilde{\mathrm{V}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)-\tilde{\mathrm{V}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)\right|\leq\bar{\mathtt{C}}\times T\times\log^{3}T.

(5.2)

2. A technical decomposition.

\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
can be decomposed as the sum

\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}=\mathfrak{h}^{\nu}+R,

(5.3)

where
\mathfrak{h}^{\nu}
is the logarithmic potential generated by a positive measure supported on an annulus of width
\hat{T}:=\log T
right outside
\Lambda
, and
R
is harmonic in
\Lambda
, such that we control the derivative of
R
up to the edge by:

|R|_{1,\Lambda}\leq\bar{\mathtt{C}}\times\log^{2}T,

(5.4)

and moreover we control the mass of
\nu
locally at scale
\hat{T}=\log T
:

\sup_{x\in\partial\Lambda}\nu(\mathfrak{D}(x,\log T))\leq\bar{\mathtt{C}}\log^{2}T.

(5.5)

Definition 5.2

(Good sub-system)
.

We consider the event “
\Lambda
is a good-system” defined as the sub-set of all point configurations
\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Conf}
such that:

1.

The discrepancy
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\Lambda)
satisfies:
|\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\Lambda)|\leq T\log^{2}T
.

2.

\mathbf{X}
belongs to the event
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
defined by:

(a)

There is absolutely no point in
\Lambda
at distance
\leq e^{-\log^{2}T}
from
\partial\Lambda
.

(b)

There is no more than
T\log T
points in
\Lambda
at distance
\leq 1
from
\partial\Lambda
.

This event is of course
\Lambda
-local and even
\{x\in\Lambda,\dist(x,\partial\Lambda)\leq 1\}
-local.

The effective external potential is often good.

Let
\Lambda_{i}:=\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T)
(
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
) be the disks introduced at the end of Section
3.2
. We define a common external potential for all
\Lambda_{i}
’s by setting (cf. (
4.1
)):

\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x):=\int_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\Lambda_{i}}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y).

(5.6)

Proposition 5.3

(The effective external potential is often good)
.

There exists a constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
depending only on
\beta
(and on the choice of
\delta
as in Assumption
1.5
) such that the following holds.
With
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
-probability greater than
1-\mathcal{N}\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}T}{\bar{\mathtt{C}}}\right)
, for all
i=1,\dots,\mathcal{N}
the external potential
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is a good external potential on
\Lambda_{i}
with constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
(in the sense of Definition
5.1
).

The proof of Proposition
5.3
is elementary but cumbersome. We postpone it to Section
C
. Since the constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
given by Proposition
5.3
depends only on
\beta,\delta
, let us keep in mind that if we say that some constant depends on the “good external potential constant”
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
then in fact it itself only depends on
\beta,\delta
.

The sub-systems are often good

Lemma 5.4
.

For
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
large enough, if
T\geq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
, then with
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
-probability
\geq 1-\mathcal{N}\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}T}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right)
, for all
i=1,\dots,\mathcal{N}
, the conditions of Definition
5.2
(expressing the fact that “
\Lambda_{i}
is a good sub-system”) are satisfied.

Proof.

Using the “discrepancy” part (
2.11
) of the local laws (Proposition
2.4
) we see that if
T
is large enough (depending on
\beta
) then for any fixed
i
we have:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(|\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\Lambda_{i})|\geq T\log T\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}T}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right).

Checking the conditions of
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
requires more care.

Claim 5.5

(The conditions of
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
are often met)
.

For any fixed
i
we have:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}T}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right).

Proof of Claim
5.5
.

From the local laws, it is easy to see that the second condition in Definition
5.2
is often satisfied. Indeed we can cover the
1
-neighborhood of the boundary
\partial\Lambda_{i}
by
\mathtt{C}T
squares of sidelength
1
, each of which contains at most
\frac{\log T}{100\mathtt{C}}
points with probability
\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}T}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right)
(for
T
large enough). We conclude with a union bound, which does not hinder the estimate.

The first condition of Definition
5.2
is more subtle and we rely on the “one-particle cluster” bound from
[
Tho22
]
mentioned in Lemma
2.9
. Cover the region
\Gamma_{i}:=\{z\in\Lambda,\dist(z,\partial\Lambda_{i})\leq e^{-\log^{2}T}\}
by
\mathcal{O}(Te^{\log^{2}T})
disks of radius
r=10e^{-\log^{2}T}
. For each disk, we know from (
2.16
) that the probability of it being occupied by at least one particle is smaller than
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}r^{2}
. Then an union bound shows that the probability of at least one point falling anywhere in
\Gamma_{i}
is bounded by
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}Te^{-\log^{2}T}
, which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

We use a union bound over
\mathcal{N}
such events to handle all the
\Lambda_{i}
’s at once.
∎

Until the end of Section
5
, we consider a “good external potential”
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}"
with constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
as in Definition
5.1
and we assume that
|\mathsf{n}-|\Lambda||\leq T\log T
.

5.2
2DOCP’s with non-uniform neutralizing background

Instead of adding
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
, let us consider 2DOCP’s in which the “neutralizing background” is no longer the uniform one, but a perturbation thereof.

•

Let
\mathbf{m}
be a (non-negative) measure on
\Lambda
and let
\zeta
be some non-negative function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Assume that
\mathbf{m}
is supported in
\Lambda
and that
\zeta
vanishes on the support of
\mathbf{m}
.

•

Assume that the measure
\mathbf{m}
can be written as the sum of a measure which has a bounded density with respect to the Lebesgue measure
\mathbf{m}_{0}
on
\Lambda
and of a singular measure which has a bounded density with respect to the arc-length measure
\mathtt{d}s
on
\partial\Lambda
. This assumption will be justified later in Section
D.2
. In particular, it implies that
\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}(y)
is finite.

•

Let
\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}
be a
\mathsf{n}
-tuple of points in
\Lambda
and
\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}
be the associated point configuration, we let
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m})
be the logarithmic interaction energy
computed with respect to

\mathbf{m}
, namely:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}):=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{(x,y)\in\Sigma_{N}\times\Sigma_{N},x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}-\mathbf{m})(x)\mathtt{d}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}-\mathbf{m})(y).

(5.7)

We define a probability density
\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\cdot,\mathbf{m},\zeta)
on the space of
\mathsf{n}
-tuples of points in
\Lambda
by setting:

\mathtt{d}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m},\zeta):=\frac{\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\zeta(x_{i})\right)\right)}{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m},\zeta)}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}},

(5.8)

where
\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m},\zeta)
is the partition function, namely the normalizing constant:

\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}(\mathbf{m},\zeta):=\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\zeta(x_{i})\right)\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}.

(5.9)

It is well-known that there is a way to pass from an external potential
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
(which should be here treated as a
perturbation
of a reference potential) as in Section
4.3
to the appropriate “equilibrium” measure and vice-versa (we refer e.g. to the lecture notes
[
Ser15
, Sec. 2&3]
). However, there is here a specific difficulty due to the behavior of
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
near
\partial\Lambda
