and we postpone the necessary discussion (inspired by similar concerns in
[
BBNY17
]
) to Section
D.1
.

Electric formalism for
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m})

We extend here some of the formalism from Sections
2.2
and
2.3
in a fairly straightforward way. Define the “true electric potential/field” associated to a point configuration
\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}
and the background measure
\mathbf{m}
as (cf. Definition
2.1
):

\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}}:=-\log\ast(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}-\mathbf{m}),\quad\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}}=-\nabla\log\ast(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}-\mathbf{m}),

their truncated versions being defined as in Definition
2.3
. Then we have the following identity, which extends (
2.4
):

Lemma 5.6
.

Assume that the total mass of
\mathbf{m}
is equal to
\mathsf{n}
. Let
\{\eta(x),x\in\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}\}
be a truncation vector with
\eta(x)\leq\mathsf{r}(x)
(the nearest-neighbor distance introduced in (
2.3
)) for all
x\in\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}
. We have:

\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}+\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}\log\eta(x)\right)-\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}\int_{\mathfrak{D}(x,\eta(x))}\mathsf{f}_{\eta(x)}(t-x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}(t),

(5.10)

provided the disks
\mathfrak{D}(x,\eta(x))
do not intersect
\partial\Lambda
.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Since we place ourselves away from the singular part of
\mathbf{m}
, the proof works exactly as when
\mathbf{m}
has a bounded density, see
[
AS21
, Lemma 2.2]
.
∎

We also extend the notation
\mathrm{Ener}
from (
2.5
) by setting:
\mathrm{Ener}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m},\Omega):=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}}|^{2}
.

5.3
Good properties of sub-systems with good external potentials

If
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is a good external potential, there exists a probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
on
\Lambda
and a function
\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}
such that:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\cdot,\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}},\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}\right),

(5.11)

we refer to Section
D.2
for a definition and precise study of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
and to Lemma
D.6
for a proof of (
5.11
). The key features of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
are that:

1.

It might be singular on
\partial\Lambda
.

2.

It might have “holes” near
\partial\Lambda
(and
\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}>0
on these holes).

3.

It has a constant, positive density
\frac{1}{\mathsf{n}}
as soon as one looks at distance
\geq\bar{\mathtt{C}}^{\prime}\log T
from
\partial\Lambda
(with
\bar{\mathtt{C}}^{\prime}
depending on the “good external potential” constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
). On that region we have
\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}\equiv 0
.

For good external potentials, it is thus equivalent to consider the “2DOCP with external background”
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}
(as in (
4.10
)) or the “2DOCP with background measure”
\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\cdot,\mathbf{m},\zeta)
(with
\mathbf{m}:=\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
) and we will simply write
\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}
for the corresponding Gibbs measure (and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}
for expectations under
\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}
). We now compare the properties of this “generalized 2DOCP” to the ones of the original Gibbs measure
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}
. The “good properties” are easier to obtain away from the boundary of
\Lambda
and for simplicity we will often work in “the bulk”
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
defined as:

\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}:=\mathfrak{D}(\omega,T/2)\subset\mathfrak{D}(\omega,T)=\Lambda.

Global law

Recall that the event
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
was introduced in Definition
5.2
.

Proposition 5.7

(Global law for sub-systems with good external potential)
.

There exists a constant
C

Global

depending only on
\beta
and the “good potential” constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
such that:

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\hyperref@@ii[prop:global_law_SUBSYS]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{Global}}}T^{2}\log^{5}T.

(5.12)

We prove Proposition
5.7
in Section
D
. The bound (
5.12
) should of course be compared with (
2.7
) which is valid for the full system. When considering sub-systems with good external potentials we are (only) losing some power of
\log T
, which we have not tried to optimize.

Local laws in the bulk

Proposition 5.8

(Local laws for sub-systems with good external potential)
.

There exists a universal constant
\mathtt{C}
and a constant
C

Local

(depending only on
\beta
and the “good potential” constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
) such that if
T
is large enough (depending on
\beta,\bar{\mathtt{C}}
) then for all
\ell\geq\rho_{\beta}
, for all
x
in
\Lambda
, provided that the square
\square(x,\ell)
is included in
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
we have:

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\mathrm{Ener}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\square(x,\ell)\right)\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\hyperref@@ii[prop:local_law_SUBSYS]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{Local}}}\beta\ell^{2},

(5.13)

where the “electric energy” is computed with respect to the background measure
\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
, and also:

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{\mathtt{C}}\mathsf{Pts}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\square(x,\ell)\right)\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\hyperref@@ii[prop:local_law_SUBSYS]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{Local}}}\beta\ell^{2}.

This should be compared to Proposition
2.4
for the full system.

Proof of Proposition
5.8
.

This follows from
[
Leb17
,
AS21
]
, but it requires some explanation.

We wish to obtain local laws, namely good controls on the electric energy (controls on the number of points are obtained as a byproduct) that are proportional to the volume down to large enough microscopic scales as in
[
AS21
, Thm. 1]
. Only this time we are in presence of an external potential / a non-uniform background measure
\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
, and we were only able to derive a
global bound
as in Proposition
5.7
, with a global energy estimate that is slightly larger than the total volume. Our goal is thus twofold:

1.

Extend the bootstrap in scales of
[
Leb17
,
AS21
]
to a situation where the background measure is not constant.

2.

Show that the bootstrap in scale not only
propagates
estimates to smaller scales, but in fact
improves
(if needed) the estimates at each step, which allows to get rid of the logarithmic correction in (
5.12
)

The first point is, in fact, very simple. Indeed, although the methods of
[
Leb17
,
AS21
]
are
not
suited to situations where the background measure has singularities or holes (in fact this is the reason why local laws are not proven near the edge for the full system), they are local by design and work as soon as we look at distances
\geq\mathtt{C}\log T
from the boundary, because then the background measure
\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
is equal to
1
(see Section
5.3
). Since we only care about the bulk of
\Lambda
, this is fine.

The second point is more interesting. The basic tool for the proof of local laws is the fact that thanks to the screening construction of Serfaty
et al.
one can, up to some errors, decouple the system in a given region
\Omega\subset\Lambda
of characteristic length
\mathcal{R}
from the system in
\Lambda\setminus\Omega
. The main price to pay is the first energy error term in
[
AS21
, (4.7)]
which reads “
\frac{\ell}{\tilde{\ell}}S
”. In their framework the quantity
\ell
must be such that
\ell^{3}\geq\frac{S}{\tilde{\ell}}
(
[
AS21
, (4.4)]
) and
\tilde{\ell}
can be chosen
5
5

5

The interested reader can find a list of conditions on
\ell,\tilde{\ell}
at the end of the proof of
[
AS21
, Prop. 4.5]
. The choice of
\tilde{\ell}
mentioned in
[
AS21
, (4.27)]
correspond to the
smallest
possible choice, but increasing
\tilde{\ell}
up to
\mathcal{R}
still gives a valid choice.
of order
\mathcal{R}
, so in fact we are paying a price of order
\left(\frac{S}{\mathcal{R}}\right)^{4/3}
. Moreover the quantity
S
can always be bounded by the energy in a domain
2\Omega
twice as large. So
if we know
that the energy
E
at scale
2\mathcal{R}
is typically
E(2\mathcal{R})
, then we pay a price
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{E(2\mathcal{R})}{\mathcal{R}}\right)^{4/3}
. In
[
Leb17
,
AS21
]
(see also
[
BBNY17
]
) one starts from an estimate
E(2\mathcal{R})\sim(2\mathcal{R})^{2}
(the energy scales like the volume) and gets an error of order
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}^{4/3})\ll\mathcal{R}^{2}
which is indeed negligible (this can in fact be improved further down by chosing
\ell,\tilde{\ell}
more cleverly, see the proof of
[
AS21
, Prop. 2.5]
).

The key observation
6
6

6

Having a poorer estimate is equivalent to having the parameter
\mathcal{C}
in
[
AS21
, (4.25)]
depend on
\mathcal{R}
. The conditions written at the end of the proof of
[
AS21
, Proposition 4.5]
can still be satisfied as long as
\mathcal{C}
is much smaller than
\mathcal{R}
, which corresponds to an initial energy estimate in
o(\mathcal{R}^{3})
. If
\mathcal{C}
is smaller than
\mathcal{R}^{3/2}
then the error term will become smaller than
\mathcal{R}^{2}

in one step
, otherwise one would need to apply the bootstrap in scales for some time (i.e. go down in scales) before reaching the desired local laws. Our situation corresponds to
\mathcal{C}=\log^{5}\mathcal{R}
, cf. (
5.12
).
is that even if we start from a poorer estimate, say with logarithmic corrections like
E(2\mathcal{R})\sim\left(2\mathcal{R}\right)^{2}\log^{100}(\mathcal{R})
(cf. the one implied by our global law (
5.12
)) then the error
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{E(2\mathcal{R})}{\mathcal{R}}\right)^{4/3}
remains much smaller than
\mathcal{R}^{2}
. In fact, a careful inspection of the proof (which we will not need here) shows that one could start with a “global law” as bad as
\mathcal{O}(T^{5/2})
, or even
o(T^{3})
instead of (
5.12
) and still recover good local laws in the bulk.
∎

5.4
Sub-systems with good external potentials: consequences

Discrepancy bounds

Once local laws hold (in the bulk), we retrieve all statements that rely purely on energy considerations. In particular the analogous of
[
AS21
, (1.18)]
is valid, namely:

Lemma 5.9

(Discrepancy bounds in sub-systems)
.

If
\square(x,\ell)\subset\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
then:

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{\mathtt{C}}\frac{\mathsf{Dis}^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\square(x,\ell))}{\ell^{2}}\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\hyperref@@ii[prop:local_law_SUBSYS]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{Local}}}.

(5.14)

In particular, we have an a priori Poisson-like bound on the number variance:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{Dis}^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\square(x,\ell))\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\mathtt{C}\hyperref@@ii[prop:local_law_SUBSYS]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{Local}}}\ell^{2},

together with a tail estimate: if
\ell
is larger than some constant depending on
\beta,\bar{\mathtt{C}}

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[|\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\square(x,\ell))|\geq\ell\log\ell\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}\ell}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right).

Proof of Claim
5.9
.

The proof is as in
[
AS21
]
, using an inequality that relates the presence of discrepancy to a certain energy cost, e.g.
[
AS21
, Lemma B.4]
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

As an inspection of the (short) proof of
[
AS21
, Lemma B.4]
quickly reveals, there is nothing specific to a square in the previous claim, and it also applies to a disk of radius
\ell
, or to any “reasonable” shape like the boxes introduced in Definition
3.4
.

Treating smooth linear statistics

Following exactly the same proof as in
[
LS18
,
Ser20
]
(or alternatively as in
[
BBNY19
]
), one would obtain a control on linear statistics of smooth enough test functions supported in the bulk
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
. We do not need it here, however it will be crucial for us to retrieve a specific property (the “smallness of the anisotropy”), but since this only serves as a tool for another result (the quantitative translation-invariance estimate presented in Section
6
) we postpone the corresponding discussion to an appendix (see Section
E.4
). The only result that we will quote directly is one about
expectations
for fluctuations of linear statistics.

Lemma 5.11

(Expectation of linear statistics in the bulk of subsystems)
.

There exists a constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
depending only on
\beta
, and on the “local laws” constant
C

Local

(thus on the “good potential” constant) such that the following holds.

Let
\varphi
be a function of class
C^{2}
, compactly supported on a disk of radius
\ell\geq\rho_{\beta}
included in
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
. Then:

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\right|\leq\bar{\mathtt{C}}|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}\ell^{2}.

(5.15)

Proof of Lemma
5.11
.

This follows from the proofs of
[
LS18
,
Ser20
]
but is not explicitly written as such. Let
t
be a small parameter, we know from
[
LS18
, Prop. 2.10]
that:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[e^{t\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]}\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]=\frac{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}(\mathbf{m}_{s})}{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}(\mathbf{m})}e^{\mathcal{O}(t^{2})},

where
\mathbf{m}_{s}
is the measure
\mathbf{m}_{s}:=\mathbf{m}-s\Delta\varphi
, where
s=\frac{t}{2\pi\beta}
. This is valid because
\varphi
is assumed to be supported in
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
, where
\mathbf{m}
has density
1
. By e.g.
[
LS18
, Lemma 3.6]
we know that we can replace
\mathbf{m}_{s}
by the approximate measure
\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{s}:=(\Id+s\nabla\varphi)\#\mathbf{m}
up to quadratic terms, i.e.:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[e^{t\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]}\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]=\frac{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{s})}{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}(\mathbf{m})}e^{\mathcal{O}(t^{2})}.

Next, a consequence of
[
Ser20
, Prop. 4.2]
is that the ratio of partition functions can be written as:

\frac{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{s})}{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}(\mathbf{m})}=\exp\left(s\mathcal{O}\left(|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\supp\nabla\varphi)\right)+\mathcal{O}(s^{2})\right),

and thus taking the limit
t\to 0
(or equivalently
s\to 0
) and using the local laws we get (
5.15
) after identifying the first order terms.
∎

6
Quantitative estimate on translation-invariance

In this section, we denote by
\vec{u}
be the vector
\vec{u}:=(0,1)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

6.1
The “spin wave” and its properties

An auxiliary function

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, let
f^{(\varepsilon)}:x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be defined as:

f^{(\varepsilon)}(x):=\begin{cases}x_{1}&\text{ if }|x|\leq 1\\
x_{1}(1-\varepsilon\log|x|)&\text{ if }1\leq|x|\leq e^{1/\varepsilon}\\
0&\text{ if }|x|\geq e^{1/\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

The function
f^{(\varepsilon)}
is continuous, piecewise
C^{2}
and compactly supported on the disk of radius
e^{1/\varepsilon}
. We have, by direct computations:

•

On (the interior of) the unit disk,
\partial_{1}f^{(\varepsilon)}\equiv 1
,
\partial_{2}f^{(\varepsilon)}\equiv 0
and the second (and third) derivatives of
f^{(\varepsilon)}
vanish.

•

For
1\leq|x|\leq e^{1/\varepsilon}
, we have :

–

\partial_{1}f^{(\varepsilon)}(x)=1-\varepsilon\log|x|-\varepsilon\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{|x|^{2}}
,
\partial_{2}f^{(\varepsilon)}(x)=-\varepsilon\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{|x|^{2}}
.

–

The second derivatives of
f^{(\varepsilon)}
satisfy the pointwise bound
|f^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}(x)\preceq\frac{\varepsilon}{|x|}
.

–

The third derivatives of
f^{(\varepsilon)}
satisfy the pointwise bound
|f^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{3},\star}(x)\preceq\frac{\varepsilon}{|x|^{2}}
.

In particular, observe that the first partial derivatives of
f^{(\varepsilon)}
are bounded by
1
with a jump of size
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
along both
\partial\mathfrak{D}(0,1)
and
\partial\mathfrak{D}(0,e^{1/\varepsilon})
, while the second partial derivatives have a jump of size
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
along
\partial\mathfrak{D}(0,1)
and of size
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon/e^{1/\varepsilon})
along
\partial\mathfrak{D}(0,e^{1/\varepsilon})
.

Thus after applying a mollification to
f^{(\varepsilon)}
at scale
\frac{1}{2}
near
\partial\mathfrak{D}(0,1)
and at scale
\frac{1}{2}e^{1/\varepsilon}
near
\partial\mathfrak{D}(0,e^{1/\varepsilon})
we may consider a function
\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}
which is smooth, compactly supported in
\mathfrak{D}(0,2e^{1/\varepsilon})
and such that (for some universal constant
\mathtt{C}
):

•

\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}(x)=x_{1}
for
|x|\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

•

The first derivatives of
\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}
are bounded by
2
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

•

|\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon
for
|x|\leq 2
and
|\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon}{|x|}
for
2\leq|x|\leq 2e^{1/\varepsilon}
.

•

|\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{3},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon
for
|x|\leq 2
and
|\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{3},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon}{|x|^{2}}
for
2\leq|x|\leq 2e^{1/\varepsilon}
.

Definition of the “spin wave”

Next, we define our “spin wave”
7
7

7

This terminology alludes to similar constructions used in the theory of continuous spin systems to prove so-called “Mermin-Wagner” theorems, see e.g.
[
FV17
, Sec. 9.2]
or
[
Sim14
, Chapter 3]
.

\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
as the vector field

\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}:=\nabla^{\perp}\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}=\left(-\partial_{2}\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)},\partial_{1}\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}\right).

Lemma 6.1
.

The following properties of
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
are straightforward:

1.

\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
is smooth, compactly supported on
\mathfrak{D}(0,2e^{1/\varepsilon})
(because so is
\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}
).

2.

\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}(x)=\vec{u}=(0,1)
for
|x|\leq\frac{1}{2}
(because then
\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}
).

3.

\Div\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(by definition of
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
as the perpendicular gradient of a smooth function).

4.

|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{0}}\leq 2
(it is bounded by the first derivative of
\bar{f}^{(\varepsilon)}
),
|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon
and more precisely:

\left|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}\right|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\times\begin{cases}\varepsilon&\text{ for }|x|\leq 2\\
\frac{\varepsilon}{|x|}&\text{ for }2\leq|x|\leq 2e^{1/\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

(6.1)

5.

|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon
for
|x|\leq 2
and
|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon}{|x|^{2}}
for
2\leq|x|\leq 2e^{1/\varepsilon}
.

We have thus constructed a smooth,
divergence-free
vector field which is constant near the origin
and has an arbitrary small
\mathrm{H}^{1}
norm
(of order
\varepsilon
). The downside is that the size of the support of
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
is
exponential
with respect to the parameter
1/\varepsilon
.

6.2
Slowly varying localized translations

For
\ell>0
, let the vector field
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}
be defined for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}(x):=\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}(x/\ell)
. Since
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}
is continuous and compactly supported, it generates a global flow
\{\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}\}_{t}
with the following properties:

Lemma 6.2
.

For all
|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}
we have:

1.

\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}
is an area-preserving diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

2.

We have
\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}(x)=x+t\vec{u}
for
|x|\leq\ell/4
.

3.

We have
\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}(x)=x
for
|x|\geq 2\ell e^{1/\varepsilon}
.

Thus for fixed
|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}
the diffeomorphism
\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}
coincides with the translation by
t\vec{u}
on the disk
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/4)
and with the identity outside
\mathfrak{D}(0,2e^{1/\varepsilon})
, we call it a
localized translation
as in
[
Geo99
]
. The main difference with the construction of
[
Geo99
]
is that we have
\int|\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}-\Id|_{\mathsf{1},\star}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
instead of
\mathcal{O}(1)
. Interestingly enough, a
bounded (but not small)

H^{1}
norm for
\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}-\Id
(which induces a bounded, but not small energy cost, as we will show in Section
E
) is enough to prove translation-invariance
in the infinite-volume setting
, but fails to give anything valuable in finite-volume. However, according to a remark in
[
Sim14
, Sec. III.7]

“it appears that any model in which this weaker property is valid, the [possibility of finding a construction with arbitrarily small energy cost exists]”
. This remains very intriguing to us.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

The fact that
\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}
is area-preserving follows from Liouville’s theorem, since the vector field
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
(and thus
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}
) is divergence-free by construction. Moreover, since we ensured that
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}\equiv\vec{u}
on
\mathfrak{D}(0,\frac{1}{2})
, the rescaled vector field
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}
coincides with
\vec{u}
on the disk
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/2)
hence we have
\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}(x)=x+t\vec{u}
as long as
x+t\vec{u}
remains in
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/2)
. In particular, if
|x|\leq\ell/4
and since
|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}
by assumption, we have
\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}(x)=x+t\vec{u}
. On the other hand,
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}
vanishes identically outside
\mathfrak{D}(0,2\ell e^{1/\varepsilon})
and thus the flow there coincides with the identity map.
∎

We will study
\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}
a bit more closely in Section
E.1
for technical purposes.

6.3
Effect of localized translations on the energy

We now apply the “spin wave” / “localized translation” construction to a sub-system with good external potential. Let
\Lambda
(a disk of radius
T
),
\mathsf{n}
,
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
be as in Section
5
, with
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
a good external potential, let
\mathbf{m}=\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
be the corresponding non-uniform background and assume that the properties listed in Section
5.3
hold. Let
L
be as in Section
3
and let us choose
\varepsilon,\ell
in such a way that:

e^{\sqrt{\log\ell}}\leq L\leq\frac{\ell}{10},\quad 5\ell e^{1/\varepsilon}\leq T\leq 10\ell e^{1/\varepsilon},\quad\log\ell\leq\varepsilon^{-1},\quad\varepsilon^{-1}\leq\ell^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{-3}.

(6.2)

Let
\mathrm{ErrAve}(t,\varepsilon,\ell,\mathbf{X})
denote the following “averaging error”:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{t}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{-t}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})\right)+\mathrm{ErrAve}(t\vec{u},\varepsilon,\ell,\mathbf{X}),

(6.3)

where
\Phi_{t}\mathbf{X}
denotes the configuration obtained after applying
\Phi_{t}
to all the points of
\mathbf{X}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

There exists a constant
\mathtt{C}
depending on
\beta
on the “local laws” constant
C

Local

(and thus on the good external potential constant) such that:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\sup_{|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}}|\mathrm{ErrAve}(t\vec{u},\varepsilon,\ell,\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}})|\leq\mathtt{C}t^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\geq 1-\exp\left(-\ell^{2}\right).

(6.4)

We prove Proposition
6.3
in Section
E
and use it next to “mollify” observables before taking expectations.

6.4
Effect of localized translations on expectations

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a measurable function on
\mathrm{Conf}(\Lambda)
, let
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathrm{Conf}(\Lambda)
be an event. Assume that the function
\mathsf{G}
is
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/10)
-local and that
\mathcal{E}
is
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
-local. Recall that
|t|\leq\ell/10
.

For all
\tau\in(0,1)
and for all
\sigma>0
, we have:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\mathsf{ErrorQI}(t\vec{u},\varepsilon,\ell,\tau,\sigma,\mathsf{G},\mathbf{X}),

(6.5)

with an error term
\mathsf{ErrorQI}
bounded as follows:

\left|\mathsf{ErrorQI}(t\vec{u},\varepsilon,\ell,\tau,\sigma,\mathsf{G},\mathbf{X})\right|\leq 2\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X}\pm t\vec{u})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(e^{\beta\tau}-1\right)\\
+2e^{\beta\tau}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X}\pm\vec{u})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[|\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}\pm t\vec{u})|\geq\sigma\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
+\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathrm{ErrAve}(t\vec{u},\varepsilon,\ell,\mathbf{X})\geq\tau\big|\mathcal{E}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(6.6)

We postpone the proof of Proposition
6.4
to Section
E.5
. There is of course nothing special about the vector
\vec{u}
, and we may replace it by any unit vector.

6.5
Application: expectation of discrepancies in sub-systems

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a box of size
L
as introduced in Definition
3.4
. Assume that it is contained in the disk
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/10)
. Then we have:

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathfrak{B})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\right|\leq\bar{\mathtt{C}}L\left(\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\log L\right)^{1/3}.

(6.7)

The point of (
6.7
) is that if
\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\log L
is
\ll 1
then the bound on the average discrepancy is
\ll L
, and thus
much better than the crude estimate via the standard deviation
using Claim
5.9
. This is crucial for us.

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

Let us take
\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}):=\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\mathfrak{B})
, which is clearly
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/10)
-local in view of our assumption on
\mathfrak{B}
. Moreover
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
(by its Definition
5.2
) is clearly
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
-local. Fix the parameter
\bar{t}
as:

\bar{t}:=\left(\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\log L\right)^{-1/3}.

(6.8)

We first check that
\bar{t}
has the correct range (using (
6.2
)):

\bar{t}\leq\varepsilon^{-1/3}\leq\ell^{2/3}\leq\frac{\ell}{10}.

In particular, for all vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|v\|\leq\bar{t}
we may apply the result of Proposition
6.4
to a localized translation in the direction
\frac{v}{\|v\|}
instead of
\vec{u}
.

Introduce a smooth cut-off function
\overline{\chi}
equal to
1
for
|x|\leq\frac{\bar{t}}{2}
and to
0
for
|x|\geq\bar{t}
, such that
|\overline{\chi}|_{\mathsf{k}}\leq\mathtt{C}\bar{t}^{-k}
(for
\mathsf{k}=1,2
). We impose that
\overline{\chi}
be an even function. For all
|v|\leq\bar{t}
we have:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\mathfrak{B})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathfrak{B}(\mathbf{X}+v,\mathfrak{B})+\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}-v,\mathfrak{B})\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\mathsf{ErrorQI}(v,\varepsilon,\ell,\tau,\sigma,\mathsf{Dis}(\cdot,\mathfrak{B}),\mathbf{X}).

Integrating this against
\frac{\overline{\chi}}{|\overline{\chi}|_{L^{1}}}
(which has mass
1
) and using the fact that
\overline{\chi}
is even we obtain:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[]\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathfrak{B})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]=\frac{1}{|\overline{\chi}|_{L^{1}}}\int\overline{\chi}(v)\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}+v,\mathfrak{B})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right)+\mathsf{ErrorQI}(v)\mathtt{d}v.

Observe that
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}+v,\mathfrak{B})=\mathrm{Fluct}[\mathtt{1}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\cdot+v)](\mathbf{X})
, so that we may re-write the integral in the right-hand side as a convolution. Introducing the function
\varphi:=\mathtt{1}_{\mathfrak{B}}\ast\frac{\overline{\chi}}{|\overline{\chi}|_{L^{1}}}
we get:

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathfrak{B})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[]\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\right|\leq\sup_{|v|\leq\bar{t}}\mathsf{ErrorQI}\left(v,\varepsilon,\ell,\tau,\sigma,\mathsf{Dis}(\cdot,\mathfrak{B}),\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}\right).

(6.9)

By construction, the function
\varphi
is now a smooth cut-off function, equal to
1
on
\{x\in\mathfrak{B},\dist(x,\partial\mathfrak{B})\geq\bar{t}\}

and to
0
for
\{x\notin\mathfrak{B},\dist(x,\partial\mathfrak{B})\geq\bar{t}\}
. In particular, the support of
\nabla\varphi
has an area
\mathcal{O}(L\bar{t})
, and moreover we have
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{k}}\leq\mathtt{C}\bar{t}^{-\mathsf{k}}
for
\mathsf{k}=1,2
(by Young’s convolution inequality). From Lemma
5.11
, we thus know:

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[]\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]\right]\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right|\preceq\frac{1}{\bar{t}^{2}}\times L\bar{t}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{L}{\bar{t}}\right),

(6.10)

with multiplicative constants depending on
\beta,\delta
.

On the other hand, we know by Proposition
6.3
that choosing
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
large enough we can ensure:

\sup_{|v|\leq\bar{t}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\left\{\left|\mathrm{ErrAve}(v,\varepsilon,\ell,\mathbf{X})\right\}\right|\geq\bar{\mathtt{C}}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\bar{t}^{2}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\right)\leq\exp\left(-\ell^{2}/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right).

Moreover
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}+v,\mathfrak{B})=\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\mathfrak{B}-v)
and we have by Lemma
5.9
:
\sup_{|v|\leq\bar{t}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{Dis}^{2}(\mathbf{X},\mathfrak{B}-v)\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\bar{\mathtt{C}}L^{2}
(because the translated object
\mathfrak{B}-v
remains a box within
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
) and, still by Lemma
5.9
, for
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
large enough we have:

\sup_{|v|\leq\bar{t}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\left\{|\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}+v,\mathfrak{B})|\geq L\log L\right\}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{1}{\bar{\mathtt{C}}}\log^{2}L}.

(Remark that the
\sup
is outside
\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta},\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}
in those bounds - it would be significantly more challenging otherwise.)

Thus, choosing
\tau=\bar{\mathtt{C}}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\bar{t}^{2}
,
\sigma=L\log L
in the statement of Proposition
6.4
, the error term
\mathsf{ErrorQI}
reduces to:

\mathsf{ErrorQI}(v,\varepsilon,\ell,\tau,\sigma,\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\mathfrak{B}),\mathbf{X})\preceq\bar{\mathtt{C}}L\log L\left(\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\bar{t}^{2}+\exp\left(-\ell^{2}/\mathtt{C}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}}\log^{2}L\right)\right),

uniformly for
|v|\leq\bar{t}
and thus (using (
6.2
) and keeping only the dominant term):

\sup_{|v|\leq\bar{t}}\mathsf{ErrorQI}\leq\bar{\mathtt{C}}L\log L\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\bar{t}^{2}.

(6.11)

Combining (
6.9
) with (
6.10
) and (
6.11
), and using our choice (
6.8
) for
\bar{t}
, we obtain (
6.7
).
∎

Remark 6.6
.

One can use the same argument to study expectations of discrepancies
in the full system
. Then one does not need to use
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
since local laws are know to hold unconditionally, and one can take
\varepsilon
as small as
\log^{-1}N
.

Corollary 6.7

(An application)
.

Assume that
L,T
satisfy the following relation:

T=100L\exp(10L).

(6.12)

Then we have:

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\mathfrak{B})\right]\right|\leq\bar{\mathtt{C}}L^{0.67}.

(6.13)

Proof.

We can take
\ell=10L
and
\varepsilon=\ell^{-1}=\frac{1}{10L}
, and
T=100L\exp(10L)=10\ell e^{1/\varepsilon}
. The conditions of (
6.2
) are clearly satisfied. The right-hand side of (
6.7
) is then bounded by
\bar{\mathtt{C}}L^{2/3}\log L=\mathcal{O}(L^{0.67})
(for
L
large enough).
∎

7
Conclusion: proof of Theorem
1

Let
\delta>0
be fixed. Let
x,R
be such that
\dist(\mathfrak{D}(x,R),\partial\Sigma_{N})\geq\delta\sqrt{N}
as assumed in (
1.5
). Let
\varepsilon_{R}
be chosen as:

\varepsilon_{R}:=\log^{-0.3}(R),

(7.1)

For simplicity, we will focus on the case of an
excess
of points, i.e. a positive discrepancy, the other case being treated similarly. Define the event
\mathcal{A}_{R}
:

\mathcal{A}_{R}:=\{\mathsf{Dis}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}(x,R)\right)\geq\varepsilon_{R}R\}

The conclusion that we want to reach (as stated in (
1.6
)) is that for
R,\bar{\mathtt{C}}
large enough (depending on
\beta
and on the parameter
\delta
, but not on
x
):

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{A}_{R}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\log^{1.5}R\right).

(7.2)

We have not tried to optimize
\varepsilon_{R}
or the exponent in (
7.2
), what matters for us is that
\varepsilon_{R}\to 0
and that
1.5>1
so our probabilistic tail is better than algebraic. With the methods of the present paper, there is a hard limit on the smallness of
\varepsilon_{R}
- it has to be at least
\log^{-1}R
.

Step 1. Choosing
L
and cornering the discrepancy

Let
L
be chosen as:

L:=\log^{0.99}R.

(7.3)

In particular,
for
R
greater than some constant
(depending only on
\beta
) we have:

\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{R}}=\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}\log^{0.3}R\leq\log^{0.99}R=L\leq R/10,

(7.4)

(where
\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}
is the constant depending only on
\beta
introduced in Proposition
3.1
) so the first condition of (
3.1
) is satisfied.

Moreover let
s
be chosen as
\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}}\min\left(\frac{L^{3}}{R},L\varepsilon_{R}\right)
, namely (for
R
large enough):
s:=\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}}\frac{L^{3}}{R}
. By definition, the second condition of (
3.1
) is then satisfied. Let us compute:

\exp\left(-\frac{s\varepsilon_{R}R}{4}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon_{R}L^{3}}{4\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{-2.67}R}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right).

For each
k
with
0\leq k\leq R^{2}
, let
\mathcal{B}_{R}(k)
be the event:

\mathcal{B}_{R}(k):=\left\{\text{The discrepancy in the annulus $\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{R-2L+\frac{kL}{R^{2}}}$ is larger than $\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R$}\right\}

Combining Proposition
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
we obtain:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}(\mathcal{A}_{R})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{R^{2}}\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}(\mathcal{B}_{R}(k))+\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{-2.67}R}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right),

(7.5)

and we now focus on bounding
\mathcal{B}_{R}(k)
(the index
k
plays no particular role, and for simplicity we forget about it).

For each
k
, as explained in Section
3.2
, we can decompose the annulus
\mathfrak{D}_{R}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{R-2L+\frac{kL}{R^{2}}}
into boxes
\left\{\mathfrak{B}_{i},i\in\{0,\dots,\frac{R}{L}-1\}\right\}
of size
L
as in Definition
3.4
.

Step 2. Choosing
T,M
and a well-separated family

Let
T
be chosen (as in (
6.7
)) as:

T:=100\times\log^{0.99}R\times\exp\left(10\log^{0.99}R\right)=100L\exp\left(10L\right),

(7.6)

and let
M
be chosen as:

M:=T^{6}.

(7.7)

Since
L=\log^{0.99}R
(according to (
7.3
)), the conditions of (
3.4
) are clearly satisfied for
R
large enough.

Now, for
l\in\{0,\dots,M-1\}
, let
\mathcal{C}(l)
be the event:

\mathcal{C}(l):=\left\{\sum_{i=l\textrm{ mod }M}\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{B}_{i})\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{R}R}{4M}.\right\}

(7.8)

By Lemma
3.5
we know that:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}(\mathcal{B}_{R})\leq\sum_{l=0}^{M-1}\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}(l)\right),

(7.9)

and we now focus on bounding
\mathcal{C}(l)
(again, the index
l
plays no role in the sequel). The index
l
being fixed, we only consider the boxes
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
for
i\equiv l\mod M
and forget about the other boxes. We relabel those boxes as
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,\mathcal{N}\}
where
\mathcal{N}
is the cardinality of that family of boxes, with:

\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R}{ML}\right).

(7.10)

As in Section
3.2
we let
\Lambda_{i}
be the disk
\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T)
, where
\omega_{i}
is the “center” of the box
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
, and we recall that
\mathrm{d}_{ij}
denotes the distance between
\Lambda_{i}
and
\Lambda_{j}
. Using (
3.6
) and summing over
i=1,\dots,\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{O}(\frac{R}{ML})
, we get:

\max_{1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}_{ij}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log R}{ML}\right),\quad\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathcal{N}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}_{ij}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R\log R}{(ML)^{2}}\right).

(7.11)

A “good event”

Let
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
be the logarithmic potential generated by the system outside the
\Lambda_{i}
’s as in (
5.6
). For
\bar{\mathtt{C}}>0
, let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\bar{\mathtt{C}})
be the event:

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\bar{\mathtt{C}}):=\left\{\text{$\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}$ is a “good external potential” on each $\Lambda_{i}$ with constant $\bar{\mathtt{C}}$.}\right\}

(7.12)

(See Definition
5.1
). By Proposition
5.3
we know that if
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
is chosen large enough and if
T
is large enough (i.e.
R
is large enough) depending only on
\beta
and on the parameter
\delta
from (
1.5
), then we have:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\bar{\mathtt{C}})\right)\geq 1-\mathcal{N}\exp\left(-\log^{2}T/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right).

Since
\mathcal{N}
is always smaller than
R=e^{\log R}
(see (
7.10
)) and since, by our choice (
7.6
) of
T
we have:

\exp\left(-\log^{2}T/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\log^{1.98}R/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right)\ll\exp(-\log R),

we deduce that (for
R,\bar{\mathtt{C}}
large enough depending on
\beta,\delta
):

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\bar{\mathtt{C}})\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\log^{2}T/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right).

(7.13)

On the other hand, for each
i=1,\dots,\mathcal{N}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{i}
be the event: “
\Lambda_{i}
is a good sub-system”, as in Definition
5.2
. By Lemma
5.4
, we know that:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)\geq 1-\mathcal{N}\exp\left(-\log^{2}T/\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\right),

and by the same parameter comparison as above we get:
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\log^{2}T/\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\right)
. We thus have (for
R,\bar{\mathtt{C}}
large enough):

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\bar{\mathtt{C}})\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)+\exp\left(-\log^{2}T/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right).

(7.14)

Using approximate conditional independence

Let
\omega
be chosen as:

\omega:=L^{-1.33}.

(7.15)

For
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
, let
\mathsf{G}_{i}
be the following function on
\mathrm{Conf}
, which is clearly non-negative and
\Lambda_{i}
-local:

\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}):=e^{\omega\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X},\mathfrak{B}_{i})}.

(7.16)

If
\mathbf{X}_{N}
is in
\mathcal{C}
then by definition (see (
7.8
)) we have:

\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})=e^{\omega\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{B}_{i})}\geq\exp\left(\frac{\omega\varepsilon_{R}R}{4M}\right),

and thus by Markov’s inequality (dropping the
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
-dependency):

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\omega\varepsilon_{R}R}{4M}\right)\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}\cap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}}\right].

(7.17)

We are in a position to use Proposition
4.3
with
\mathcal{E}_{N}:=\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}\cap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}
. We obtain:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}\cap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}}\right]\leq\exp\left(2\beta\sup_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\mathrm{ErrorCI}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\right)\\
\times\sup_{\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ext}}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}},\{\mathsf{n}_{i}\}\ \mathrm{adm.}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i},\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}_{i}\right]

(7.18)

Let us note that the event
\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}
implies that the “admissible” number of points in each
\Lambda_{i}
is bounded by
10T^{2}
(see Definition
5.2
) which checks the first condition of (
4.6
), and that the second condition of (
4.6
) is implied by the third condition
8
8

8

The distance between two centers
\omega_{i},\omega_{j}
is bounded below by
ML
and thus
\dist(\Lambda_{i},\Lambda_{j})\geq ML-2T\geq 98T
.
of (
3.4
). We may thus use Lemma
4.1
which, together with (
3.6
), implies that the conditional independence error
\mathrm{ErrorCI}
between the
\Lambda_{i}
’s is bounded by:

\sup_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}}|\mathrm{ErrorCI}[\mathbf{X}_{N}|\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{\mathcal{N}}]|=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{T^{5}R\log R}{(ML)^{2}}\right),

(7.19)

which controls the first term in the right-hand side of (
7.18
), and we now focus on the second one.

Controlling expectations in each “sub-system”

Let us fix
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}
and work in
\Lambda_{i}
. We can assume that
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
-good on
\Lambda_{i}
because of (
7.12
).

Claim 7.1
.

Let
\mathsf{D}_{i}:=\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathfrak{B}_{i})
. We have:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]=1+\mathcal{O}\left(L^{-0.66}\right)\leq e^{\mathcal{O}(L^{-0.66})}

(7.20)

Proof.

We start by decomposing the expectation as:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]=\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\mathtt{1}_{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]+\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\mathtt{1}_{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right].

(7.21)

Using a Taylor’s expansion, we may write the first term in the right-hand side of (
7.21
) as:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\mathtt{1}_{\omega|\mathsf{D}_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]=\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[\left(1+\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}+\mathcal{O}\left(\omega^{2}\mathsf{D}_{i}^{2}\right)\right)\mathtt{1}_{\omega|\mathsf{D}_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]\\
\leq\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[\mathtt{1}_{\omega|\mathsf{D}_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]+\omega\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}[\mathsf{D}_{i}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}]+\mathcal{O}\left(\omega^{2}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}[\mathsf{D}_{i}^{2}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}]\right)+\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[\omega|\mathsf{D}_{i}|\mathtt{1}_{\omega|\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right].

(7.22)

Using Corollary
6.7
to control
\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}[\mathsf{D}_{i}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}]
and Lemma
5.9
to control
\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}[\mathsf{D}_{i}^{2}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}]
, and inserting into (
7.21
) we obtain:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]\leq 1+\omega\mathcal{O}(L^{0.67})+\omega^{2}\mathcal{O}(L^{2})\\
+\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\mathtt{1}_{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]+\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[\omega|\mathsf{D}_{i}|\mathtt{1}_{\omega|\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\right]-\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[\mathtt{1}_{\omega|\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right],

(7.23)

and it remains to control the second line of (
7.23
), using Lemma
5.9
. We write:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\mathtt{1}_{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{2\omega^{2}L^{2}\frac{\mathsf{D}^{2}_{i}}{L^{2}}}\mathtt{1}_{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]\\
\leq\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{4\omega^{2}L^{2}\frac{\mathsf{D}^{2}_{i}}{L^{2}}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[\mathtt{1}_{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq e^{\bar{\mathtt{C}}\omega^{2}L^{2}}\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[\left\{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}\right\}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right],

where we have used Cauchy-Schwarz’s ineaquality, then the fact that
\omega^{2}L^{2}\ll 1
(see (
7.15
)) in order to apply Hölder’s inequality, and the exponential moment (
5.14
). Using (
5.14
) again we get that:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[\left\{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}\right\}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]\leq\exp\left(-L^{0.66}/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right),

using Markov’s inequality the fact that
\frac{L^{2}}{\omega^{2}}=L^{0.66}
(see (
7.15
)). We can thus write:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\mathtt{1}_{|\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}|>\frac{1}{2}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-L^{0.66}/\bar{\mathtt{C}}}\right).

The two other terms in the second line of (
7.23
) are handled the same way. We obtain:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{\mathsf{n}_{i},\Lambda_{i}}\left[e^{\omega\mathsf{D}_{i}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{i}}\right]\leq 1+\omega\mathcal{O}(L^{0.67})+\omega^{2}\mathcal{O}(L^{2})+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-L^{0.66}/\bar{\mathtt{C}}}\right),

Inserting
9
9

9

There might be a confusion between the
error
term
\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-L^{0.66}/\bar{\mathtt{C}}}\right)
and the fact that we write
1+\mathcal{O}\left(L^{-0.66}\right)\leq e^{\mathcal{O}(L^{-0.66})}
, but
e^{-L^{0.66}}
and
e^{L^{-0.66}}
are two different terms.
the value
\omega=L^{-1.33}
we obtain (
7.20
).
∎

Conclusion.

Inserting (
7.19
) and (
7.20
) into (
7.18
) we obtain:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathsf{G}_{i}(\mathbf{X}_{N})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}\cap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}}\right]\leq\exp\left(\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{T^{5}R\log R}{(ML)^{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R}{ML}L^{-0.66}\right)\right).

Since
M=T^{6}
(by (
7.7
)) it is easy to check that (for
R
large enough) the dominant term in the exponent is by far the second one because
T\gg L
. Returning to (
7.17
) we thus obtain:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\bar{\mathtt{C}})\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\omega\varepsilon_{R}R}{4M}\right)\exp\left(\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R}{ML}L^{-0.66}\right)\right),

and since
\omega=L^{-1.33}
((
7.15
)),
L=\log^{0.99}R
((
7.3
)) and
\varepsilon_{R}=\log^{-0.3}R
((
7.1
)) the first factor dominates the second one:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\bar{\mathtt{C}})\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\omega\varepsilon_{R}R}{8M}\right),

and thus after inserting the value of
M
(
7.7
) defined in terms of
T
as in (
7.6
) we obtain the following Markov-type inequality:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\bar{\mathtt{C}})\bigcap_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\exp\left(\log R+o(\log R)\right)\right).

In particular, for
R
large enough this is smaller than
\exp\left(-R^{1/2}\right)
. Returning successively to (
7.14
), (
7.9
), (
7.5
) and using the fact that
\log T\geq\log^{0.99}R
we obtain:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\log^{1.98}R/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right),\quad\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{B}_{R}\right)\leq\exp\left(\mathcal{O}(\log R)-\log^{1.98}R/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\log^{1.98}R/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right),

and finally:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathcal{A}_{R}\right)\leq\exp\left(\mathcal{O}(\log R)-\log^{1.98}R/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\log^{1.98}R/\bar{\mathtt{C}}\right)

which proves (
7.2
) and thus concludes the proof of Theorem
1
.

Appendix A
Discussion of the model

There are several slightly different ways to define the two-dimensional one-component plasma.

•

Some papers work with an “infinitely extended equilibrium” Coulomb system, e.g.
[
MY80
,
Leb83
,
JLM93
]
. The mathematical existence of such infinite-volume limits is not yet clear for
\beta\neq 2
, see however
[
AS21
, Corollary 1.1]
for existence of infinite-volume
limit points
in the weak topology.

•

In the statistical physics literature, it is common to place
N
particles in a “uniform neutralizing background of opposite charge” which occupies a certain domain
\Sigma_{N}
with constant density
\rho_{N}:=\frac{-N}{|\Sigma_{N}|}
. There is
perfect confinement
in the sense that the particles are not allowed to live outside
\Sigma_{N}
. The domain is not always explicitely chosen, though it often ends up being a disk, mostly by default or for the convenience of symmetry
10
10

10

There is also some interest for studying the 2DOCP on a sphere, which avoids having to deal with a boundary.
. Some authors state their results for different “reasonable shapes” as e.g.
[
SM76
]
. The influence of the background can be seen as applying some potential to each point charge, while they all interact with each other. Indeed, one may write:

\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})=\frac{1}{2}\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{N}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{N}(y)+\int\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{back}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{N}(x)+C_{N},

(A.1)

where
\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{back}}
is the logarithmic potential generated by the background, namely:

\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{back}}(x):=\int_{\Sigma_{N}}-\log|x-y|\rho_{N}\mathtt{d}y,

and
C_{N}
is a constant (the self-interaction of the background with itself) that does not depend on
\mathbf{X}_{N}
(only on
N
) and can thus be absorbed in
\mathrm{K}^{\beta}_{N}
. This model is sometimes called a
jellium
.

•

In the mathematical physics literature around the planar Coulomb gas (e.g.
[
ZW06
,
AHM11
,
SS15
]
) the particles/eigenvalues
\mathrm{X}_{N}
are usually not confined
a priori
in a certain domain of the Euclidean space, but are rather subject to a certain external “confining” potential/field/weight
\mathrm{V}
acting as
\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{back}}
in (
A.1
) (this model is sometimes called a
(two-dimensional)
\beta
-ensemble
by analogy with well-known one-dimensional models coming from random matrix theory). Via a certain mean-field energy functional, the choice of
\mathrm{V}
determines
11
11

11

Alternatively, one can associate to
\mathrm{V}
a “thermal equilibrium measure” as in
[
AS21
]
, which has unbounded support and plays the role of those three objects.
:

1.

A compact subset
\Sigma_{N}
, sometimes called the droplet.

2.

An
equilibrium measure

\mu_{N}
on
\Sigma_{N}
.

3.

An effective confining potential
\zeta_{N}
.

After “splitting” (see
[
SS15
, Lemma 3.1]
), the energy takes the following form, cf. (
1.1
) and (
A.1
):

\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N})=\frac{1}{2}\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N}-\mu_{N}\right)(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N}-\mu_{N}\right)(y)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\zeta_{N}(x_{i})+C_{N}.

(A.2)

The confining potential
\zeta_{N}
vanishes on
\Sigma_{N}
and is positive outside of it - thus penalizing particles that leave
\Sigma_{N}
, and indeed the confinement is strong: few particles fall outside
\Sigma_{N}
and if they do they stay close by (see
[
Ame21
]
for a quantitative statement). Hence in typical situations most of the particles are located on
\Sigma_{N}
and arrange themselves according to the density
\mu_{N}
. The canonical choice is the quadratic potential
\mathrm{V}(x)=|x|^{2}
for which
\Sigma_{N}
is a large disk of radius comparable to
\sqrt{N}
and
\mu_{N}
is the uniform measure on
\Sigma_{N}
.

We choose to work (a) in a disk (b) with a perfect confinement for the sake of convenience and simplicity of exposition, but one can certainly replace
\Sigma_{N}
by a square or by any “reasonable shape” without affecting the conclusions of Theorem
Theorem
as our argument makes no use of the global geometry of the system. Our results are also valid without a perfect confinement: adding an effective confining potential
\zeta_{N}
to
\mathsf{F}_{N}
as in (
A.2
) is transparent as our constructions always take place inside
\Sigma_{N}
, so Theorem
Theorem
holds for the two-dimensional
\beta
-ensemble/Coulomb gas with quadratic potential. However, considering a non-uniform density (which might happen if
\mathrm{V}
is not quadratic) would require some care.

Appendix B
Auxiliary proofs of preliminary results

B.1
Proof of Proposition
2.4

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

The first item corresponds to the first statement in
[
AS21
, Theorem 1]
, combined with their Lemma B.2. To be precise, the statement of
[
AS21
, Theorem 1]
involves the quantity
\mathsf{F}^{\square_{R}(x)}
which is short for
\mathsf{F}^{\square_{R}(x)}(\mathrm{X}_{N},U)
with
U=\mathbb{R}^{2}
as defined in
[
AS21
, Eq. (2.24)]
. The potential
u
appearing there is defined in
[
AS21
, Section 2.3]
but as used in the main statement of
[
AS21
]
it coincides with the true potential (because, with their notation, “
U=\mathbb{R}^{2}
” in this case). It remains to observe that Theorem 1 in
[
AS21
]
states a control on
\mathsf{F}^{\square_{R}(x)}
which can be turned into a control on the electric energy only (as we write in Proposition
2.4
), this is precisely the purpose of
[
AS21
, Lemma B.2]
and in particular their equation (B.8), which shows that one can indeed control the electric energy in terms of
\mathsf{F}^{\square_{R}(x)}
. As a last technical comment for the careful reader: note that since here “
U=\mathbb{R}^{2}
” (their notation) the various truncations
\mathsf{r},\tilde{\mathsf{r}},\tilde{\tilde{\mathsf{r}}}
all coincide.

The second item of Proposition
2.4
is
[
AS21
, (1.18)]
. This, or
[
AS21
, (1.19)]
, implies (
2.10
).
∎

B.2
Proof of Lemma
2.6

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

For
x
in
\mathbf{X}
let us define the truncation
\eta(x)
as:

\eta(x)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }x\notin\Omega\\
\mathsf{r}(x)&\text{if }x\in\Omega,\end{cases}

where
\mathsf{r}
is the “nearest-neighbor” distance as in (
2.3
). Let us recall that, by definition, we always have
|\mathsf{r}|\leq 1/4
. Let
\mathrm{E}
be an electric field compatible with
\mathbf{X}
on
\supp\varphi
and let
\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}
be the electric field truncated accordingly. We have, in the sense of distributions on
\supp\nabla\varphi
:

-\dive\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}=2\pi\left(\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}}\delta^{(\eta(x))}_{x}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right),

where we replace the Dirac mass
\delta_{x}
by its “smeared out” version
\delta^{(\eta(x))}_{x}
, which is the uniform measure of mass
1
on the circle of center
x
and radius
\eta(x)
. Let us write
\mathbf{X}_{\vec{\eta}}:=\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}}\delta^{(\eta(x))}_{x}
. The measures
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{X}_{\vec{\eta}}
coincide outside a
1
-neighborhood of
\supp\nabla\varphi
and the atoms there have been smeared out at a distance at most
1
, thus:

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{X}_{\vec{\eta}}\right)(x)\right|\leq|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\Omega}\times\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X},\Omega),

which can be localized, if
\tilde{\Omega}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\Omega}_{m}
cover
\supp\nabla\varphi
we have:

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{X}_{\vec{\eta}}\right)(x)\right|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sup_{x\in\Omega_{i}}|\nabla\varphi(x)|\times\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X},\Omega_{i}),

where
\Omega_{i}
contains a
1
-neighborhood of
\tilde{\Omega}_{i}
. Now, let us re-write the fluctuations of
\varphi
as:

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right)(x)\right|\leq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\vec{\eta}}(x)-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right)(x)\right|+|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\Omega}\times\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X},\Omega)\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(x)\dive\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}\right|+|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1},\Omega}\times\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X},\Omega).

Integrating by parts and using Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality yields:

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(x)\dive\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}\right|=\left|\int_{\supp\nabla\varphi}\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}\right|\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\supp\nabla\varphi}|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Finally, it remains to observe that with our choice of truncation
\eta
as above and the definition of
\Omega
, we have:

\int_{\supp\nabla\varphi}|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}\leq\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\mathsf{r}}}|^{2},

where
\vec{\mathsf{r}}
is the nearest-neighbor truncation, which concludes the proof of (
2.12
). This last step can also be localized by decomposing
\supp\nabla\varphi
into several domains.
∎

B.3
Proof of Proposition
2.8

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

We start by rewriting the Laplace transform of the fluctuations as a ratio of partition functions, this is a standard trick that goes back (at least) to
[
Joh98
]
. In the two-dimensional context, it can be found e.g. in
[
LS18
, Proposition 2.10]
. Let
\mathbf{m}_{s}
be the signed measure defined by:

\mathbf{m}_{s}:=\mathbf{m}_{0}-\frac{s}{2\pi\beta}\Delta\varphi,

(B.1)

which coincides with
\mathbf{m}_{0}
outside of
\mathcal{A}
(let us recall that
\mathcal{A}
is some annulus containing
\supp\Delta\varphi
). Since
\nabla\varphi
is compactly supported, the total mass of
\Delta\varphi
(seen as a measure) is
0
so
\mathbf{m}_{s}
and
\mathbf{m}_{0}
have the same total mass on
\mathcal{A}
. Moreover as soon as the parameter
s
satisfies the condition (
2.14
) (
|s|\leq\frac{\pi\beta}{4|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}}
) then
\mathbf{m}_{s}
has a non-negative density which is bounded between
\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{3}{2}
on
\mathcal{A}
. We introduce the following notation:

\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathbf{m}_{s}):=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N}-\mathbf{m}_{s}\right)(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N}-\mathbf{m}_{s}\right)(y).

Claim B.1

(Laplace transform as ratio of partition functions)
.

The following identity holds:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(s\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]\right)\right]=\exp\left(\frac{s^{2}}{4\pi\beta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\right)\frac{\int_{\Sigma_{N}^{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathbf{m}_{s})\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}}{\int_{\Sigma_{N}^{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathbf{m}_{0})\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}}

(B.2)

Proof of Claim
B.1
.

We have by definition:

\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(s\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]\right)\right]=\frac{\int_{\Sigma_{N}^{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathbf{m}_{0})-\frac{s}{\beta}\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi])\right)\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}}{\int_{\Sigma_{N}^{N}}\exp\left(-\beta\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathbf{m}_{0})\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{N}}.

Since
\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta\log=\delta_{0}
(see (
2.2
)) one may write
-\frac{s}{\beta}\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]
as:

-\frac{s}{\beta}\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]:=-\frac{s}{\beta}\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\varphi(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N}-\mathbf{m}_{0}\right)(x)=\iint-\log|x-y|\left(\frac{s}{2\pi\beta}\Delta\varphi(y)\right)\mathtt{d}y\mathtt{d}(\mathbf{X}_{N}-\mathbf{m}_{0})(x),

and then “complete the square”.
∎

In order to compare the partition functions in (
B.2
), it is common to introduce some sort of transportation map from
\mathbf{m}_{0}
to
\mathbf{m}_{s}
. The fact that the density of
\mathbf{m}_{0}
is constant and the one of
\mathbf{m}_{s}
has radial symmetry reduces the computation to a one-dimensional problem which can be solved exactly and explicitely (the general, non-radial case requires an abstract argument or resorting to an approximate transportation, which makes the analysis more involved). Proposition
2.8
is then a consequence of
[
Ser20
, Prop. 4.2]
.

Claim B.2

(Radial monotone rearrangement)
.

For convenience let us assume that
\varphi
is radially symmetric around
0
, and let
r_{\mathrm{max}}
be the outer radius of the annulus
\mathcal{A}
. Let
F_{s}:[0,r_{\mathrm{max}}]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the cumulative radial distribution function of the density
\mathbf{m}_{s}
, namely:

F_{s}:=r\mapsto\int_{0}^{r}\left(1-\frac{s}{2\pi\beta}\Delta\varphi(\rho)\right)2\pi\rho\mathtt{d}\rho=\pi r^{2}-\frac{s}{\beta}\int_{0}^{r}\Delta\varphi(\rho)\rho\mathtt{d}\rho,

where we denote by
\Delta\varphi(\rho)
the value of
\Delta\varphi(x)
at any point
x
with
|x|=\rho
. Let
\Phi_{s}
be the transport map defined by:

\Phi_{s}(r):=F_{s}^{-1}(\pi r^{2})\ (r\geq 0).

(B.3)

Finally, let
\vec{\Phi}_{s}
be the map
\vec{\Phi}_{s}(x):=\Phi_{s}(|x|)\frac{x}{|x|}
. Then:

1.

\vec{\Phi}_{s}
is a
C^{1}
-automorphism of the disk
\mathfrak{D}(0,r_{\mathrm{max}})
, which transports
\mathbf{m}_{0}
onto
\mathbf{m}_{s}
.

2.

The map
\psi_{s}:=x\mapsto\vec{\Phi}_{s}(x)-x
is supported on the annulus
\mathcal{A}
and satisfies:

|\psi_{s}|_{\mathsf{1}}\preceq s|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}.

(B.4)

Proof of Claim
B.2
.

Since
s
satisfies (
2.14
),
F_{s}
is strictly increasing and continuous, thus
\Phi_{s}=F_{s}^{-1}
is well-defined (it is the so-called “monotone rearrangement”). Since
\mathbf{m}_{0},\mathbf{m}_{s}
are radially symmetric the map
\vec{\Phi}_{s}
transports
\mathbf{m}_{0}
onto
\mathbf{m}_{s}
(as two-dimensional measures), and we have
\vec{\Phi}_{s}(x)=x
for
x\notin\mathcal{A}
.

A computation in polar coordinates shows that
|\psi_{s}|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq|\Phi_{s}^{\prime}-1|_{\mathsf{0}}
, on the other hand the derivatives of
\Phi_{s}
can be estimated in terms of the perturbation measure
\Delta\varphi
by using the transportation identity (
B.3
), which reads:

\Phi_{s}(r)^{2}-r^{2}=\frac{s}{\pi\beta}\int_{0}^{\Phi_{s}(r)}\Delta\varphi(\rho)\rho\mathtt{d}\rho,

and taking derivatives. In particular one finds (after an integration by parts) that
|\Phi_{s}(r)-r|\preceq s|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}r
, and then that:
|\Phi_{s}^{\prime}(r)-1|\preceq s|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}
.
∎

Next let us extend the notation
\vec{\Phi}_{s}
to
\vec{\Phi}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{N}):=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\vec{\Phi}_{s}(x_{i})}
.

Claim B.3

(Effect of the transportation on the energy)
.

We have:

\left|\mathsf{F}_{N}(\vec{\Phi}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{N}),\mathbf{m}_{s})-\mathsf{F}_{N}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathbf{m}_{0})\right|\preceq s|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}).

Proof of Claim
B.3
.

We apply
[
Ser20
, Prop 4.2]
with (in the notation of that paper)
U_{\ell}=\mathcal{A}
, which contains the support of
\psi_{s}
and thus of its derivative. Then
[
Ser20
, (4.5)]
states that the electric energy in
\mathcal{A}
stays bounded along the transport by
\vec{\Phi}_{s}
, and
[
Ser20
, (4.6)]
that in fact its derivative is bounded by
|\psi_{s}|_{\mathsf{1}}
times the initial electric energy plus the number of points in
\mathcal{A}
.
∎

To conclude, we change variables in (
B.2
) using
\vec{\Phi}_{s}
and use Claim
B.3
to estimate the effect on the energy. The Jacobian term that appears is again of order
s|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}
times the number of points in the support of
\psi_{s}
and can be incorporated in the previous error term.
∎

B.4
Cornering the discrepancy: proof of Proposition
3.1

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Let
\varphi
be a nonnegative, non increasing, compactly supported
\mathrm{C}^{2}
test function with radial symmetry around
z
such that:

1.

\varphi\equiv 1
on
\mathfrak{D}_{R-2L}
,
\varphi\equiv 0
outside
\mathfrak{D}_{R-L}
,
\varphi
takes values in
[0,1]
.

2.

|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq\frac{100}{L},\quad|\varphi|_{\mathsf{2}}\leq\frac{100}{L^{2}}
.

Let
\widetilde{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be the function defined by
\widetilde{\varphi}(x):=\varphi(|x-z|)
. Assume that:

\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\bigcap_{R-2L\leq r\leq R-L}\left\{\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}(z,r))\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R\right\}

(B.5)

Claim B.4

(
\widetilde{\varphi}
detects the discrepancy)
.

Under (
B.5
) we have:

\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{2}.

(B.6)

Proof of Claim
B.4
.

Without loss of generality we may assume that
z=0
. We take advantage of the radial symmetry of
\widetilde{\varphi}
and re-write
\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]
as:

\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]=\int_{\mathfrak{D}_{R-L}}\widetilde{\varphi}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(x)=\int_{0}^{R-L}\varphi(r)\left(\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}r}\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r})\right)\mathtt{d}r.

Integrating by parts, we get:
\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]=-\int_{R-2L}^{R-L}\varphi^{\prime}(r)\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r})\mathtt{d}r.
By assumption (
B.5
) we have
\mathsf{Dis}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}_{r})\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{2}
for all
r
in the domain of integration, moreover by construction
-\varphi^{\prime}\geq 0
and its integral is
1
. We thus obtain (
B.6
).
∎

We have constructed a radially symmetric test function
\widetilde{\varphi}
that is of class
C^{2}
and detects a fraction of the discrepancy. Let us compare (
B.6
) with the control on the size of
\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]
given by Proposition
2.8
.

Claim B.5

(Fluctuations of
\widetilde{\varphi}
)
.

With
s
as in (
3.1
)

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[e^{s\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]}\right]\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}(s^{2}+s)\frac{R}{L}.

(B.7)

Proof of Claim
B.5
.

By construction,
\widetilde{\varphi}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{2}
test function with compact support and radial symmetry, and
|\widetilde{\varphi}|_{\mathsf{2}}\leq\frac{100}{L^{2}}
. We assumed
L\leq\frac{R}{10}
and
|s|\leq\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}}\frac{L^{3}}{R}
(see condition (
3.1
)), hence up to choosing the constant
\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}
large enough (depending on
\beta
) we can ensure that
s
satisfies
|s|\leq\frac{\pi\beta}{4|\widetilde{\varphi}|_{\mathsf{2}}}
. Moreover the support of
\Delta\widetilde{\varphi}
is an annulus. In particular we may apply Proposition
2.8
and control the exponential moment of
\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]
by:

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(s\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]\right)\right]=\frac{s^{2}}{4\pi\beta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\widetilde{\varphi}|^{2}+\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(s|\widetilde{\varphi}|_{\mathsf{2}}\mathcal{O}\left(\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\mathsf{EnerPts}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{R-L}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{R-2L}\right)\right)\right)\right].

(B.8)

The quantity
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\widetilde{\varphi}|^{2}
is readily bounded (up to some multiplicative constant) by
RL
(the area of the annulus where
\Delta\widetilde{\varphi}
is supported) times
\frac{1}{L^{2}}
(the order of magnitude of
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{1}}^{2}
), and thus:

\frac{s^{2}}{4\pi\beta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\widetilde{\varphi}|^{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{s^{2}R}{L}\right).

(B.9)

It remains to estimate the contribution of the following term:

\mathrm{Rem}:=\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(s|\widetilde{\varphi}|_{\mathsf{2}}\mathcal{O}\left(\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\mathsf{EnerPts}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{R-L}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{R-2L}\right)\right)\right)\right].

By construction we know that
|\widetilde{\varphi}|_{2}
is of order
\frac{1}{L^{2}}
. Cover the annulus
\mathfrak{D}_{R-L}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{R-2L}
by a family
\{\square_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\#I=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R}{L}\right)
squares of sidelength
\mathcal{O}\left(L\right)
and write:

\exp\left(s|\widetilde{\varphi}|_{\mathsf{2}}\mathcal{O}\left(\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\mathsf{EnerPts}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{R-L}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{R-2L}\right)\right)\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}sR}{L^{3}}\frac{1}{\#I}\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{EnerPts}(\square_{i})\right).

By convexity we get:

\mathrm{Rem}\leq\frac{1}{\#I}\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}sR}{L^{3}}\mathsf{EnerPts}(\square_{i})\right)\right].

(B.10)

Up to chosing
\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}
large enough we can ensure that the parameter
\frac{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}sR}{L^{3}}
in the right-hand side of (
B.10
) is smaller than any fixed constant. Then for each
i\in I
the local laws (Proposition
2.4
) yield:

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}sR}{L^{3}}\left(\mathsf{EnerPts}(\square_{i})\right)\right)\right]\leq\frac{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}sR}{L^{3}}\times\hyperref@@ii[prop:local_laws]{\mathtt{C}_{\mathtt{LL}}}L^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{\beta}\left(\frac{sR}{L}\right).

Taking an average over
i\in I
yields:

\log\mathrm{Rem}=\mathcal{O}_{\beta}\left(\frac{sR}{L}\right),

(B.11)

and combining it with (
B.8
), (
B.9
) and (
B.11
), we obtain (
B.7
) as claimed.
∎

Next, applying Markov’s inequality in exponential form to (
B.7
), we get:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathrm{Fluct}[\widetilde{\varphi}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{2}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{s\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{2}+(s^{2}+s)\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\left(\frac{R}{L}\right)\right).

Since we assume
L\geq\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{R}}
and
s\leq\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}}L\varepsilon_{R}
, up to choosing
\mathtt{C}_{\ref{prop:chase_the_goat}}
large enough we can ensure that:

-\frac{s\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{2}+(s^{2}+s)\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\left(\frac{R}{L}\right)\leq-\frac{s\varepsilon_{R}\cdot R}{4},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Study of the external potential: proof of Proposition
5.3

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Let us fix an index
i\in\{1,\dots,\mathcal{N}\}
and study the external potential
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
(as defined in (
4.1
)) on
\Lambda_{i}
. Let
\mathrm{V}_{i}
be the potential generated on
\Lambda_{i}
by “everything outside
\Lambda_{i}
”, namely:

\mathrm{V}_{i}(x):=\int_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{i}}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y),\ x\in\Lambda_{i}.

(C.1)

For all
x
in
\Lambda_{i}
we have, by definition:

\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\mathrm{V}_{i}(x)-\sum_{j,j\neq i}\int_{\Lambda_{j}}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y).

(C.2)

For convenience, we will first study
\mathrm{V}_{i}
itself and then use a rough bound on the remaining terms in (
C.2
). In the rest of this section we choose an auxiliary length scale
\hat{T}
as:

\hat{T}:=\log T.

C.1
Decomposition and regularization

Decomposition of
\mathrm{V}_{i}

Let us introduce a smooth cut-off function
\chi_{i}
equal to
1
on
\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+\hat{T})
and to
0
outside
\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+2\hat{T})
, with
|\chi_{i}|_{\mathsf{k}}\leq\mathtt{C}\hat{T}^{-\mathsf{k}}
and let:

•

R^{\prime}_{i}
be the field generated by the background measure in the annulus
\mathcal{A}:=\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+2\hat{T})\setminus\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T)
, weighted by
\chi_{i}
, namely:

R^{\prime}_{i}:=-\log\ast\left(-\chi_{i}\mathtt{1}_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{i}}\mathbf{m}_{0}\right).

•

\mathfrak{h}^{\nu_{i}}
be the field generated by the positive measure
\nu_{i}
corresponding to the point charges in
\mathcal{A}
, weighted by
\chi_{i}
, namely:

\mathfrak{h}^{\nu_{i}}:=-\log\ast\left(\chi_{i}\mathtt{1}_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{i}}\mathbf{X}_{N}\right).

•

R_{i}
be the field generated by the signed measure
(1-\chi_{i})\mathbf{f}_{N}
, namely:

R_{i}:=-\log\ast\left((1-\chi_{i})\mathbf{f}_{N}\right).

(C.3)

Of course, we have
\mathfrak{h}^{\nu_{i}}+R^{\prime}_{i}=-\log\ast\left(\chi_{i}\mathbf{f}_{N}\right)
and thus the following decomposition holds (cf. (
5.3
)):

\mathrm{V}_{i}=\mathfrak{h}^{\nu_{i}}+R^{\prime}_{i}+R_{i}.

(C.4)

Remark C.1
.

By Newton’s theorem, the logarithmic potential
R^{\prime}_{i}
is
constant
within the disk
\Lambda_{i}
thanks to its radial symmetry. So its derivative and its “interior” normal derivative
\partial_{n}^{-}R^{\prime}_{i}
(see (
D.7
)) are identically
0
there, and it will play no further role in the proof.

Regularization of
\mathrm{V}_{i}
near the edge.

Since there might be charges in
\nu_{i}
located very close to
\partial\Lambda_{i}
, we cannot expect in general to have an upper bound on
\mathrm{V}_{i}
in
\Lambda_{i}
valid
up to the edge
. In order to study
\mathrm{V}_{i}
near
\partial\Lambda_{i}
, we introduce a smooth cut-off function
\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,1]
such that:

\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}(z)=0\text{ if }|z|\leq\frac{1}{4},\quad\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}(z)=1\text{ if }|z|\geq 1,\quad|\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq 10,

and we use
\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}
to define a
regularized
version of
\mathrm{V}_{i}
as follows:

\widetilde{\mathrm{V}}_{i}(x):=\int_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{i}}-\log|x-y|\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}(x-y)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y),\ x\in\Lambda_{i}.

(C.5)

Claim C.2
.

We have:

•

\widetilde{\mathrm{V}}_{i}(x)\leq\mathrm{V}_{i}(x)+100
for all
x\in\Lambda_{i}
.

•

\mathrm{V}_{i}(x)=\widetilde{\mathrm{V}}_{i}(x)
for all
x\in\Lambda_{i}
with
\dist(x,\partial\Lambda_{i})\geq 1
.

Proof of Claim
C.2
.

For the first point, observe that adding the short-distance cut-off
\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}
diminishes the (non-negative) influence of point particles at distance less than
1
, as well as the influence of the background in a small disk (which is negative but uniformly bounded). The second point is obvious.
∎

We introduce the (regularized) “boundary” term
B_{i}
:

B_{i}:x\mapsto B_{i}(x):=\int_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{i}}\chi_{i}(y)\times\log|x-y|\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}(x-y)\ \mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y).

(C.6)

We can decompose
\widetilde{\mathrm{V}}_{i}
as (compare with (
C.4
)):

\widetilde{\mathrm{V}}_{i}=B_{i}+R_{i},

(C.7)

with
R_{i}
as above in (
C.3
) (observe that for
x\in\Lambda_{i}
and
y
in the support of
1-\chi_{i}
we have
\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}(x-y)=1
i.e. the regularization by
\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}
is transparent and does not appear in
R_{i}
).

C.2
Study of the boundary term

Claim C.3
.

We have, with
B_{i}
as in (
C.6
):

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\sup_{x\in\Lambda_{i}}|\nabla B_{i}(x)|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\log^{2}T\ \right]\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\log^{2}T\right).

(C.8)

Proof of Claim
C.3
.

For
x\in\Lambda_{i}
arbitrary and
y
in the support of
\chi_{i}
we have:

\|\nabla\left(\log|x-y|\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}(x-y)\right)\|\preceq\frac{1}{1+|x-y|}

(this is possible thanks to the regularization due to
\chi_{\mathrm{uv}}
near
\partial\Lambda_{i}
). To control
|\nabla B_{i}(x)|
it is thus enough to bound, for
x\in\Lambda_{i}
:

\int\mathtt{1}_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{i}}(y)\chi_{i}(y)\frac{1}{1+|x-y|}\left(\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{N}(y)+\mathtt{d}y\right).

(C.9)

By construction
\chi_{i}\mathtt{1}_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{i}}
is supported in the annulus
\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+2\hat{T})\setminus\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T)
. Let us cover this annulus by
\mathcal{O}(\frac{T}{\hat{T}})
squares of sidelength
\hat{T}
. In each square, by the local laws, we can ensure that there are at most
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\hat{T}^{2}
points with probability
1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\hat{T}^{2}\right)
and so after an union bound we can ensure that
all of them
contain at most
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\hat{T}^{2}
points with probability
1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\hat{T}^{2}\right)
(
\hat{T}^{2}
has been chosen in order to beat the combinatorial loss due to such union bounds). If so, then we have:

\int\mathtt{1}_{\Sigma_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{i}}(y)\chi_{i}(y)\frac{1}{1+|x-y|}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{N}(y)\preceq\hat{T}\int_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{i}}\frac{1}{1+|x-y|}\mathtt{d}y,

for which a rough bound is
\mathcal{O}\left(\log^{2}T\right)
. The continuous part of (
C.9
) is of the same order.
∎

We now focus on studying
R_{i}
. In the next sections the constants
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}
will also depend on
\delta
as in Assumption (
1.5
).

C.3
Estimate on the first derivative at the center

Lemma C.4
.

We have, with
R_{i}
as in (
C.3
):

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left[|\nabla R_{i}(\omega_{i})|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\right]\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}T}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right).

(C.10)

Proof of Lemma
C.4
.

Let us bound the first partial derivative of
R_{i}
. By definition of
R_{i}
we have:

\partial_{1}R_{i}(x)=-\int_{\Sigma_{N}}(1-\chi_{i}(y))\partial_{1}\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y).

1. The first outer layer.

Let
\sigma
be a cut-off function with radial symmetry around
\omega_{i}
such that:

\sigma(z)=1\text{ if }|z-\omega_{i}|\leq 2T,\quad\sigma(z)=0\text{ if }|z-\omega_{i}|\geq 3T,\quad|\sigma|_{\mathsf{k}}\leq\mathtt{C}T^{-\mathsf{k}}\text{ for $\mathsf{k}\in\{1,2\}$}.

We start by evaluating the contribution to
R_{i}
coming from the support of
\sigma
.

Claim C.5
.

Let
A:=\int(1-\chi_{i}(y))\sigma(y)\partial_{1}\log|\omega_{i}-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y)
. We have:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(|A|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\hat{T}^{2}\right).

(C.11)

Proof of Claim
C.5
.

We are looking at the fluctuations of
\varphi:=y\mapsto\left(1-\chi_{i}(y)\right)\sigma(y)\partial_{1}\log|\omega_{i}-y|
which is of class
\mathrm{C}^{1}
and compactly supported within
\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},3T)\setminus\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+\hat{T})
. Let us recall that:

•

(1-\chi_{i}),\sigma
are bounded and
\partial_{1}\log|\omega_{i}-y|
is of order
T^{-1}
for
y
in the support of
\varphi
.

•

|\chi_{i}|_{\mathsf{1}}
is of order
\hat{T}^{-1}
,
|\sigma|_{\mathsf{1}}
is of order
T^{-1}
and
\left|\partial_{1}\log|\omega_{i}-\cdot|\right|_{\mathsf{1},\supp\varphi}
is of order
T^{-2}.

Moreover let us make the following observations:

•

On the annulus
\mathcal{A}_{1}:=\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+2\hat{T})\setminus\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+\hat{T})
, of area
\mathcal{O}(T\hat{T})
,
\|\nabla\varphi\|
is of order
(T\hat{T})^{-1}
(the dominant contribution comes from differentiating
\chi_{i}
).

•

On the annulus
\mathcal{A}_{2}:=\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},3T)\setminus\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+2\hat{T})
, of area
\mathcal{O}(T^{2})
,
\|\nabla\varphi\|
is of order
T^{-2}
(
\chi_{i}
does not play a role anymore).

Using Lemma
2.6
in its localized version (
2.13
) we are left to bound:

A_{1}:=\left(T\hat{T}\right)^{-1}\times\left(\mathrm{Ener}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(T\hat{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathsf{Pts}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}_{1}\right)\right)

and
