A_{2}:=T^{-2}\times\left(\mathrm{Ener}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\times(T^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathsf{Pts}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}_{2}\right)\right).

To control the first term
A_{1}
, we cover the annulus
\mathcal{A}_{1}
by
\mathcal{O}(\frac{T}{\hat{T}})
squares of sidelength
\hat{T}
and use the local laws. We obtain:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}_{1})\geq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}T\hat{T}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\hat{T}^{2}\right),

where we used a union bound on
\exp\left(\mathcal{O}(\log T)\right)
events and the fact that
\log T\ll\hat{T}^{2}
. In particular this ensures that:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(|A_{1}|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\hat{T}^{2}\right).

On the other hand, to control the second term
A_{2}
we may apply the local laws to the full disk
\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},3T)
and see that:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(\mathsf{EnerPts}\left(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},3T)\right)\geq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}T^{2}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{T^{2}}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right),

which ensures that
\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left(|A_{2}|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{T^{2}}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right)
. We deduce (
C.11
).
∎

2. Dyadic scales up to the boundary.

Let
K:=\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{10}\dist\left(\Lambda_{i},\partial\Sigma_{N}\right)\right)
. By our assumption (
1.5
) we have
K\geq\log_{2}\left(\frac{\delta\sqrt{N}}{10}\right)
.

For
\log_{2}(2T)\leq k\leq K
we let
\tau_{k}
be a smooth cut-off function such that:

•

\tau_{k}
has radial symmetry around
\omega_{i}
and is supported on the annulus
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k}:=\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},2^{k+1})\setminus\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},2^{k})
of area
\mathcal{O}(2^{2k})
.

•

We have
|\tau_{k}|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq\mathtt{C}2^{-k}
and
|\tau_{k}|_{\mathsf{2}}\leq\mathtt{C}2^{-2k}
(with
\mathtt{C}
independent of
k
).

•

We have the following “partition of unity”-type of identity:

\sigma(z)+\sum_{k=\log_{2}(2T)}^{K}\tau_{k}(z)\equiv 1\text{ for }|z-\omega_{i}|\leq 2^{K-1}.

(C.12)

For each
k
we let
\phi_{k}
be the following map:

\phi_{k}:y\mapsto-\tau_{k}(y)\partial_{1}\log|\omega_{i}-y|.

One can check that:

|\phi_{k}|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq\mathtt{C}2^{-2k},\quad|\phi_{k}|_{\mathsf{2}}\leq\mathtt{C}2^{-3k}.

Claim C.6
.

We have, for
k=\log_{2}(2T),\dots,K
:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\left|\mathrm{Fluct}[\phi_{k}]\right|\geq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}2^{-k/2}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}2^{k/2}\right).

(C.13)

Proof of Claim
C.6
.

We use the “fine bounds” on fluctuations given by Proposition
2.8
. We choose the parameter
s=c2^{k}
for some constant
c
, as allowed by (
2.14
), and observe that, in view of the bounds mentioned above:

s^{2}\int_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k}}|\nabla\phi_{k}|^{2}\preceq s^{2}2^{2k}\times\left(2^{-2k}\right)^{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(c^{2}\right).

Moreover we may use the local laws to write (note that we deliberately stopped before reaching the boundary thanks to our choice of
K
):

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left(e^{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}s|\phi_{k}|_{\mathsf{2}}\left(\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k})\right)}\right)\leq\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left(e^{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}c2^{-2k}\left(\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k})\right)}\right)\leq c\mathtt{C}_{\beta}.

In summary, we have:

\log\mathbb{E}^{\beta}_{N}\left[e^{c2^{k}\mathrm{Fluct}[\phi_{k}]}\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(c^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}_{\beta}\left(c\right),

and Markov’s inequality yields the claim.
∎

Summing up the controls on
\phi_{k}
and using a union bound, we obtain:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\left|\sum_{k=\log_{2}(2T)}^{K}\mathrm{Fluct}[\phi_{k}]\right|\geq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}T^{-1/2}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}T^{1/2}\right)\ll\exp\left(-\log^{2}T\right).

(C.14)

∎

3. The rest of the system.
It remains to study the contribution to
\nabla R_{i}
coming from the part of the system far from
\omega_{i}
. Let us introduce an artifical cut-off
\gamma
supported on
2\Sigma_{N}
. We have

\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\left(1-\left(\sigma(y)+\sum_{k=\log_{2}(2T)}^{K}\tau_{k}(y)\right)\right)\partial_{1}\log|y-\omega_{i}|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y),\\
=\int\gamma(y)\left(1-\left(\sigma(y)+\sum_{k=\log_{2}(2T)}^{K}\tau_{k}(y)\right)\right)\partial_{1}\log|y-\omega_{i}|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{f}_{N}(y)

and we may thus apply Lemma
2.6
to the function:

\varphi:y\mapsto\gamma(y)\left(1-\left(\sigma(y)+\sum_{k=\log_{2}(2T)}^{K}\tau_{k}(y)\right)\right)\partial_{1}\log|y-\omega_{i}|,

whose derivative is of order at most
\frac{1}{\delta^{2}N}
in view of Assumption
1.5
(this is where the dependency in
\delta
comes in). The support of
\varphi
has area
N
, the number of points is obviously bounded by
N
and the energy there is
\mathcal{O}(N)
with high probability by the
global
law. We obtain:

|\mathrm{Fluct}[\varphi]|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}},\text{ with probability }1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}N\right).

Keeping the dominant contributions of these three steps, we obtain (
C.10
).
∎

C.4
Estimate on the second derivative up to the boundary.

Lemma C.7
.

We have, with
R_{i}
as in (
C.3
):

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\sup_{x\in\Lambda_{i}}|\mathsf{D}^{2}R_{i}(x)|\times\left(1+\dist(x,\partial\Lambda_{i})\right)\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\right]\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\hat{T}^{2}\right).

(C.15)

Proof of Lemma
C.7
.

We bound one of the four second partial derivatives (they can all be treated the same way) and use the same decomposition as in the proof of Lemma
C.4
.

1. The first outer layer.
To control the first outer layer, we proceed like for Claim
C.3
. For
x\in\Lambda_{i}
arbitrary and
y
in the support of
(1-\chi_{i})\sigma
we have:

\|\mathsf{D}^{2}\log|x-y|\|\preceq\frac{1}{\left(\hat{T}+|x-y|\right)^{2}}

(the points
x,y
being at distance at least
\hat{T}
from each other). It is thus enough to bound:

\int\sigma(y)\left(1-\chi_{i}(y)\right)\frac{1}{\left(\hat{T}+|x-y|\right)^{2}}\left(\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{N}(y)+\mathtt{d}y\right).

(C.16)

Assume as above that each square of sidelength
\hat{T}
covering the annulus
\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T+2\hat{T})\setminus\mathfrak{D}(\omega_{i},T)
contains at most
\mathtt{C}\hat{T}^{2}
points (an event that occurs with probability
1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}}\hat{T}^{2}\right)
and does not depend on
x
). We may then compare (
C.16
) to the following one-dimensional integral:

\hat{T}\int_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{i}}\frac{1}{\left(\hat{T}+|x-y|\right)^{2}}\mathtt{d}y,

for which a rough bound is
\mathtt{C}\frac{1}{1+\dist(x,\partial\Lambda_{i})}
.

2. Dyadic scales up to the boundary.
We proceed as in the previous proof, but instead of using the fine bounds of Proposition
2.8
(which are only valid function-wise, so
x
-wise here) we use the rougher controls of Lemma
2.6
which allow for a uniform control. For each
k
we let
\phi_{k}
be the following map:

\phi_{k,x}:y\mapsto-\tau_{k}(y)\partial_{12}\log|x-y|.

If
x
is in
\Lambda_{i}
and
y\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k}
for
k\geq\log_{2}(2T)
we have
|\phi_{k,x}|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq\mathtt{C}2^{-3k}
. Hence using (
2.12
) we know that we can bound
\mathrm{Fluct}[\phi_{k,x}]
by:

2^{-3k}\times\left(2^{2k}+\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k})\right).

Using the local laws, we may control
\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k})
by
\mathtt{C}_{\beta}2^{2k}
with probability
1-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}2^{2k}\right)
. We deduce that:

\mathbb{P}^{\beta}_{N}\left[\sup_{x\in\Lambda_{i}}\left|\mathrm{Fluct}[\phi_{k,x}]\right|\geq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}2^{-k}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}2^{2k}\right).

Summing again the contributions through an union bound, we get that the contribution to the second derivative of
R_{i}
due to the dyadic annuli is bounded by
\mathtt{C}T^{-1}
with probability
1-\exp\left(-\frac{T^{2}}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right)
.

3. The rest of the system.
We argue as in the previous proof, but thanks to the additional derivative we gain a factor
\frac{1}{\delta\sqrt{N}}\ll\dist(x,\Lambda_{i})^{-1}
.
∎

C.5
Summary and conclusion

Bounding the contributions of other sub-systems

Returning to (
C.1
), (
C.2
), a rough bound on
\|\mathsf{D}\log|x-y|\|
for
x\in\Lambda_{i}
and
y\in\Lambda_{j}
, together with the fact that each sub-system contains by assumption
\mathcal{O}(T^{2})
points, ensures that (using (
3.6
)):

\left|\mathrm{V}_{i}-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}\right|_{\mathsf{1},\Lambda_{i}}\leq\mathtt{C}T^{2}\sum_{j,j\neq i}\frac{1}{\mathrm{d}_{ij}}=\mathcal{O}\left(T^{2}\frac{\log R}{ML}\right).

(C.17)

(Let us note that it is fairly easy to improve (
C.17
) by expanding the interaction in a more precise way and controlling some fluctuations, but it would add technicalities while not making a big difference in our final statement.)

Summary

1. Control up to the edge.

Combining Lemma
C.4
and Lemma
C.7
and integrating between
\omega_{i}
and any given
x
in
\Lambda_{i}
we deduce that
|\nabla R_{i}(x)|\leq\mathtt{C}\log T
for all
x
in
\Lambda_{i}
. Combining this with the decomposition (
C.7
) and Claim
C.3
we deduce that

\|\nabla\widetilde{\mathrm{V}}_{i}\|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}\log^{2}T\text{ on $\Lambda_{i}$...}

…and thus after integrating between
\omega_{i}
and any given
x
in
\Lambda_{i}
we obtain:

\left|\widetilde{\mathrm{V}}_{i}(x)-\widetilde{\mathrm{V}}_{i}(\omega_{i})\right|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\beta}T\log^{2}T

2. Properties of the decomposition.

We have already bounded
|R_{i}|_{\mathsf{1},\Lambda}
, and we have used the control of
\nu
at scale
\hat{T}:\log T
several times in the argument above.

In conclusion, with probability
1-\exp\left(-\frac{\hat{T}^{2}}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right)
the potential
\mathrm{V}_{i}
satisfies the requirements to be a “good external potential” on
\Lambda_{i}
. Thus in view of (
C.17
), since we enforce (
3.4
), with probability
1-\exp\left(-\frac{\hat{T}^{2}}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right)
the potential
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is a “good external potential with constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
”, where
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
is some large enough constant depending only on
\beta
and
\delta
.

We conclude with a union bound on
\mathcal{N}
such events.

Appendix D
Global laws for sub-systems: proof of Proposition
5.7

D.1
Effect of an harmonic perturbation on the equilibrium measure

In this section, we revisit (parts of) the analysis of
[
BBNY17
, Sec.3]
. Broadly speaking, our goal is to understand the effect of the external potential
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
on the typical repartition of charges within the subsystem. In particular, we want to use the fact that
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is
harmonic
on
\Lambda
and is generated by a “nice” external configuration. We start by recalling some elements of logarithmic potential theory.

D.1.1
Some potential theory

Let
\mathrm{V}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
be a lower semi-continuous function satisfying, for some
\varepsilon>0
, the following growth condition:

\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\left(\mathrm{V}(z)-(2+\varepsilon)\log|z|\right)=+\infty.

If
\mathrm{V}
takes the value
+\infty
, assume that it is not too wild e.g. that
\mathrm{V}
takes finite values on some open disk. Denote by
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{V}}
the following functional defined on the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{V}}:\mu\mapsto\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu(x)\mathtt{d}\mu(y)+\int\mathrm{V}\mathtt{d}\mu.

(D.1)

The next proposition
12
12

12

The statements of Proposition
D.1
only hold “quasi everywhere” (q.e.), which means “up to a set of capacity zero”. This makes no difference for us and, for simplicity, we omit it.

covers well-known properties of the minimization problem associated to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{V}}
.

Proposition D.1

(The equilibrium measure)
.

There exists a unique minimizer
\mu_{\mathrm{V}}
of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{V}}
, which we call the “equilibrium measure” associated to
\mathrm{V}
. Its support, denoted by
S_{\mathrm{V}}
, is compact.

The equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathrm{V}}
is characterized by the following fact: there exists a constant
c_{\mathrm{V}}
(depending on
\mathrm{V}
) such that the logarithmic potential generated by
\mu_{\mathrm{V}}
, namely
\mathfrak{h}^{\mu_{\mathrm{V}}}(x):=\int-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}}(y)
satisfies Euler-Lagrange equations of the form:

\begin{cases}\mathfrak{h}^{\mu_{\mathrm{V}}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{V}=c_{\mathrm{V}}&\text{ on }S_{\mathrm{V}}\\
\mathfrak{h}^{\mu_{\mathrm{V}}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{V}\geq c_{\mathrm{V}}&\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus S_{\mathrm{V}}.\end{cases}

(D.2)

Moreover, the equilibrium measure is connected to the solution of an
obstacle problem
in the following sense: if we define
u_{\mathrm{V}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
by setting:

u_{\mathrm{V}}(z):=\sup\left\{v(z),v\text{ is subharmonic},\ v\leq\frac{\mathrm{V}}{2}\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{2},\ \limsup_{|z|\to\infty}\left(v(z)-\log|z|\right)<\infty\right\},

(D.3)

and let the “coincidence set”
S^{*}_{\mathrm{V}}
be
S^{*}_{\mathrm{V}}:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2},u_{\mathrm{V}}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{V}(z)\right\}
. Then
S_{\mathrm{V}}\subset S^{*}_{\mathrm{V}}
(in words: the support of the equilibrium measure is contained in the coincidence set of the obstacle problem) and we have:

u_{\mathrm{V}}(z)=c_{\mathrm{V}}-\mathfrak{h}^{\mu_{\mathrm{V}}}(z)\text{ on $\mathbb{R}^{2}$}.

In particular the density of
\mu_{\mathrm{V}}
is given equivalently by
\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta u_{\mathrm{V}}
or
\frac{1}{4\pi}\Delta\mathrm{V}
on
S_{\mathrm{V}}
.

Proof of Proposition
D.1
.

The first part is a classical result by Frostman
[
Fro35
]
. The connection with the obstacle problem has been investigated in
[
HM13
]
and an exposition can be found in
[
Ser15
, Sec.2]
. See also
[
BBNY17
, Sec. 2.1, 2.2]
and the references therein.
∎

D.1.2
Harmonic perturbations

We now study how the equilibrium measure reacts to certain perturbations of the external potential. Here we closely follow the exposition of
[
BBNY17
, Sec. 3.3]
while making several changes.

The reference measure.

Let
\rho\geq 1
be some fixed radius, we work on the disk
\rho\mathfrak{D}:=\mathfrak{D}(0,\rho)
(for convenience we adopt here the notation of
[
BBNY17
]
). Let us assume that the reference potential
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}
is given by:

\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}(z)=\begin{cases}\frac{|z|^{2}}{\rho^{2}}&\text{ for }|z|\leq\rho,\\
+\infty&\text{ for }|z|>\rho.\end{cases}

(D.4)

It satisfies the assumptions of Section
D.1.1
, and it is easy to check that the associated equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}
is the uniform measure on the disk
\rho\mathfrak{D}
- in other words, we have
\mu_{V}=\frac{1}{|\rho\mathfrak{D}|}\mathbf{m}_{0}
on
\rho\mathfrak{D}
. Its density is
\frac{1}{\pi\rho^{2}}
, in particular the assumption that “
\frac{1}{4\pi}\Delta\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}\geq\alpha
in
\rho\mathfrak{D}
” made in
[
BBNY17
, Sec. 3.3]
is satisfied here but with
\alpha
proportional to
\rho^{-2}
.

Class of perturbations.

We work with the same class of potentials
\mathrm{W}
as in
[
BBNY17
]
, namely we take
\mathrm{W}
as:

\mathrm{W}:=\tau\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}+\rho^{-2}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\nu}+R\right),\text{ where:}

(D.5)

•

\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}
is the reference potential of (
D.4
) and
\tau
is a real parameter. For us
\tau
will always be close to
1
, and for convenience we can assume that
|\tau-1|\leq\frac{1}{10}
.

•

\mathfrak{h}^{\nu}
is the logarithmic potential generated by a
positive
, finite distribution
\nu
of charges located
outside
of the disk
\rho\mathfrak{D}
. For convenience (and since that is enough for us), we will assume that
\nu
is supported on an annulus
\mathfrak{D}(0,\rho+\rho^{\prime})\setminus\mathfrak{D}(0,\rho)
for some
\rho^{\prime}\leq\frac{\rho}{100}
. In
[
BBNY17
, Prop. 3.3, 3.4]
the controls are given in terms of the
total mass

\|\nu\|
of
\nu
. We want more precise estimates and introduce to that aim the following quantity:

|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime}):=\sup_{x\in\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}}\nu(\mathfrak{D}(x,\rho^{\prime})),

(D.6)

which controls the mass of
\nu
in a
local
fashion, at scale
\rho^{\prime}
along
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
.

•

R
is some harmonic function on
\rho\mathfrak{D}
which is continuous up to the boundary. An important role is played by the
normal derivative of
R
on
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
. We introduce the notation:

\partial_{n}^{-}R(z):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{R(z)-R(z-\varepsilon\vec{n})}{\varepsilon},\quad\|\partial_{n}^{-}R\|_{\infty,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}}:=\sup_{z\in\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}}|\partial_{n}^{-}R(z)|,

(D.7)

where for
z\in\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
we denote by
\vec{n}
the outer unit normal vector (to the circle) at
z
.

Our scaling
\rho^{-2}
in front of the perturbation terms in (
D.5
) is not present in
[
BBNY17
, (3.7)]
, but it is of course equivalent to taking the mass of
\nu
and the potential
R
to be of order
\rho^{-2}
in their statements. This scaling will compensate the fact that for us the quantity
\alpha
of
[
BBNY17
]
is of order
\rho^{-2}
(see above).

Effect of the perturbation.

Passing from
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}
to
\mathrm{W}
as in (
D.5
) has three effects on the equilibrium measure:

1.

The support loses some parts located near the circle
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
.

2.

A singular measure appears on the circle
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
.

3.

The continuous density in the new support changes from
\Delta\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}
to
\tau\Delta\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}
.

In other words, the measure
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
can be written as:

\mu_{\mathrm{W}}:=\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}+\left(\eta-\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}\mathtt{1}_{B}\right),

with
B
a subset of
\rho\mathfrak{D}
located near
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
and
\eta
a measure that is absolutely continuous with respect to the arclength measure
\mathtt{d}s
on
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
.

Of course the combination of all effects must have total mass
0
so that the resulting equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
still be a probability measure. The point of the following propositions is to give quantitative controls on the location of
B
(first item) and the density of
\eta
(second item). The techniques are heavily borrowed from
[
BBNY17
, Prop. 3.3 & 3.4]
, with some simplications due to our specific context, and precisions thanks to our use of
|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}
as in (
D.6
).

Proposition D.2

(The support of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
contains a large sub-disk)
.

The support of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
contains the set of points
z\in\rho\mathfrak{D}
at distance at least
\kappa
from
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
, with
\kappa
such that:

\kappa\geq\mathtt{C}\max\left(20\rho^{\prime},\ \frac{|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}},\ |1-\tau|\rho+\|\partial^{-}_{n}R\|_{\infty,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}}\right).

(D.8)

Proposition D.3

(The singular component has a controlled density)
.

The Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\eta
with respect to the
arclength
measure on
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
is bounded by:

\|\frac{\mathtt{d}\eta}{\mathtt{d}s}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathtt{C}\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\|\eta\|+\rho^{-2}\frac{|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}+\rho^{-2}\|\partial^{-}_{n}R\|_{\infty,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}}+|1-\tau|\rho^{-1}\right).

(D.9)

The next two subsections are devoted to the proofs of Propositions
D.2
and
D.3
. We follow the proofs of the corresponding results in
[
BBNY17
]
while emphasizing the required modifications.

D.1.3
Proof of Proposition
D.2

Importing an explicit construction.

The key ingredient for the proof of
[
BBNY17
, Prop 3.3]
is an explicit construction. To quote from
[
BBNY17
, Proof of Prop 3.3]
: “let
D:=\{z\in\rho\mathfrak{D},\dist(z,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D})\geq\kappa\}
, we show that
D
[is included in the support of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
] by exhibiting for every
z_{0}\in D
a function
v=v_{z_{0}}
that satisfies
v(z_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}(z_{0})
and:

\begin{cases}\text{$v$ is sub-harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^{2}$},\\
\text{$v\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}$ on $\mathbb{R}^{2}$},\\
\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\left(v(z)-\log|z|\right)<\infty.\end{cases}

(D.10)

Thus we have
u_{\mathrm{W}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}
in
D
(cf. (
D.3
)) and since the perturbation
\mathrm{W}-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}
is harmonic on
\rho\mathfrak{D}\supset D
we have
13
13

13

This follows from the last statement in Proposition
D.1
.
(as densities)
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}=\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}>0
on
D
and thus
D
is contained in the support of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
.” It remains to construct such a function
v
, for which a crucial tool is the following lemma from
[
BBNY17
]
. For any
r>0
, let
l_{r}
be the logarithmic potential generated by the uniform probability measure spread on
\mathfrak{D}(0,r)
.

Lemma D.4

(An explicit construction)
.

Let
z_{0},w
be two points in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
r
such that
r\leq\frac{1}{2}|z_{0}-w|
. Then there exists
\tilde{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
k\in\mathbb{R}
(both depending on
z_{0}
and
w
) such that:

\frac{1}{2}\left(l_{r}(z_{0}-\tilde{z})+k\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\log|z_{0}-w|,\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(l_{r}(z-\tilde{z})+k\right)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\log|z-w|\text{ for }z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(D.11)

Moreover the point
\tilde{z}
can be found on the line segment between
z_{0}
and
w
with:

|\tilde{z}-z_{0}|=\frac{r^{2}}{|z_{0}-w|}.

(D.12)

Proof of Lemma
D.4
.

This is
[
BBNY17
, Lemma 3.6]
with three minor differences. First of all, in their statement they assume that
r\leq\frac{1}{2}|z_{0}-w|

and
that
r\in(0,1)
(which is always the case in their setting), whereas we take
r
arbitrary (still with the condition
r\leq\frac{1}{2}|z_{0}-w|
). There is in fact no additional generality in our statement: the case
r\in(0,1)
extends to the general case by scaling. Secondly, they allow for any
\sigma\geq\frac{1}{2}
but we will only need
\sigma=\frac{1}{2}
(so “
\sigma
” does not appear here). Finally, the estimate (
D.12
) is not written down in
[
BBNY17
]
, however it is a straightforward consequence of the fact that
|\tilde{z}-z_{0}|\leq r\leq\frac{1}{2}|z_{0}-w|
(which is given by their statement and assumption) and the first equality in
[
BBNY17
, (3.14)]
(with
\sigma=\frac{1}{2}
).
∎

Next, we follow
[
BBNY17
, Proof of Prop 3.3]
with a slight adaptation to prove our Proposition
D.2
.

Proof of Proposition
D.2
.

Contrarily to
[
BBNY17
]
we will not scale everything back to the unit circle. We fix
z_{0}
in
D:=\{z\in\rho\mathfrak{D},\dist(z,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D})\geq\kappa\}
, with
\kappa
satisfying the three conditions of (
D.8
) and seek to construct
v
satisfying (
D.10
). Let
u_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}
be the solution to the obstacle problem for the reference potential
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}
as in (
D.3
), let
\tilde{R}
be the harmonic extension of
R
outside
\rho\mathfrak{D}
as in
[
BBNY17
, (3.17)]
, let
G(z)=\max(0,\log|\rho^{-1}z|)
. Let
\gamma:=2\|\partial_{n}^{-}R\|_{\infty,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}}
as defined in (
D.7
). Compared to
[
BBNY17
]
we always take
\sigma=\frac{1}{2}
(and
\sigma
does not appear here).

There remains to define one last term, for which we differ slightly from
[
BBNY17
, (3.18)]
as explained below. For each
w
in the support of
\nu
, we apply Lemma
D.4
to
z_{0},w
as above and (which is new compared to
[
BBNY17
]
) we choose the parameter
r
depending on
w
as follows:

r=r(w)=\frac{1}{10}|z_{0}-w|,

which is valid choice as it is obviously smaller than
\frac{1}{2}|z_{0}-w|
. We obtain a point
14
14

14

\tilde{z}
depends also on
z_{0}
but we will not write down this dependency as we work for any fixed
z_{0}
.

\tilde{z}=\tilde{z}(w)
and a real number
k=k(w)
such that (
D.11
) are satisfied. Let us make the following important observation: we know by the construction of Lemma
D.4
that
\tilde{z}
lies on the line segment between
z
and
w
, at distance
\frac{1}{100}|z_{0}-w|
from
z_{0}
. Since by assumption we have on the one hand:

\dist(z_{0},\partial\rho\mathfrak{D})\geq\kappa\geq 20\rho^{\prime},

and on the other hand
\supp\nu\subset\mathfrak{D}(0,\rho+\rho^{\prime})\setminus\mathfrak{D}(0,\rho)
it is easy to check that:

|\tilde{z}-z_{0}|\leq\frac{1}{100}\left(\dist(z_{0},\partial\rho\mathfrak{D})+\rho^{\prime}\right)\leq\frac{1}{4}\dist(z_{0},\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}),

and thus the point
\tilde{z}
remains in
\rho\mathfrak{D}
, in fact the entire disk
\mathfrak{D}\left(\tilde{z},r(w)\right)
is contained in
\rho\mathfrak{D}
.

This being done for all
w\in\supp\nu
we may define a map
L:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by:

L(z):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\supp\nu}\left(l_{r(w)}(z-\tilde{z}(w))+k(w)\right)\nu(\mathtt{d}w).

(D.13)

Finally, as in
[
BBNY17
, (3.18)]
we form the map
v=v_{z_{0}}
by setting:

v:z\mapsto\tau u_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}(z)+\rho^{-2}\left(L(z)+\tilde{R}(z)\right)+\rho^{-1}\gamma G(z),

(D.14)

and claim that is satisfies (
D.10
).

Sub-harmonicity

The distributional Laplacian is given by:

\Delta v=\tau\Delta u_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}+\rho^{-2}\Delta L+\rho^{-2}(2\partial_{n}^{-}R+\gamma)\mathtt{d}s,

(D.15)

the measure
\mathtt{d}s
being the arclength measure on
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
(we used the general formula of
[
BBNY17
, (3.10)]
and the fact that the jump of the normal derivatives of
G
along
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
is here
\rho^{-2}
). In the interior of
\mathfrak{D}
we obtain (taking only the first two terms in the right-hand side of (
D.15
), using the last item of Proposition
D.1
to evaluate
\Delta u_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}
, and an explicit computation of
\Delta l_{r}
- we recall that
l_{r}
is the logarithmic potential generated by the uniform probability measure on the disk of radius
r
):

\Delta v=\frac{\tau}{2\rho^{2}}-\rho^{-2}\left(\int_{\supp\nu}\frac{1}{r(w)^{2}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathfrak{D}(\tilde{z}(w),r(w))}\ \nu(\mathtt{d}w)\right)

(the differences with the analysis of
[
BBNY17
]
being that here
\alpha
(their notation) is of order
\rho^{-2}
,
\sigma
(their notation) is always
\frac{1}{2}
, that
L
here is scaled by
\rho^{-2}
and most importantly that the distance
r
is here chosen
depending on
w
as above). In order to check that
\Delta v\geq 0
(which is a requirement in (
D.10
)) we thus need to guarantee that:

\int_{\supp\nu}\frac{1}{r(w)^{2}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathfrak{D}(\tilde{z}(w),r(w))}\ \nu(\mathtt{d}w)\leq\frac{\tau}{2},\text{ with $\frac{\tau}{2}\geq\frac{9}{20}$ because $|\tau-1|\leq\frac{1}{10}$ by assumption.}

(D.16)

Here we stop following the route of
[
BBNY17
]
and instead recall that by construction
r(w)=\frac{1}{10}|z_{0}-w|
, thus we may write (bounding the indicator function by
1
):

\int_{\supp\nu}\frac{1}{r(w)^{2}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathfrak{D}(\tilde{z}(w),r(w))}\ \nu(\mathtt{d}w)\leq 100\int_{\supp\nu}\frac{1}{|w-z_{0}|^{2}}\nu(\mathtt{d}w).

Cover the annulus
\mathfrak{D}(0,\rho+\rho^{\prime})\setminus\mathfrak{D}(0,\rho)
, which by assumption contains the support of
\nu
, by
\mathcal{O}(\rho/\rho^{\prime})
squares of sidelength
\rho^{\prime}
. The quantity
|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}
allows us to bound the mass of
\nu
on each such square. Then for
z\in\rho\mathfrak{D}
, if
z
is at distance at least
20\rho^{\prime}
from the boundary then we can compare the integral
\int\frac{1}{|z-w|^{2}}\ \nu(\mathtt{d}w)
to

\frac{|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}\times\int_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{D}}\frac{1}{|z-w|^{2}}\mathtt{d}w,

which is
\mathcal{O}\left(|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})\times\frac{1}{\rho^{\prime}\dist(z,\partial\mathfrak{D})}\right)
and thus smaller than
\mathtt{C}\frac{|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{\kappa\rho^{\prime}}
if
z
is at distance at least
\kappa
from the boundary. In particular, if
\kappa
is larger than some constant times
\frac{|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}
we do have (
D.16
).

For the singular part on the boundary we need to ensure that
2\partial_{n}^{-}R+\gamma\geq 0
, which is true by definition of
\gamma
.

Obstacle property

We want to check that
v(z_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}(z_{0})
and that
v(z)\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}(z)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(in fact it is enough to check it on the disk as
\mathrm{W}
is infinite outside). This step goes on exactly like in the original proof. We already know that
u_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}(z)
on
\rho\mathfrak{D}
(because
u_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}
solves the “reference” obstacle problem), that
\tilde{R}(z)=R(z)
on
\rho\mathfrak{D}
(by definition), and that
G
vanishes on
\rho\mathfrak{D}
. Thus in view of (
D.5
) it suffices to have:

L(z_{0})=\mathfrak{h}^{\nu}(z_{0}),\quad L(z)\leq\mathfrak{h}^{\nu}(z)\text{ for $z\neq z_{0}$},

which was precisely guaranteed by construction of
L
using Lemma
D.4
, see (
D.11
) and (
D.13
).

Growth at infinity

Here we proceed again a bit differently than in
[
BBNY17
]
. Recall that we have chosen our function as:

\tau u_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}(z)+\rho^{-2}\left(L(z)+\tilde{R}(z)\right)+\rho^{-1}\gamma G(z).

As
|z|\to\infty
we have
u_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}}(z)\sim\log|z|
,
L(z)\sim-\|\nu\|\log|z|
,
\tilde{R}(z)=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
G(z)\sim\log z
. Thus the third condition of (
D.10
) is satisfied if we have:

\|\nu\|\geq\rho^{2}(1-\tau)+\rho\gamma.

We argue that we may always assume that this is the case. Indeed, if not, then we may distribute
15
15

15

This is inspired by a different but similar argument in the original proof.
positive charges uniformly with density
\gamma+\rho|1-\tau|
all around the circle
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
on an annulus of width
1
, and redefine the perturbation
R
accordingly in order for the total potential to remain the same. This operation changes
\nu
: it increases its total mass to a convenient level and increases
|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(1)
by
\gamma+\rho|1-\tau|
. In view of the “Sub-harmonicity” paragraph above, this is harmless as long as we assume that
\kappa
larger than some constant times
\gamma+\rho|1-\tau|
in (
D.8
).

On the other hand, this operation does not affect the quantity
\|\partial_{n}^{-}R\|_{\infty,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}}
as defined in (
D.7
), because placing a radially symmetric density of charges outside
\rho\mathfrak{D}
creates a
constant
potential within
\rho\mathfrak{D}
.

Hence without loss of generality we may assume that
\|\nu\|\geq\rho^{2}(1-\tau)+\rho\gamma
. This concludes the proof of Proposition
D.2
.
∎

D.1.4
Proof of Proposition
D.3

Proof of Proposition
D.3
.

This time the changes are minor compared to
[
BBNY17
, Proof of Prop. 3.4]
. We follow their computations until the moment where they bound:

\int\frac{z-w}{|z-w|^{2}}\cdot\vec{n}\ \nu(\mathtt{d}w)\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\nu\|,

where
z
is some fixed point on the circle and
\vec{n}
is the normal vector to the circle at
z
. We distinguish between
|w-z|\leq 10\rho^{\prime}
and
\geq 10\rho^{\prime}
. For the first contribution, we note
16
16

16

As in
[
BBNY17
, Proof of Prop. 3.4]
, except that they scale everything back to
\rho=1
.
that
\frac{z-w}{|z-w|^{2}}\cdot\vec{n}
is always smaller than
\frac{1}{2\rho}
and thus:

\int_{|w-z|\leq 10\rho^{\prime}}\frac{z-w}{|z-w|^{2}}\cdot\vec{n}\ \nu(\mathtt{d}w)\leq\frac{\mathtt{C}|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{2\rho}

We bound the rest of the integral by
\int_{|w-z|\geq 10\rho^{\prime}}\max\left(0,\frac{z-w}{|z-w|^{2}}\cdot\vec{n}\right)\nu(\mathtt{d}w)
, which we may compare to:

\frac{|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{D}\rho,|w-z|\geq 10\rho^{\prime}}\max\left(0,\frac{z-w}{|z-w|^{2}}\cdot\vec{n}\right)\mathtt{d}w,

which is itself smaller than:

\frac{|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}\int_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{D}\rho}\max\left(0,\frac{z-w}{|z-w|^{2}}\cdot\vec{n}\right)\mathtt{d}w=\frac{|\nu|_{\mathrm{loc}}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{O}(1)

by one-dimensional scale-invariance. This is the dominant contribution.

The rest of the proof does not change, and we obtain their final estimate controlling the Radon-Nikodym derivative pointwise by:

\frac{1-\tau}{2}\partial_{n}^{-}\mathrm{V}+\int\frac{z-w}{|z-w|^{2}}\cdot\vec{n}\ \nu(\mathtt{d}w)-\partial_{n}^{-}R+\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|.

There we bound
\partial_{n}^{-}\mathrm{V}
by
\rho^{-1}
(see (
D.4
)).
∎

D.2
Application to a generalized 2DOCP with good external potential

We now apply the knowledge gained in Section
D.1
to pass from a 2DOCP with good external potential to a 2DOCP with well-controlled non-uniform neutralizing background (see Sections
5.1
and
5.2
).

Let
\Lambda
be a disk of radius
\rho=T
and
\mathsf{n}
be the number of points in
\Lambda
. Let
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
be a “good external potential” on
\Lambda
. Consider the potential
\mathrm{W}
defined as:

\mathrm{W}:=\tau\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}+\frac{2}{\mathsf{n}}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}},\quad\tau:=\frac{|\Lambda|}{\mathsf{n}},

(D.17)

where
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}
is as in (
D.4
) (with
\rho=T
) and let
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
be the associated equilibrium measure, which we write (with the notation of Section
D.1.2
) as:

\mu_{\mathrm{W}}=\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}+\left(\eta-\tau\mathtt{1}_{B}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}\right).

(D.18)

We will use repeatedly below the fact that (see Definition
5.2
):

\tau=1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log^{2}T}{T}\right).

(D.19)

Some properties of the new equilibrium measure

Claim D.5
.

For some constant
\mathtt{C}_{\mu}
depending on the “good external potential”
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
constant of
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
, the following holds:

The measure
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}-\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}
has mass
1-\tau=1-\frac{|\Lambda|}{\mathsf{n}}
, and is made of:

1.

A continuous (negative) part of density
-\frac{\tau}{|\Lambda|}
supported on a subset
B
located at distance
\leq\kappa
from
\partial\rho\mathfrak{D}
, with
\kappa
as in (
D.8
). We can take:

\kappa=\mathtt{C}_{\mu}\times\log^{2}T.

(D.20)

2.

A singular component
\eta
living on
\partial\Lambda
, with a density
\frac{\mathtt{d}\eta}{\mathtt{d}s}
bounded as in (
D.9
). We have:

\|\frac{\mathtt{d}\eta}{\mathtt{d}s}\|\leq\mathtt{C}_{\mu}\frac{\log^{2}T}{T^{2}}.

(D.21)

The total mass of the continuous part, of the singular part, and the total variation of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}-\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}
is bounded by
\mathtt{C}_{\mu}\frac{\log^{2}T}{T}
.
Moreover, we have:

\mathsf{n}^{2}\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\left(\eta-\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}\mathtt{1}_{B}\right)(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\eta-\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}\mathtt{1}_{B}\right)(y)=\mathcal{O}\left(\mathtt{C}_{\mu}^{2}T^{2}\log^{5}(T)\right).

(D.22)

Proof of Claim
D.5
.

Inserting Definition
5.1
of a “good external potential” into the bounds (
D.8
) and (
D.9
) with
\rho^{\prime}=\hat{T}=\log T
, and using (
D.19
) we obtain (
D.20
) and (
D.21
).

The total mass of the continuous part is of order
T\times\mathtt{C}_{\mu}\log^{2}T\times T^{-2}=\frac{\log^{2}T}{T}
(it is contained in a region at distance
\leq\mathtt{C}_{\mu}\log^{2}T
from the boundary of the disk of radius
T
, and its density is of order
T^{-2}
).

The mass of the singular part matches the mass of the continuous part up to an error
1-\tau
, but (
D.19
) holds. Hence the mass of the singular part is also bounded by
\mathtt{C}_{\mu}\frac{\log^{2}T}{T}
, and so is the total variation of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}-\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}
.

Scaling everything back to a disk of radius
1
, and bounding the self-interaction using (
D.20
), (
D.21
), we get (
D.22
).
∎

Inserting the equilibrium measure into the energy

Let
\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}
be given by:

\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}=\mathfrak{h}^{\mu_{\mathrm{W}}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}-\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{W}}(\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}.

(D.23)

It is a standard fact of logarithmic potential theory that the function
\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}
vanishes on the support of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
and is non-negative outside of it, and we refer to
\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}
as the “effective confining potential” (see
[
Ser15
, Definition 2.18]
).

Lemma D.6
.

We have, using the notation of (
4.10
) and (
5.8
):
\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}}^{\beta}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\cdot,\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}},\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}\right)
thus (
5.11
) is justified.

Proof of Lemma
D.6
.

We start with the following claim:

Claim D.7
.

Let
\mu_{\mathsf{n}}:=\frac{1}{\mathsf{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\delta_{x_{i}}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{n}}\mathbf{X}
be the empirical measure associated to a
\mathsf{n}
-tuple in
\Lambda
. We have:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})+\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\left(\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(y)+\int\left(\frac{|\Lambda|}{\mathsf{n}}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}(x)+\frac{2}{\mathsf{n}}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\right)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\right)\\
-\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(2\mathsf{n}|\Lambda|-|\Lambda|^{2}\right)\mathcal{I}_{\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}}(\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}})-|\Lambda|\left(\mathsf{n}-|\Lambda|\right)\int\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}\right).

Proof.

The proof is elementary but it does require some care because of the possible non-neutrality and the various scalings involved. We start with expanding the definition (
4.9
) of
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})
as:

2\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})=\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(y)+\iint_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}x\mathtt{d}y-2\iint_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\mathtt{d}y.

Introducing the empirical measure
\mu_{\mathsf{n}}
and the reference probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}
(uniform on
\Lambda
) we get:

2\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})=\mathsf{n}^{2}\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(y)+|\Lambda|^{2}\iint_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(y)\\
-2\mathsf{n}|\Lambda|\iint_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(y).

(D.24)

Let us recall the following standard identity valid on
\Lambda
(see (
D.2
) or e.g.
[
Ser15
, Thm. 2.1]
):

\int_{\Lambda}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(y)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}(x)+\left(\mathcal{I}_{\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}}(\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}})-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}\right),

(D.25)

we obtain after some computations:

2\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})=\mathsf{n}^{2}\left(\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(y)+\frac{|\Lambda|}{\mathsf{n}}\int\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\\
-\left(2\mathsf{n}|\Lambda|-|\Lambda|^{2}\right)\mathcal{I}_{\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}}(\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}})+|\Lambda|\left(\mathsf{n}-|\Lambda|\right)\int\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}.

(D.26)

Dividing by
2
and inserting the contribution of
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
, we obtain the claim.
∎

Claim D.8
.

We have:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})+\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}=\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}+\mathrm{Const.}(\mathsf{n},\Lambda,\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}})

Proof of Claim
D.8
.

On the one hand, we know by Claim
D.7
that:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})+\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\left(\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(y)+\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)+\mathrm{Const.}(\mathsf{n},\Lambda).

On the other hand, using the “splitting formula” of Sandier-Serfaty (see
[
SS15
, Lemma 2.1]
) we have:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\left(\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(y)+\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{W}}(\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x).

(D.27)

Discarding quantities that do not depend on the configuration
\mathbf{X}
, we obtain the claim.
∎

We may thus equivalently use
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}
instead of
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})+\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}
in the Boltzmann’s factor, up to some constant that gets absorbed in the partition function.
∎

D.3
Proof of Proposition
5.7

Proof of Proposition
5.7
.

By definition of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}
we have:

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]=\log\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+2\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\right)}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}\\
-\log\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\right)}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}.

(D.28)

1. Configuration-wise lower bound on the energy.

Since
\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}
is non-negative, we have:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+2\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}\geq\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}).

Because of the possible singularity of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
along the boundary, we cannot directly use results like
[
AS21
, Lemma B.2.]
to bound
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}
from below. Instead, for each
i=1,\dots,\mathsf{n}
we let
\eta(x_{i}):=\frac{1}{4}\min\left(1,\dist(x_{i},\partial\Lambda)\right)
and use this as a “truncation vector”. By construction, the disks
\mathfrak{D}(x_{i},\eta(x_{i}))
do not intersect the support of the singular part of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
, we may thus use the monotonicity property of
[
AS21
, Lemma B.1]
(or Onsager’s lemma) and write that:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\log\eta(x_{i})\right)-\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{n}).

The integral is obviously non-negative, and it remains to find a lower bound for the negative contribution coming from
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\log\eta(x_{i})
. This is where we use our restriction to the event
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
. Since
\mathbf{X}
is assumed to satisfy the conditions of Definition
5.2
we know that:

1.

There is no index
i
such that
\eta(x_{i})\leq e^{-\log^{2}T}
.

2.

There are at most
T\log T
indices
i
such that
\eta(x_{i})\leq\frac{1}{4}
.

We thus have the rough lower bound:
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\log\eta(x_{i})\geq-\mathsf{n}\log 4-T\log T\times\log^{2}T
. Hence for
\mathbf{X}\in\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
, we have the configuration-wise lower bound:
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})\geq-\mathcal{O}\left(T^{2}\right)
, which we can integrate in order to obtain (with an implicit constant depending only on
\beta
):

\log\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+2\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}\right)}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}\leq\log|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|+\mathcal{O}\left(T^{2}\right),

(D.29)

where
|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|
denotes the volume of the event
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
under the Lebesgue measure
\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}
on
\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}
.

2. Lower bound on the partition function.

To find an upper bound on the second term in the right-hand side of (
D.28
), we rely on a “Jensen’s trick” inspired by
[
GZ19
]
and write:

-\log\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\right)}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}\\
\leq-\log|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|+\beta\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|}.

(D.30)

Let us start by doing computations without the indicator
\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})
.

Claim D.9
.

We have:

\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}|}\leq\mathcal{O}(T^{2}\log^{5}T).

(D.31)

Proof of Claim
D.9
.

Let us recall that the integrand can be written (see (
D.27
)) as:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\left(\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(y)+\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{W}}(\mu_{\mathrm{W}}).

By elementary computations, we obtain:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\iint_{x\neq y}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}(y)\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}(\mathsf{n}-1)\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(y)

and similarly:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int\mathrm{W}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(x),

and thus after some simplifications we get:

\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}})+\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}\\
=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}(\mathsf{n}-1)\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(y)+\frac{1}{2}n^{2}\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}-\frac{1}{2}n^{2}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{W}}(\mu_{\mathrm{W}}).

We now insert the expressions (
D.17
), (
D.18
) for
\mathrm{W}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
, the definition (
D.1
) of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{W}}(\mu_{\mathrm{W}})
, expand and use the identity (
D.25
). We obtain:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}(\mathsf{n}-1)\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(y)+\frac{1}{2}n^{2}\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}-\frac{1}{2}n^{2}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{W}}(\mu_{\mathrm{W}})=\\
\mathsf{n}^{2}\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\left(\eta-\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}\mathtt{1}_{B}\right)(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\eta-\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}\mathtt{1}_{B}\right)(y)-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(y)\\
+\mathsf{n}\int\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\left(\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}-\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}\right)(x).

(D.32)

Using (
D.22
) we control the first term in the right-hand side of (
D.32
) by
\mathcal{O}(T^{2}\log^{5}(T))
. On the other hand, by a direct estimate, we have
\mathsf{n}\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(y)=\mathcal{O}(T^{2}\log T)
. It remains to bound
\mathsf{n}\int\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}\left(\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}-\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}\right)
from above. Since
\mu_{\mathrm{W}},\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}
have the same mass, it is equivalent to bound:

\mathsf{n}\int\left(\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)\right)\left(\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}-\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}\right)(x),

where
\omega
is the center of
\Lambda
. Let us decompose the integral into two parts:

Away from the boundary.
On
\{\dist(z,\partial\Lambda)\geq\kappa\}
we know that
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
coincides with
\tau\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}
and thus (since we take
\kappa\geq 1
):

\left|\mathsf{n}\int_{\{\dist(z,\partial\Lambda)\geq\kappa\}}\left(\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(z)-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)\right)\left(\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}-\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}\right)\right|\leq\mathsf{n}\times|1-\tau|\times\sup_{\{\dist(z,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D})\geq 1\}}|\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(z)-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)|.

We know that
|1-\tau|=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log^{2}T}{T}\right)
and by assumption we have
|\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(z)-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)|=\mathcal{O}(T\log^{3}T)
on
\{\dist(z,\partial\rho\mathfrak{D})\geq 1\}
. Thus the contribution “away from the boundary” is bounded by
\mathcal{O}\left(T^{2}\log^{5}T\right)
.

Near the boundary.
On
\{\dist(z,\partial\Lambda)\leq\kappa\}
we control each contribution separately. On the one hand, we have, using the mean value formula for
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
(which is harmonic on
\Lambda
):

\mathsf{n}\int_{\{\dist(z,\partial\Lambda)\geq\kappa\}}\left(\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(z)-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)\right)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(z)=0.

On the other hand,
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
being a non-negative measure, we may write:

-\mathsf{n}\int_{\{\dist(z,\partial\Lambda)\geq\kappa\}}\left(\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(z)-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)\right)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}(z)\leq-\mathsf{n}\int_{\{\dist(z,\partial\Lambda)\geq\kappa\}}\left(\tilde{\mathrm{V}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(z)-\tilde{\mathrm{V}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)\right)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}(z),

where we used the auxiliary function
\tilde{\mathrm{V}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
from Definition
5.1
. The mass of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
near the boundary is bounded by
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\kappa}{T}+T\|\frac{\mathtt{d}\eta}{\mathtt{d}s}\|_{\infty}\right)
. Using the results of Claim
D.5
and the definition of a “good external potential” we bound the contribution “near the boundary” by
\mathcal{O}\left(T^{2}\log^{5}T\right)
also.

This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

It remains to argue that restricting our integrals to
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
as in (
D.30
) instead of (
D.31
) has no consequence on the estimate, which is not totally obvious. We make a crucial use of the following observation: the “cluster bound” for independent points is not worse than the one mentioned in (
2.16
), and we thus have:

\frac{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|}{|\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}|}\geq 1-\exp\left(-\log^{2}T\right).

(D.33)

Claim D.10
.

We have:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})\left(\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|^{\mathsf{n}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}+\exp\left(-\log^{2}T\right).

(D.34)

Proof of Lemma
D.10
.

Let
\mathtt{C}
be some large constant, and let

\hat{\mathrm{W}}:=\mathrm{W}-\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\omega)+\mathtt{C}T^{2}.

(D.35)

Substracting the same constant to both sides, we may write that:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})\left(\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|}=\\
\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\int\hat{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})\left(\int\hat{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|}.

(D.36)

On the other hand, in view of the definition (
D.17
) of
\mathrm{W}
and the properties of the “good external potential”
\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}
as listed in Definition
5.1
, we know that by choosing the constant
\mathtt{C}
large enough we can guarantee that
\hat{\mathrm{W}}\geq 0
, in which case it is clear that:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})\left(\int\hat{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\int\hat{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}\times\left(\frac{|\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}|}{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|}\right).

(D.37)

We thus see that:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X})\left(\int\mathrm{W}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\int\hat{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\frac{|\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}|}{|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}|}-1\right).

On the other hand, the same computation as in the proof of Claim
D.9
yields:

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\left(\int\hat{\mathrm{W}}\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathsf{n}}\right)\frac{\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}}{|\Lambda|^{\mathsf{n}}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{n}^{2}\int\hat{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mu_{\mathrm{V}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\Lambda}}(x),

which we can evaluate explicitely using our choice (
D.35
) for
\hat{\mathrm{W}}
and the expression (
D.17
) for
\mathrm{W}
. We obtain some polynomial in
T
, which gets absorbed by the sub-algebraic tail of (
D.33
). This concludes the proof of (
D.34
).
∎

We could proceed similarly for the other term in the integrand (which is easier because
\mathrm{W}
does not play any role).

This concludes the proof of (
5.12
).
∎

Appendix E
Effect of localized translations on the energy: Proof of Proposition
6.3

In all this section,
\varepsilon
and
\ell
are fixed and we assume that
|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}
. To lighten notation, we will drop some dependencies with respect to
\varepsilon
and
\ell
.

E.1
Some additional properties of localized translations

We decompose
\Phi_{t}
in two ways, either as:

\Phi_{t}(x)=x+\psi_{t}(x),

(E.1)

which defines a vector field
\psi_{t}
, or alternatively as:

\Phi_{t}(x)=x+t\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}(x)+\gamma_{t}(x),

(E.2)

which defines a vector field
\gamma_{t}
.

Proposition E.1
.

1.

We have
|\Phi_{t}-\Id|_{\mathsf{0}}=|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{0}}\leq 2|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{5}
.

2.

For
|x|\leq\ell/4
, we have
|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x)=0
,
|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}(x)=0
.

3.

For
\ell/4\leq|x|\leq 2\ell e^{1/\varepsilon}
, we have:

|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{k},\star}(x)\leq\frac{\mathtt{C}t\varepsilon}{|x|^{\mathsf{k}}}\text{ for }\mathsf{k}=1,2.

(E.3)

4.

For
|x|\leq\ell/4
, we have
\gamma_{t}\equiv 0
.

5.

For
\ell/4\leq|x|\leq 2\ell e^{1/\varepsilon}
, we have:

|\gamma_{t}|_{\mathsf{k},\star}(x)\leq\frac{\mathtt{C}t^{2}\varepsilon}{|x|^{\mathsf{k}+1}},\text{ for }\mathsf{k}=0,1,2,3.

(E.4)

Proof of Proposition
E.1
.

Let us start by some general observations.

Preliminary claims.

Since the vector field
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}
is bounded by
2
(see Lemma
6.1
) the distance
|\Phi_{t}(x)-x|
is always smaller than
2|t|
and thus
|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{0}}\leq 2|t|
. This proves the first item. On the other hand, as stated in Lemma
6.2
, the flow
\Phi_{t}
acts as a translation on
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/4)
, which means that
\psi_{t}
is a constant and thus the derivatives of
\psi_{t}
vanish identically there. This proves the second item, and also implies that the second-order correction
\gamma_{t}
vanishes identically on
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/4)
.

In view of the bounds on
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
given in Lemma
6.1
and the definition of
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}:=\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon)}(\cdot/\ell)
, after scaling we obtain for
\mathsf{k}=1,2
:

|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}|_{\mathsf{k},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon\begin{cases}\frac{1}{|x|^{\mathsf{k}}}&\text{ for }|x|\geq 2\ell\\
\frac{1}{|\ell|^{\mathsf{k}}}&\text{ for }|x|\leq 2\ell.\end{cases}

Thus we may always write that
|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}|_{\mathsf{k},\star}(x)\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon\frac{1}{|x|^{\mathsf{k}}}
.

Claim E.2
.

If
|x|\geq\frac{\ell}{4}
then:

|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}|_{\mathsf{k},\star}(\Phi_{t}(x))\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon\frac{1}{|x|^{\mathsf{k}}}

(E.5)

Proof.

Since
|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}
and
|\Phi_{t}(x)-x|\leq 2|t|
, we can ensure that if
|x|\geq\frac{\ell}{4}
, then
|\Phi_{t}(x)|\geq\frac{1}{5}|x|
, so in view of the previous estimates on
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}
, we obtain the claim.
∎

Let us end this paragraph with a simple general fact which will be useful to prove the remaining bounds:

\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\|f(t)\|\leq\|\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}f(t)\|.

(E.6)

Initial controls on
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{k}}\Phi_{t}
.

Claim E.3
.

We have, for
|x|\geq\frac{\ell}{4}
:

\|\mathsf{D}\Phi_{t}(x)\|\leq 1+\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon t}{|x|},\quad\|\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{k}}\Phi_{t}(x)\|\leq\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon t}{|x|^{\mathsf{k}}}\text{ for $\mathsf{k}=2,3$}

(E.7)

Proof of Claim
E.3
.

At
t=0
we have
\Phi_{t}=\Id
and thus
\mathsf{D}\Phi_{t}\equiv\Id
. Then we can compute:

\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\|\mathsf{D}\Phi_{t}(x)\|\leq\|\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\mathsf{D}\Phi_{t}(x)\|=\|\mathsf{D}\left(\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}\circ\Phi_{t}(x)\right)\|\leq\|\mathsf{D}\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}\circ\Phi_{t}(x)\|\times\|\mathsf{D}\Phi_{t}(x)\|.

Using (
E.5
) and an elementary differential inequality, we see that:

\|\mathsf{D}\Phi_{t}(x)\|\leq e^{\frac{\mathtt{C}\varepsilon t}{|x|}}=1+\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\frac{\varepsilon t}{|x|}.

Arguing similarly, we have:
\mathsf{D}^{2}\Phi_{t}=0
at
t=0
, and:

\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\|\mathsf{D}^{2}\Phi_{t}(x)\|\leq\|\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\mathsf{D}^{2}\Phi_{t}(x)\|=\|\mathsf{D}^{2}\left(\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}\circ\Phi_{t}(x)\right)\|.

Applying Leibniz’s rule, using the previous bound under the rough form
\|\mathsf{D}\Phi_{t}\|\leq\mathtt{C}
and inserting (
E.5
) again, we obtain:

\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\|\mathsf{D}^{2}\Phi_{t}(x)\|\leq\frac{\mathtt{C}\varepsilon}{|x|^{2}}+\frac{\mathtt{C}\varepsilon}{|x|}\times\|\mathsf{D}^{2}\Phi_{t}(x)\|.

Solving again the differential inequality with initial condition
0
, we obtain:

\|\mathsf{D}^{2}\Phi_{t}(x)\|\leq\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon t}{|x|^{2}}

We proceed the same way for the third derivative.
∎

Application 1: properties of
\psi_{t}

Returning to the definition (
E.1
) of
\psi_{t}
, we see that
\mathsf{D}\psi_{t}=0
at
t=0
and we may then compute:

\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\|\mathsf{D}\psi_{t}(x)\|\leq\|\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\mathsf{D}\psi_{t}(x)\|=\|\mathsf{D}\left(\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}\circ\Phi_{t}(x)\right)\|\leq|\mathsf{D}\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}\circ\Phi_{t}(x)\|\times\|\mathsf{D}\Phi_{t}(x)\|.

By the same computations as above, we find
\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\|\mathsf{D}\psi_{t}(x)\|\leq\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon}{|x|}
which implies the bound (
E.3
) for
\mathsf{k}=1
.

To study
|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{2}}
, observe that
\mathsf{D}^{2}\Phi_{t}=\mathsf{D}^{2}\psi_{t}
and use (
E.7
).

Application 2: properties of
\gamma_{t}
.

If we want to control, say
|\gamma_{t}|_{\mathsf{0}}
, we compute:

\left|\frac{\mathtt{d}}{\mathtt{d}t}\gamma_{t}\right|=\left|\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}\circ\Phi_{t}(x)-\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}(x)\right|\leq\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{0}}}{|x|}\leq\mathtt{C}\frac{\varepsilon t}{|x|},

where we used the bound on
|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{0}}
stated as the first item of Proposition
E.1
together with Claim
E.2
in order to bound the Lipschitz constant of
\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}
between
x
and
\Phi_{t}(x)
. Integrating on
t
yields
|\gamma_{t}|_{\mathsf{0}}\preceq\varepsilon t^{2}|x|^{-1}
as claimed.

Higher derivatives are controlled the same way, using Leibniz’s rule together with Claims
E.2
and
E.3
.
∎

Upper bounds on the derivatives of
\psi_{t}
.

Let us introduce two functions
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
:

\Psi_{1}:x\mapsto\frac{\mathtt{C}t\varepsilon}{(\ell+|x|)},\quad\Psi_{2}:x\mapsto\frac{\mathtt{C}t\varepsilon}{(\ell+|x|)^{2}}

(E.8)

Choosing the constant
\mathtt{C}
suitably we have, in view of Proposition
E.1
, the pointwise bounds:

|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x)\leq\Psi_{1}(x),\quad|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}(x)\leq\Psi_{2}(x),\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

thus when looking for
upper bounds
we may replace occurrences of
|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{1},\star},|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}
by
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
. The upside of working with
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
is that they enjoy the following properties deduced from elementary calculus:

•

(Slow variation at scale
\ell
.) For all
x
we have:

\sup_{y,|x-y|\leq\ell}\frac{|\Psi_{1}(y)|}{|\Psi_{1}(x)|}\leq 2,\quad\sup_{y,|x-y|\leq\ell}\frac{|\Psi_{2}(y)|}{|\Psi_{2}(x)|}\leq 2.

(E.9)

•

(Slow variation.) For all
x
we have:

\sup_{y,|x-y|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x|}\frac{|\Psi_{1}(y)|}{|\Psi_{1}(x)|}\leq 2,\quad\sup_{y,|x-y|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x|}\frac{|\Psi_{2}(y)|}{|\Psi_{2}(x)|}\leq 2.

(E.10)

Both (
E.9
) and (
E.10
) will be convenient to simplify computations later on. We also record the following simple facts:

Claim E.4
.

If
\varepsilon
is chosen smaller than some universal constant, then:

•

|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq\frac{1}{5}
(globally).

•

For all
x,x^{\prime}
we have:

\frac{1}{2}|x-x^{\prime}|\leq|\Phi_{t}(x)-\Phi_{t}(x^{\prime})|\leq 2|x-x^{\prime}|.

•

For all
x
we have:

|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(\Phi_{t}(x))\leq 2\Psi_{1}(x),\quad|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{2},\star}(\Phi_{t}(x))\leq 2\Psi_{2}(x).

(E.11)

Proof of Claim
E.4
.

The first item follows directly from Proposition
E.1
(see in particular (
E.3
)). It implies the second item straightforwardly. To prove (
E.11
) let us recall that
|\Phi_{t}(x)-x|\leq 2|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{5}
(by the first item of Proposition
E.1
and the assumption
|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}
) and that
|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(\Phi_{t}(x))\leq\Psi_{1}(\Phi_{t}(x))
by construction (similarly for
|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{2},\star},\Psi_{2}
). We thus have:

|\psi_{t}|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(\Phi_{t}(x))\leq\Psi_{1}(\Phi_{t}(x))\leq\sup_{|y-x|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}}\Psi_{1}(y),

which is smaller than
2\Psi_{1}(x)
according to (
E.9
), as desired.
∎

E.2
The “well-spread” event

Definition E.5

(The
\mathsf{WellSpread}
event)
.

Let
\Omega
be some subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
\ell\geq 1
be a length-scale, let
K\geq 10
. We define the event
\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Omega,\ell,K)
as follows:

\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Omega,\ell,K):=\bigcap_{x\in(\ell\mathbb{Z})^{2}\cap\Omega}\left\{\mathsf{Pts}(\mathbf{X},\square(x,\ell))\leq K\ell^{2}\right\}\cap\left\{\mathrm{Ener}(\mathbf{X},\square(x,\ell))\leq K\ell^{2}\right\}.

(E.12)

Saying that
\mathbf{X}\in\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Omega,\ell,K)
essentially means that if we cover
\Omega
by squares of side-length
\ell
, then the number of points and the electric energy in each square are of order
\ell^{2}
, which is what we expect in view of the local laws. It has the following consequences:

1.

If
\mathbf{X}\in\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Omega,\ell,K)
then for each
x\in(\ell\mathbb{Z})^{2}\cap\Omega
the quantity
\mathrm{Ener}_{s}(\mathbf{X},\square(x,\ell))
(as defined in (
2.5
)) is bounded by
K\ell^{2}(1+\log s)
for
s\in(0,1)
.

2.

Let us say that a function
f
varies slowly at scale
\ell
when:

\sup_{|x^{\prime}-x|\leq 2\ell}|f(x^{\prime})|\leq 10|f(x)|.

(E.13)

Then if
f
satisfies (
E.13
) and is supported in
\Omega
, assuming that
\mathbf{X}\in\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Omega,\ell,K)
allows us to make two computational simplifications:

(a)

An energy density upper bound, namely (for
s\in(0,1)
):

\left|\int f(x)|\nabla h_{s\vec{\mathsf{r}}}|^{2}\mathtt{d}x\right|\preceq K\|f\|_{L^{1}}(1+|\log s|).

(E.14)

(b)

Sum-integral comparisons:

\left|\sum_{x\in\mathbf{X}}f(x)\mathtt{d}x\right|\preceq K\|f\|_{L^{1}}.

(E.15)

(To prove (
E.14
) and (
E.15
), cover the support of
f
by squares of sidelength
\ell
and use (
E.13
)).

Lemma E.6

(The Well-Spread event is frequent)
.

For
K
larger than some constant (depending on
\beta
and the “good external potential” constant
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
) and if
\varepsilon\ell^{2}\geq 1
(which is guaranteed by (
6.2
)), we have:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}},\ell,K)\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\ell^{2}\right)

(E.16)

Proof of Lemma
E.6
.

Covering
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
by
\mathcal{O}(T^{2}\ell^{-2})
squares of sidelength
\ell
and using the local laws in
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
on each one, together with a union bound, we get:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}},\ell,K)\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right)\geq 1-\mathtt{C}\frac{T^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{K\ell^{2}}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right).

(E.17)

Using the relation between
T,\ell
and
\varepsilon
as in (
6.2
), we obtain:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}},\ell,K)\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right)\geq 1-\mathtt{C}\exp\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}-\frac{K\ell^{2}}{\mathtt{C}_{\beta}}\right),

and thus if we choose
K
larger than some constant (depending on
\beta
and
\bar{\mathtt{C}}
) and impose that
\varepsilon\ell^{2}\geq 1
then (
E.16
) holds.
∎

E.3
The measure-preserving case

Let
|t|\leq\frac{\ell}{10}
and let
\Phi=\Phi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}
be the localized translation constructed in Section
6.2
. Let
\psi=\psi_{t}^{\varepsilon,\ell}=\Phi-\Id
as studied in Proposition
E.1
. In this section, we carefully inspect the proof of
[
Ser20
, Prop 4.2]
in order to obtain the following:

Proposition E.7

(Energy comparison along a measure-preserving map)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a point configuration with
\mathsf{n}
points in
\Lambda
. Assume that
\mathbf{X}
belongs to
\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}},\ell,K)
for a certain
K>1
. Then:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi\cdot\mathbf{X})=\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})+\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}\left[\mathbf{X},\psi\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(K^{2}t^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\right).

(E.18)

The quantity
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
appears in the proof as a black box (see (
E.46
), we refer to Section
E.4
for more details.

Remark E.8

(Comparison with existing statements)
.

Compared to the result of
[
Ser20
]
there are two modifications:

1.

We estimate the energy cost of transporting by
\Phi
through the
local
density of electric energy density and points instead of using the global one (denoted by
\Xi(t)
in
[
Ser20
]
).

2.

The quantity
|\psi|_{L^{\infty}}|\psi|_{C^{2}}
present in the control on the second derivative of the energy in
[
Ser20
, Prop. 4.2]
does not appear in our computations.

Both items are crucial for us. The localization allows us to bound the error in terms of
\int_{\Lambda}|\psi|^{2}_{1,\star}
instead of
|\psi|^{2}_{1}\times|\Lambda|
- in the case of our localized translation the former is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
while the latter is gigantic. On the other hand, even after localizing, the contribution of
|\psi|_{L^{\infty}}|\psi|_{C^{2}}
would be of order
1
but not small
17
17

17

It is in fact impossible to make it small by choosing a different “localized translation”, because in dimension
2
the (homogeneous) Sobolev space
\dot{W}^{2,1}
is embedded in
L^{\infty}
. This is, fortunately, not the case for
\dot{W}^{1,2}
.
so it was necessary to get rid of it.

Obtaining these two refinements requires significant adaptations. On the other hand, the “measure-preserving” character of
\Phi
will bring several small simplifications: the background measure is not affected by the transport so all distinctions between
\mu
and
\nu:=\Phi\#\mu
(using the notation of
[
Ser20
]
) are void. We will in particular repeatedly use the fact that (for various functions
f
):

\int f(x)\left(\mathtt{d}(\Phi\cdot\mathbf{X})(x)-\mathtt{d}x\right)=\int f(\Phi(x))\left(\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)-\mathtt{d}x\right).

Since we are working on a disk with a constant background, the logarithmic potential generated by said background is explicitly computable and given by:

\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}(x):=\int_{\Lambda}-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}y=\frac{|x|^{2}}{4}.

(E.19)

We use the explicit expression (
E.19
) for simplicity a couple times below, although one could work with a more general shape and proceed to a more careful analysis instead.

Proof of Proposition
E.7
.

We follow the steps of
[
Ser20
, Appendix A]
while making several important changes. We will only use a couple technical results as “black boxes” and copy or adapt all the main arguments and computations.

E.3.1
Transporting vector fields

Definition E.9

(Transport of vector fields)
.

If
v
is a vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we define
\Phi\#v
, the “transport of
v
by
\Phi
”, as:

\Phi\#v:=\left(\mathsf{D}\Phi\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)^{T}v\circ\Phi^{-1}.

(E.20)

The point is that when
\dive v
is a measure we have:

\dive\Phi\#v=\Phi\#\left(\dive v\right),

(E.21)

where on the left-hand side there is a
transport of vector fields
while the right-hand side is a
push-forward of measures
. The identity (
E.21
) is a special case of
[
Ser20
, Lemma A.3]
. On top of it, we make the following simple observation:

Claim E.10
.

If
v=\nabla h
is a gradient, then:

\Phi\#\nabla h=\left(\mathsf{D}\Phi\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)^{T}\nabla\left(h\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)\left(\mathsf{D}\Phi\circ\Phi^{-1}\right).

Thus is
v
is a gradient and
\Phi
is close to the identity map, then
\Phi\#v
is “almost” a gradient. The proof of Claim
E.10
is straightforward using the definition (
E.20
) and some calculus.

E.3.2
Setting up the energy comparison

We want to compare
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{Y})
to
\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})
. We start by recalling known expressions for both quantities. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}},\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{Y}}
be the true electric potentials generated by
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
in
\Lambda
in the sense of Definition
2.1
. As a truncation vector, let us choose
18
18

18

Such a choice of a very small truncation parameter appears in
[
LS18
,
Ser20
]
. It might seem “unphysical” but it is very convenient to get rid of annoying error terms, while only costing
\log s
in view of (
2.6
).

\vec{\eta}:=s\vec{\mathsf{r}}\text{ with }s=\varepsilon^{2}

(E.22)

(in particular
s\leq\frac{1}{10}
), the distances
\vec{\mathsf{r}}
being computed with respect to the configuration
\mathbf{X}
. We recall that, by Claim
E.4
:

\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq|y_{j}-y_{i}|\leq 2|x_{i}-x_{j}|,

(E.23)

and thus if we compute the nearest-neighbor distances
\vec{\mathsf{r}}
with respect to
\mathbf{Y}
instead of
\mathbf{X}
we still have
\vec{\eta}\leq 2s\vec{\mathsf{r}}\leq\frac{1}{5}\vec{\mathsf{r}}
.

From Lemma
5.6
we know that:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})-\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{Y})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\int|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}-\int|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{Y}}|^{2}\right),

where
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X}}=\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m}}
,
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{Y}}:=\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{m}}
(the background will be
\mathbf{m}=\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
everywhere and we omit it) and
\vec{\eta}
is as in (
E.22
). We introduce two additional vector fields, using the notation of (
E.20
) for the first one:

\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}:=\Phi\#\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}},\quad\nabla\hat{h}:=\nabla(-\log)\ast\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\Phi\#\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}-\mathbf{m}\right),

(E.24)

(we recall that
\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}
denotes the uniform measure of mass
1
spread on the circle of center
x_{i}
and radius
\eta_{i}
). To summarize we have, besides
\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}
which is the true electric field generated by
\mathbf{X}
,

\displaystyle\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{Y}}=\nabla(-\log)\star\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\delta_{\Phi(x_{i})}^{(\eta_{i})}-\mathbf{m}\right)

\displaystyle\nabla\hat{h}=\nabla(-\log)\star\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\Phi\#\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}-\mathbf{m}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}=\Phi\#\nabla(-\log)\star\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}-\mathbf{m}\right).

There are subtle differences between these three vector fields:

•

\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{Y}}
is a gradient, it is the true electric field generated by
\mathbf{Y}
, and in its divergence the charges are spread along a circle with centers
y_{i}=\Phi(x_{i})
(
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}
).

•

\nabla\hat{h}
is also a gradient, but the charges are spread along deformed circles
\Phi\#\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}
which are (approximately) ellipses of center
y_{i}
.

•

\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}
is not a gradient in general, but it is obtained by transporting
\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}
(which is a gradient and for which the charges are spread along circles around the original points
x_{i}
’s) according to (
B.3
).

By the identity (
E.21
),
\nabla\hat{h}
and
\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}
have the same divergence, and as in
[
Ser20
, (A.23)]
we get the decomposition:

\int|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}=\int|\nabla\hat{h}|^{2}+\int|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}-\nabla\hat{h}|^{2}.

Bounding the difference
\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}-\nabla\hat{h}
.

We control the second term in the right-hand side immediately. Using Claim
E.10
we see that:

\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}=\left(\mathsf{D}\Phi\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)^{T}\nabla\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathbf{X}}\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)\left(\mathsf{D}\Phi\circ\Phi^{-1}\right),

and thus since
\Phi=\Id+\psi
with
|\psi|_{\mathsf{1}}
smaller than
\frac{1}{5}
we have the pointwise bound:

|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}-\nabla\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathbf{X}}\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)|\leq\mathtt{C}\|\mathsf{D}\psi\circ\Phi^{-1}\|\times|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}\circ\Phi^{-1}|,

which implies (after integrating the previous inequality and changing variables by
\Phi
):

\int|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}-\nabla\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathbf{X}}\circ\Phi^{-1}\right|^{2}\leq\mathtt{C}\int|\psi|^{2}_{1,\star}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}.

This provides an upper bound on the
L^{2}
-distance between
\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}
and the space of gradients, and thus since
\nabla\hat{h}
is its projection onto that space we get:

\int|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}-\nabla\hat{h}|^{2}\leq\mathtt{C}\int_{\Lambda}|\psi|^{2}_{1,\star}(x)|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x.

(E.25)

This is the first moment where we will use the notation and simple facts of Section
6.2
combined with our
\mathsf{WellSpread}
assumption. First, replacing
|\psi|_{1,\star}
by
\Psi_{1}
(as in (
E.8
)) provides an upper bound. Next, since
\Psi_{1}
has slow variations at scale
\ell
(see (
E.9
)) and we are working on
\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda,\ell,K)
, we may apply the energy density upper bound (
E.14
) (we will apply a similar chain of argument repeatedly in the rest of the proof). Here in conclusion, we have:

\int_{\Lambda}|\psi|^{2}_{1,\star}(x)|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x\leq\int_{\Lambda}\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x\preceq K\left(\int_{\Lambda}\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x\right)(1+|\log s|),

(E.26)

which finally implies, after a direct estimate of the
L^{2}
norm of
\Psi_{1}
(see (
E.8
)), that:

\int|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}-\nabla\hat{h}|^{2}\leq\mathtt{C}Kt^{2}\varepsilon(1+|\log s|)=\mathcal{O}\left(Kt^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\right),

(E.27)

becaus we have chosen
s=\varepsilon^{2}
in (
E.22
). Going back to
[
Ser20
, (A.25)]
, and inserting (
E.27
) we write
19
19

19

Let us observe that the additional term
\mathrm{Err}
appearing in
[
Ser20
, (A.23)]
is
0
in our case because
\Phi
is measure-preserving and thus, with the notation of
[
Ser20
]
,
\nu=\mu
.
:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{Y})-\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})=\mathrm{Main}+\mathrm{Rem}+\mathcal{O}\left(Kt^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\right)

where
\mathrm{Main},\mathrm{Rem}
are given by:

\mathrm{Main}:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\int|\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}|^{2}-\int|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}\right),\quad\mathrm{Rem}:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\int|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{Y}}|^{2}-\int|\nabla\hat{h}|^{2}\right).

E.3.3
The Main term

For the term
\mathrm{Main}
, a direct expansion of
\mathrm{E}_{\vec{\eta}}=\Phi\#\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}
using the definition (
E.20
) gives:

\mathrm{Main}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\left\langle\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}},2\mathsf{D}\psi\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}\right\rangle+\int\mathcal{O}(|\psi|^{2}_{1,\star})|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}.

(E.28)

This is consistent with
[
Ser20
, (A.31)]
, the improvement being that we have no contribution of the form
|\psi|_{L^{\infty}}|\psi|_{C^{2}}
in the second order term thanks to the fact that
\Phi
is measure-preserving. Let us also note that although
\Phi
is measure-preserving, we may not have
\dive\psi=0
, however it is true that
\dive\psi=\mathcal{O}\left(|\psi|_{1,\star}^{2}\right)
pointwise and thus the
-\dive\psi
term appearing in
[
Ser20
, (A.32)]
can be absorbed in our second order correction as in (
E.28
). Arguing as in (
E.26
) we may re-write the error term in (
E.28
) and get:

\mathrm{Main}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\left\langle\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}},2\mathsf{D}\psi\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}\left(Kt^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\right).

(E.29)

In this step we see how the
H^{1}
norm of
\psi
appears as a second-order contribution to the energy change, and since by construction we have
\int|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
we may indeed hope (if
\mathrm{Main}
