is indeed the “main” term) to have a
small
energy cost.

E.3.4
The Rem term

The
\mathrm{Rem}
term is due to the difference between the electric fields
\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{Y}}
(for which charges are spread along circles) and
\nabla\hat{h}
(for which charges are spread along approximate ellipses). The fact that we can choose
small
truncations via the parameter
s
(we which recall to have chosen as
s=\varepsilon^{2}
in (
E.22
), we could even have used an higher power of
\varepsilon
) will turn out to be crucial in order to control those errors. Let us keep the notation of
[
Ser20
]
and use:

•

\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}
to denote the charge spread uniformly on the circle of center
y_{i}=\Phi(x_{i})
and radius
\eta_{i}
,

•

\hat{\delta}_{y_{i}}
to denote the push-forward by
\Phi
of the measure
\delta_{x_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}
.

We also write
v_{i}
for the function

v_{i}:=-\log\star\left(\hat{\delta}_{y_{i}}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}\right).

(E.30)

As in
[
Ser20
, (A. 41)]
we decompose
\mathrm{Rem}
as
\mathrm{Rem}_{1}+\mathrm{Rem}_{2}+\mathrm{Rem}_{3}
and analyse each term separately.

The
\mathrm{Rem}_{1}
term.

\mathrm{Rem}_{1}
is defined as:

\mathrm{Rem}_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\int v_{i}\left(\hat{\delta}_{y_{i}}+\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}\right).

We write as in
[
Ser20
, (A.42)]
(with
\vec{n}
the unit normal vector to the circle)

\mathrm{Rem}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{1}{\eta_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\partial\mathfrak{D}(y_{i},\eta_{i})}\left(\psi(y)-\psi(y_{i})\right)\cdot\vec{n}+\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(y_{i})\right).

Let us use
\Psi_{1}
as an upper bound to the derivative of
\psi
, we have:

\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(y_{i})\preceq\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\left(\Psi_{1}\right)^{2}(x_{i}),

Moreover, a Taylor’s expansion yields:

\psi(y)-\psi(y_{i})=\mathsf{D}\psi(y_{i})(y-y_{i})+\mathcal{O}(|\psi|_{\mathsf{2},\mathrm{loc}}(y_{i})\eta_{i}^{2}),

but since
\Phi
is measure-preserving we know that
\dive\psi(y_{i})=\mathcal{O}(|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\star}(y_{i}))
, thus:

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\frac{1}{\eta_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\partial\mathfrak{D}(y_{i},\eta_{i})}\left(\psi(y)-\psi(y_{i})\right)\cdot\vec{n}=\mathcal{O}(s\Psi_{2}(x_{i})+\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})).

Since
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
have slow variations and since we are working under the
\mathsf{WellSpread}
assumption we may compare sums to integrals as in (
E.15
), hence:

\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\left(\Psi_{1}\right)^{2}(x_{i})+s\Psi_{2}(x_{i})\leq K\int\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x+Kt^{2}s=\mathcal{O}(Kt^{2}\varepsilon).

We thus obtain:

\mathrm{Rem}_{1}=\mathcal{O}(Kt^{2}\varepsilon).

(E.31)

The
\mathrm{Rem}_{2}
term.

\mathrm{Rem}_{2}
is defined as (see (
E.30
))

\mathrm{Rem}_{2}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathsf{n}}\int v_{i}\left(\hat{\delta}_{y_{j}}-\delta_{y_{j}}^{(\eta_{j})}\right).

We write as in
[
Ser20
, (A.45)]
:

\left|\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathsf{n}}\int v_{i}\left(\hat{\delta}_{y_{j}}-\delta_{y_{j}}^{(\eta_{j})}\right)\right|\preceq\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq\mathsf{n}}\eta_{i}^{2}\eta_{j}\left(\frac{|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})}{|y_{i}-y_{j}|^{3}}+\frac{|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})|\psi|_{\mathsf{2},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})}{|y_{i}-y_{j}|^{2}}\right),

and then proceed a bit differently.

First, we use
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
as upper bounds to the derivatives of
\psi
, use (
E.23
) and (
E.10
) in order to replace the
y_{i},y_{j}
’s in the right-hand side by the corresponding
x_{i},x_{j}
’s up to some multiplicative constant. Next, we decompose (as in
[
Ser20
]
) the sum between contributions coming from “close” and “far away” pairs of points.

Distances smaller than
10\ell
.

Since we always have
|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}\geq\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}\mathsf{r}_{j}
and since we take the truncation
\eta_{i}=s\mathsf{r}_{i}
we may write for each fixed
i

\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq 10\ell}\eta_{i}^{2}\eta_{j}\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}+\frac{\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\Psi_{2}(x_{i})}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}\right)\\
\leq s^{3}\left(\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})+\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\Psi_{2}(x_{i})\right)\times\#\left\{j,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq 10\ell\right\}.

Since we condition on
\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}},\ell,K)
we may bound
\#\left\{j,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq 10\ell\right\}
by
\mathtt{C}K\ell^{2}
, and thus:

s^{3}\left(\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})+\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\Psi_{2}(x_{i})\right)\times\#\left\{j,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq 10\ell\right\}\preceq s^{3}K\ell^{2}\left(\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})+\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\Psi_{2}(x_{i})\right).

Next we compare the sum (over
i
) of the previous quantity to an integral using (
E.15
) and get:

\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq 10\ell}\eta_{i}^{2}\eta_{j}\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}+\frac{\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\Psi_{2}(x_{i})}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}\right)\leq\mathtt{C}s^{3}K^{2}\ell^{2}\int\left(\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)+\Psi_{1}(x)\Psi_{2}(x)\right)\mathtt{d}x,

(E.32)

and the right-hand side can be evaluated using (
E.8
)

s^{3}K^{2}\ell^{2}\int\left(\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)+\Psi_{1}(x)\Psi_{2}(x)\right)\mathtt{d}x=\mathcal{O}\left(s^{3}K^{2}\ell^{2}\varepsilon t^{2}\right).

(E.33)

Remark E.11
.

We do lose some information when replacing all distances by the smallest one over a large zone of size
\ell
, but for technical reasons it seemed hard to do much better, and it works well enough for us.

Distances larger than
10\ell
.

The function:
z\mapsto\frac{1}{|z|^{3}}
“varies slowly at scale
\ell
” (in the sense of (
E.13
)) on
\{|z|\geq 10\ell\}
thus using (
E.15
) for each fixed
i
we can compare

\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\geq 10\ell}\frac{1}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}

to
K
times the corresponding integral, namely:

\int_{\{|x-x_{i}|\geq 10\ell\}}\frac{1}{|x_{i}-x|^{3}}\mathtt{d}x=\mathcal{O}(1).

Similarly the sum
\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},|x_{j}-x_{i}|\geq 10\ell}\frac{1}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}
can be compared to:

K\times\int_{\Lambda\cap\{|x-x_{i}|\geq 10\ell\}}\frac{1}{|x_{i}-x|^{2}}\mathtt{d}x=K\times\mathcal{O}(\log T).

Using the obvious bound
\eta_{i}^{2}\eta_{j}\leq s^{3}
we thus obtain:

A:=\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},|x_{j}-x_{i}|\geq 10\ell}\eta_{i}^{2}\eta_{j}\left(\frac{|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}+\frac{|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})|\psi|_{\mathsf{2},X}(x_{i})}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}\right)\\
\preceq s^{3}K\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})+|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})|\psi|_{\mathsf{2},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})\log T.

Using again
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
instead and comparing again the sum to an integral, we obtain:

A\preceq s^{3}K^{2}\left(\int\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)+\log T\Psi_{1}(x)\Psi_{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x\right),

(E.34)

which can be evaluated using (
E.8
) (and (
6.2
)):

A\preceq s^{3}K^{2}\left(\int\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)+\log T\Psi_{1}(x)\Psi_{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x\right)\preceq s^{3}K^{2}\left(\varepsilon t^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}\log T\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(s^{3}K^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}\right)

(E.35)

Conclusion for
\mathrm{Rem}_{2}
.
Combining (
E.33
) and (
E.35
) and discarding negligible terms we get:

\mathrm{Rem}_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(s^{3}K^{2}\ell^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(K^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}\right),

(E.36)

where we have used (
6.2
) to simplify the expression.

The
\mathrm{Rem}_{3}
term.

\mathrm{Rem}_{3}
is defined as (
\mathbf{m}
is outside of the sum over
j
.):

\mathrm{Rem}_{3}:=-\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}}\int_{\Lambda}v_{i}\left(\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i}\hat{\delta}_{y_{j}}-\mathbf{m}\right).

Writing
\hat{\delta}_{y_{j}}-\mathbf{m}=\hat{\delta}_{y_{j}}-\delta_{y_{j}}^{(\eta_{j})}+\delta_{y_{j}}^{(\eta_{j})}-\mathbf{m}
and recalling that
v_{i}:=-\log\star\left(\hat{\delta}_{y_{i}}-\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}\right)
, one can express
\mathrm{Rem}_{3}
(as in
[
Ser20
, Substep (5.3)]
) as:

\mathrm{Rem}_{3}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}}\int\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}\left(\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}-\hat{\delta}_{y_{i}}\right).

We recall that the truncated field
\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}
coincides with
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
inside the
i^{th}
spread out charge.

The analysis of
[
Ser20
, Step 4.]
shows that, for each
i
we have, as in
[
Ser20
, Substep 5.3]
:

\int\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}\left(\delta_{y_{i}}^{(\eta_{i})}-\hat{\delta}_{y_{i}}\right)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u\\
+\mathcal{O}\left(|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}|_{C^{2}(B(y_{i},2\eta_{i}))}\eta_{i}^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})\right).

(E.37)

Here and below we temporarily borrow the notation of
[
Ser20
]
for local controls on derivatives of
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}
, namely:

|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}|_{C^{\mathsf{k}}(B(x,r))}=L^{\infty}\text{ norm of the $\mathsf{k}$-th derivative of $\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}$ over the ball/disk of center $x$ and radius $r$}.

We cannot use (
E.37
) as such because of the various dependencies in
\mathrm{Y}
, which we need to analyse.

Step 1: Preliminary claims

Claim E.12
.

[Variation of the first derivatives]
For all
a\in\{1,2\}
,
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}
and
|u|\leq\eta_{i}
we have:

\left|\partial_{a}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\partial_{a}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\right|\preceq K\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\ell^{2}+|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\log T\right)

Proof of Claim
E.12
.

We have by definition:

\partial_{a}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\partial_{a}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i}\partial_{a}(-\log)(y_{j}-(y_{i}+u))-\partial_{a}(-\log)(x_{j}-(x_{i}+u))\\
-\partial_{a}\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}(y_{i}+u)+\partial_{a}\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}(x_{i}+u),

where
\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}
is the logarithmic potential generated by the background measure on
\Lambda
, whose expression is given in (
E.19
). Since
y_{i}=x_{i}+\psi(x_{i})
the difference
\partial_{a}\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}(y_{i}+u)-\partial_{a}\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}(x_{i}+u)
is easily bounded by
|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}
. We now focus on the contribution coming from the point particles. We have:

\left|\partial_{a}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\partial_{a}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\right|\leq\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i}\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}.

Let us split the sum into two parts corresponding to “close” and “far away” pairs of points.

Distances smaller than
10\ell
.

On the one hand we have:

\sum_{j,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq 10\ell,j\neq i}\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}\preceq\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\times\#\left\{j,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq 10\ell\right\}\preceq K\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\ell^{2}.

To be precise, here we have used a mean value argument to argue that:

\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}\leq\sup_{x\in[x_{i},x_{j}]}|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x),

then we wrote
|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x)\leq\Psi_{1}(x)
and finally we used (
E.9
). Then we applied the local control on the number of points implied by
\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}},\ell,K)
.

Distances larger than
10\ell
.

On the other hand:

\sum_{|x_{j}-x_{i}|\geq 10\ell}\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}\preceq K|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\int_{|x-x_{i}|\geq 10\ell,x\in\Lambda}\frac{1}{|x-x_{i}|^{2}}\mathtt{d}x\preceq K|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\log T.

This time we simply bounded
|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|
by
2|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}
and used the fact that
z\mapsto\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}
“varies slowly at scale
\ell
” (in the sense of (
E.13
)) on
\{|z|\geq 10\ell\}
in order to compare the sum to an integral as in (
E.15
).

Combining those estimates proves the claim.
∎

Claim E.13

(Variation of the second derivatives)
.

For all
a,b\in\{1,2\}
,
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}
and
|u|\leq\eta_{i}
we have:

\left|\partial_{ab}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\partial_{ab}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\right|\preceq K\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}}\ell^{2}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})+\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\log T+|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\frac{1}{\ell+|x_{i}|}\right).

Proof of Claim
E.13
.

The proof is similar to Claim
E.12
. We have by definition:

\partial_{ab}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\partial_{ab}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(x_{i}+u)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i}\partial_{ab}(-\log)(y_{j}-(y_{i}+u))-\partial_{ab}(-\log)(x_{j}-(x_{i}+u))\\
-\partial_{ab}\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}(y_{i}+u)+\partial_{ab}\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}(x_{i}+u),

however the second derivatives of
\mathrm{V}_{\Lambda}
are
constant
(see (
E.19
)) so we can discard those terms.
Writing
y=\Phi(x)=x+\psi(x)
, we get:

\left|\partial_{ab}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\partial_{ab}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\right|\leq\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i}\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}.

We now split the sum into three parts:
|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq\ell
,
|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}|
and
|x_{j}-x_{i}|\geq\left(\ell\cup\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}|\right)
.

Distances smaller than
10\ell
.

Arguing as in the proof of Claim
E.12
we get:

\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i}\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}\preceq K\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}}\ell^{2}\Psi_{1}(x_{i}).

Distances between
10\ell
and
\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}|
.

For
10\ell\leq|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}|
we write by a mean value argument:

\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}\leq\sup_{x\in[x_{i},x_{j}]}|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x)\times\frac{1}{|x_{j}-x_{i}|^{2}},

then we may again replace
|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\star}(x)
by
\Psi_{1}(x)
and use property (
E.10
) to bound it by
\Psi_{1}(x_{i})
up to some multiplicative constant. Next, comparing a sum to an integral, we have:

\sum_{j,|x_{j}-x_{i}|\geq 10\ell}\frac{1}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}\preceq K\log T.

In conclusion, we get for fixed
i
:

\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},j\neq i,10\ell\leq|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}|}\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}\leq K\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\log T.

Large distances.

To estimate the remaining contribution, we write:

\sum_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{n},|x_{j}-x_{i}|\geq\max(10\ell,\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}|)}\frac{|\psi(x_{i})-\psi(x_{j})|}{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{3}}\preceq K|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\int_{|x-x_{i}|\geq\max(10\ell,\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}|)}\frac{1}{|x-x_{i}|^{3}}\mathtt{d}x\preceq K|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\frac{1}{|x_{i}|+\ell},

where we compared the sum to an integral using (
E.15
).

Combining all three estimates proves the claim.
∎

Remark E.14
.

In Claims
E.12
and
E.13
we have studied the contributions coming from the point particles and the background separately. Taking cancellations between those terms into account would yield more accurate estimates, but we do not need them here.

Step 2: Studying the first-order term.

Let us recall that we are still trying to express
\mathrm{Rem}_{3}
purely in terms of the original points
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\mathsf{n}})
.

Going back to (
E.37
), let us write the first-order term as:

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u\\
+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\right)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u.

We keep the first term in the right-hand side as such and we focus on the second one, which we decompose as:

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\right)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u\\
=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i})-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i})\right)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u\\
+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\left(\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\right)-\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i})-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i})\right)\right)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u.

(E.38)

Let us also write that
\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})=\mathsf{D}\psi(x_{i})\times u+\mathcal{O}\left(|\psi|_{\mathsf{2},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})\eta_{i}^{2}\right)
, and observe that according to Claim
E.13
we have (for
|u|=\eta_{i}
)

\left|\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\right)-\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i})-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i})\right)\right|\preceq\eta_{i}\times K\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}}\ell^{2}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})+\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\log T+|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\frac{1}{\ell+|x_{i}|}\right).

We may thus re-write the right-hand side of (
E.38
) as:

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i})-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i})\right)\cdot\mathsf{D}\psi(x_{i})u\mathtt{d}u+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\left(\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i})-\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i})\right)\times\mathcal{O}\left(|\psi|_{\mathsf{2},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})\eta_{i}^{2}\right)\mathtt{d}u\\
+\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{i}\times K\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}}\ell^{2}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})+\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\log T+|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\frac{1}{\ell+|x_{i}|}\right)\right)\times|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})\times\eta_{i}.

The first term vanishes by symmetry, and we can bound the second term further using Claim
E.12
. In conclusion, we obtain (using that
\eta_{i}\leq s\mathsf{r}_{i}\leq s
):

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}(y_{i}+u)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u\\
+K\left(s^{2}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\Psi_{2}(x_{i})\ell^{2}+s^{2}|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\Psi_{2}(x_{i})\log T+s^{2}\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})\left(\ell^{2}+\log T\right)+s^{2}|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\frac{1}{\ell+|x_{i}|}\right).

(E.39)

Summing the error term in (
E.39
) over
i
yields:

\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}K\left(s^{2}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\Psi_{2}(x_{i})\ell^{2}+s^{2}|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\Psi_{2}(x_{i})\log T+s^{2}\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})\left(\ell^{2}+\log T\right)+s^{2}|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\frac{1}{\ell+|x_{i}|}\right)\\
\leq K^{2}\left(s^{2}\ell^{2}\int\Psi_{1}(x)\Psi_{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x+s^{2}|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\log T\int\Psi_{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x+s^{2}\left(\ell^{2}+\log T\right)\int\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x+s^{2}|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\int\Psi_{1}(x)\frac{1}{\ell+|x|}\mathtt{d}x\right)\\
\leq K^{2}\left(s^{2}\ell^{2}\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}+s^{2}t^{2}\log T+s^{2}\left(\ell^{2}+\log T\right)\varepsilon t^{2}+s^{2}t^{2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(K^{2}\varepsilon t^{2}\right),

(E.40)

where we have used (
6.2
) to simplify the expression involving
T,\ell,\varepsilon
and
s
(we recall that
s=\varepsilon^{2}
).

Step 3: Re-writing the error term.
The error term in (
E.37
) involves
|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}|_{C^{2}(B(y_{i},2\eta_{i}))}
, which is expressed in terms of the
transported
points and thus remains an issue for us. Using Claim
E.13
however, we see that:

|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}|_{C^{2}(B(y_{i},2\eta_{i}))}\leq|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}|_{C^{2}(B(x_{i},2\eta_{i}))}+K\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}}\ell^{2}|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})+|\psi|_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})\log T+|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\frac{1}{\ell+|x_{i}|}\right).

(E.41)

Now, the analysis of
[
Ser20
, Lemma A.2]
gives:

|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}|_{C^{2}(B(x_{i},2\eta_{i}))}\preceq\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}}\left(1+\int_{\mathfrak{D}(x_{i},\mathsf{r}_{i})}|\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}\right).

(E.42)

Remark E.15
.

The proof of (
E.42
) uses the fact that
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}
is almost harmonic on the disk
\mathfrak{D}(x_{i},\mathsf{r}_{i})
, and is simplified by the fact that the background measure (
\mu
in the notation of
[
Ser20
]
) is here constant (beware: when reading
[
Ser20
, (A.5), (A.6)]
, our background measure corresponds to
N\mu
and not
\mu
- compare
[
Ser20
, (3.1)]
with our Definition
2.1
. Also, the nearest-neighbor distances in
[
Ser20
]
are of order
N^{-1/2}
where ours are of order one, this is due to a different choice of scaling, but
[
Ser20
, Lemma A.2]
is valid in any case).

Combining (
E.41
) and (
E.42
) the error term appearing in (
E.37
) can be re-written as:

|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}|_{C^{2}(B(y_{i},2\eta_{i}))}\eta_{i}^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})\preceq\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})\frac{\eta_{i}^{2}}{\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}}\left(1+\int_{\mathfrak{D}(x_{i},\mathsf{r}_{i})}|\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}|^{2}\right)\\
+K\Psi_{1}^{2}(x_{i})\eta_{i}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}_{i}^{2}}\ell^{2}\Psi_{1}(x_{i})+\Psi_{1}(x_{i})\log T+|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\frac{1}{\ell+|x_{i}|}\right),

Summing over
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}
, using the
\mathsf{WellSpread}
assumption, comparing to an integral and using again that
\eta_{i}\leq s\mathsf{r}_{i}
we obtain:

\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}|_{C^{2}(B(y_{i},2\eta_{i}))}\eta_{i}^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})\\
\preceq K(1+|\log s|)s^{2}\int\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)\mathtt{d}x+K^{2}s^{2}\left(\left(\ell^{2}+\log T\right)\int_{\Lambda}\Psi_{1}^{3}(x)\mathtt{d}x+|\psi|_{\mathsf{0}}\int_{\Lambda}\Psi_{1}^{2}(x)\frac{1}{\ell+|x|}\mathtt{d}x\right)

(E.43)

Estimating everything explicitly and keeping only the dominant term we may thus control the error term in (
E.37
) by:

\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{Y}}|_{C^{2}(B(y_{i},2\eta_{i}))}\eta_{i}^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1},\mathrm{loc}}(x_{i})=\mathcal{O}\left(K^{2}(1+\log|s|)s^{2}\ell^{2}\varepsilon t^{2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(K^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}\right),

(E.44)

where we have used that
s=\varepsilon^{2}
and (
6.2
) again.

Step 4: Conclusion for
\mathrm{Rem}_{3}
.

In conclusion we obtained that:

\mathrm{Rem}_{3}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u+\mathcal{O}\left(K^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}\right).

(E.45)

E.3.5
Concluding the proof of Proposition
E.7

Combining all the previous steps, we find that:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi\cdot\mathbf{X})=\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X})+\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}(\mathbf{X},\psi)+\mathcal{O}\left(K^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}\right),

where the “anisotropy” term
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
is defined as the sum of the linear (in
\psi
) terms obtained in
\mathrm{Main}
and
\mathrm{Rem}_{3}
, namely:

\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}(\mathbf{X},\psi):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\left\langle\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}},2\mathsf{D}\psi\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{\vec{\eta}}^{\mathrm{X}}\right\rangle+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{n}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{|u|=\eta_{i}}\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\mathrm{X}}(x_{i}+u)\cdot\left(\psi(x_{i}+u)-\psi(x_{i})\right)\mathtt{d}u.

(E.46)

There was a linear term appearing in
\mathrm{Rem}_{1}
but it was found to be negligible, and comparing (
E.46
) to
[
Ser20
, (4.8)]
, the reader might observe that there is another term missing (the last term in
[
Ser20
, (4.8)]
), in fact for us this term is
\mathcal{O}\left(K\varepsilon t^{2}\right)
and can thus be incorporated in the error term. This is due to the fact that our
\Phi
is measure-preserving.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
E.7
.
∎

E.4
Smallness of the anisotropy

Applying the result of Proposition
E.7
to
\Phi_{t}
and
\Phi_{-t}
we obtain that if
\mathbf{X}
is in
\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}},\ell,K)
we have:

\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{t}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{-t}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})\right)-\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}[\mathbf{X},\psi_{t}+\psi_{-t}]+\mathcal{O}\left(K^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}\right).

(E.47)

Let us decompose
\psi_{t},\psi_{-t}
as in (
E.1
), (
E.2
). The terms of first order in
t
cancel each other, and it remains to bound
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}[\mathbf{X},\gamma_{t}+\gamma_{-t}]
. At this point, using the “rough” bounds of
[
AS21
, Prop. 4.2]
on the anisotropy
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
, even in a localized way, would yield a bounded,
but not small
, error term - which would make the whole approach pointless. We thus need to rely on a finer understanding of anisotropy terms as put forward in
[
LS18
,
Ser20
]
(see also
[
BBNY19
]
for similar concerns about their “angle term”).

For simplicity, we will focus on the context of interest for us, namely when the background measure
\mathbf{m}
is given by
\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
as above - in particular it is constant on
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
.

Anisotropy.

Let
\psi
be a continuous vector field supported on
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
and let
U
be a neighborhood of
\supp\psi
. We define
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi)
as:

\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi):=\iint_{(x,y)\in\Lambda\times\Lambda,x\neq y}\psi(x)\cdot\nabla\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}_{n}-\mathbf{m}\right)(x)\mathtt{d}\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{m}\right)(y).

(E.48)

(It has an alternative expression using the electric field, which is the one that we used above in (
E.46
), see
[
Ser20
, (4.14)]
). The expression (
E.48
) makes sense because
\mathbf{m}
has a continuous density near
\supp\psi
and is such that
\iint-\log|x-y|\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}(y)
is finite. Thanks to a clever integration by parts, one can control
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
as follows (see
[
Ser20
, (4.10)]
):

\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi)\leq\mathtt{C}|\psi|_{\mathsf{1}}\times\left(\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X},\supp\psi)\right).

(E.49)

If
\psi
lives on a disk of radius
\ell
, if
|\psi|_{\mathsf{1}}=\mathcal{O}(\ell^{-2})
and if local laws hold then we can expect the anisotropy to typically be
\mathcal{O}(1)
. Let us now explain how
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
shows up in the computations.

Energy comparison.

Assume that
\psi
is a vector field of class
C^{3}
, supported on a disk of radius
\ell
within
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
, and such that:

|\psi|_{\mathsf{k}}\leq\mathtt{C}_{\psi}\ell^{-\mathsf{k}-1},\text{ for }\mathsf{k}=1,2,3.

(E.50)

Let
\tau
be a real parameter such that (for some universal
\mathtt{C}
large enough):

|\tau||\psi|_{\mathsf{1}}\leq\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}},\quad|\tau||\psi|_{\mathsf{2}}\leq\log\ell\frac{1}{\mathtt{C}}

(E.51)

For all
\tau
such that (
E.51
) is satisfied, let
\Phi_{\tau}:=\Id+\tau\psi
and
\mathbf{m}_{\tau}:=\Phi_{\tau}\#\mathbf{m}
.

Lemma E.16
.

We have the following expansion for all
\mathbf{X}

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{\tau}\cdot\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m}_{\tau})=\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m})+\tau\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi)+\tau^{2}\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}(\mathbf{X},\psi),

(E.52)

where
\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}(\mathbf{X},\psi)
is controlled by:

\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}(\mathbf{X},\psi)\leq\mathtt{C}_{\psi}^{2}\frac{\log\ell}{\ell^{4}}\mathsf{EnerPts}(\mathbf{X},\supp\psi),

(E.53)

the energy being computed with
\mathbf{m}
as neutralizing background.

Proof of Lemma
E.16
.

This follows from the second-order expansion of the energy as found in
[
Ser20
, Lemma 4.1, Prop 4.2]
. There is some care required in order to check that
[
Ser20
, (4.11)]
does indeed yield the claimed second-order correction, but this is made simpler by our assumption (
E.50
) and the fact that
\mathbf{m}
is constant on
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
.
∎

Since
\supp\psi
has volume
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{2})
, in view of the local laws we expect
\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}
to be
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{-2}\log\ell)
(with a constant depending on
\mathtt{C}_{\psi}
). The anisotropy is thus the first-order contribution to the energy change induced by a transport which is a small perturbation of the identity map.

\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
versus
\mathsf{Ani}
.

What is called the “anisotropy” in
[
LS18
]
and
[
Ser20
]
is not exactly the same term, in fact
[
LS18
]
refers to
\mathsf{Ani}
and
[
Ser20
]
to
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
, where the latter is as defined above and the former corresponds to:

\mathsf{Ani}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi):=\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi)-\frac{1}{4}\int\Div\psi(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x).

(E.54)

So in fact “
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
” contains a possibly non-vanishing contribution
\frac{1}{4}\int\Div\psi(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}(x)\approx\frac{1}{4}\int\Div\psi(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}(x)
which we need to substract in order to obtain “
\mathsf{Ani}
” which is the term that will eventually be found to be negligible. The term appearing in the energy expansion (
E.52
) is
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
.

Both
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}
and
\mathsf{Ani}
satisfy the same control (
E.49
). In most relevant cases,
\psi
happens to be such that
|\psi|_{\mathsf{1}}\times|\supp\psi|=\mathcal{O}(1)
. Since (by local laws) the electric energy is typically proportional to the volume, we deduce that (for such “usual”
\psi
’s)
the anisotropy
\mathsf{Ani}
is (at most) of order
1
. Analytically speaking, i.e. as far as deterministic, function-wise bounds are concerned, it is very challenging to do better. However, a key result underlying some of the recent progress in the study of 2DOCP’s is that
\mathsf{Ani}
is, so to speak,
often smaller than it seems
. This is a
probabilistic
statement found in
[
LS18
, Corollary 4.4]
,
[
Ser20
, Lemma 7.2.]
, see also (with a different formalism)
[
BBNY19
, Section 8.5]
. Let us sketch the proof of this fact.

Smallness of the anisotropy via “Serfaty’s trick”.

Let
\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau})
be the partition function associated to the background
\mathbf{m}_{\tau}
(we keep the same “effective confinement”
\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}
for all
\tau
) namely:

\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau}):=\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}_{\tau})+\mathsf{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x_{i})\right)\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}.

The key point is that there are two ways to evaluate the ratio
\frac{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau})}{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m})}
:

1.

By the transportation approach of
[
LS18
,
Ser20
]
, involving a change of variables
(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\left(\Phi_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,\Phi_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{N})\right)
in the very definition of
\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau})
and an analysis of its effect on the energy. As seen in Lemma
E.16
, the anisotropy of
\psi
appears there as one of the contributions.

2.

By using “free energy expansions”, i.e. explicit expressions of (the logarithm of) the two partition functions up to some error term that has to be negligible. This was done in
[
LS18
]
with a non-quantitative error term originating in the analysis of
[
LS17
]
, in
[
BBNY19
]
using their own expansion, and much improved in
[
Ser20
]
using the error bounds of
[
AS21
]
.

This gives two expressions for the same quantity, and since the anisotropy appears only (in exponential moments) in the first one, then it must be confounded with some error terms of the second one. This is fruitful for
\tau
large (but not too large), and thus also for smaller values of
\tau
by Hölder’s inequality.

This “trick”, which yields a form of “smallness of the anisotropy”, is used in
[
LS18
,
BBNY19
,
Ser20
]
as a tool to prove central limit theorems for fluctuations of smooth linear statistics. Unfortunately, it is hard to pinpoint a clear general statement in the literature, so we recall the proof in the next paragraphs. Recall that we take
\mathbf{m}=\mathsf{n}\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
as background measure, which has constant density
1
in the bulk.

1. Comparison along a transport.

Here for technical reasons we need to work with partition functions restricted to
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
, and we write:

\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}):=\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}})\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}_{\tau})+\mathsf{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x_{i})\right)\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}}.

We have the following “comparison of partition functions”:

Claim E.17
.

\log\frac{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})}{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})}=\left(\frac{\beta}{4}-1\right)\left(\int\log\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}_{\tau}-\int\log\mathbf{m}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}\right)\\
+\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\tau\mathsf{Ani}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi]+\left(\tau|\psi|_{\mathsf{2}}+\tau^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1}}\right)\mathsf{EnerPts}(\supp\psi)+\tau^{2}\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}\right)\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right].

(E.55)

Proof of Claim
E.17
.

We follow the same steps as in
[
LS18
, Prop 4.3]
. First we write
\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})
as:

\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})=\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{Y}_{\mathsf{n}})\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{Y}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}_{\tau})+\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{Y}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\right)\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{Y}_{\mathsf{n}}

and perform the change of variables
\mathbf{Y}_{\mathsf{n}}=\Phi_{\tau}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}
. By construction we have
\Phi_{\tau}=\Id
outside
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
and in particular the term
\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{Y}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)
(which only detects points outside the support of
\mu_{\mathrm{W}}
) is not affected by this, nor is the indicator
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
which only cares about points very close to
\partial\Lambda
. We obtain:

\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})\\
=\int_{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\Phi_{\tau}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\Phi_{\tau}\#\mathbf{m}\right)+\mathsf{n}\int\zeta_{\mathrm{W}}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\right)+\int\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{\tau}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)\right)\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}_{\mathsf{n}},

(E.56)

where the last term in the integrand is the Jacobian of the tranformation. Using (
E.52
) we may compare the energy before and after the transport.

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\Phi_{\tau}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\Phi_{\tau}\#\mathbf{m}\right)=\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}\right)+\tau\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{1}}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi]+\tau^{2}\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans},

which we can re-write (using (
E.54
)) as:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\Phi_{\tau}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\Phi_{\tau}\#\mathbf{m}\right)=\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}\right)+\frac{\tau}{4}\int\Div\psi(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)+\tau\mathsf{Ani}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m},\psi]+\tau^{2}\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}.

The terms
\int\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{\tau}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)
and
\tau\int\Div\psi(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)
are both equal to a deterministic quantity up to small error terms. On the one hand, we have:

\int\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{\tau}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)=\int\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{\tau}(x)\mathtt{d}x+\mathrm{Fluct}[\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{\tau}]

and on the other hand:
\tau\int\Div\psi(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)=\int\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{\tau}(x)\mathtt{d}\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}}(x)+\tau^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}|\psi(x_{i})|^{2}_{\mathsf{1}}
.
The quantity
\int\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{s}(x)\mathtt{d}x
coincides (see.
[
LS18
, (4.11)–(4.13)]
) with:

\int\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{s}(x)\mathtt{d}x=\int\log\mathbf{m}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}-\int\log\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}_{\tau},

(E.57)

hence we obtain:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\Phi_{\tau}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\Phi_{\tau}\#\mathbf{m}\right)=\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(\int\log\mathbf{m}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}-\int\log\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\right)\\
+\mathrm{Fluct}[\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{\tau}]+\tau^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}|\psi(x_{i})|^{2}_{\mathsf{1}}+\tau^{2}\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}.

Using Lemma
2.6
we can control:
\mathrm{Fluct}[\log\det\mathsf{D}\Phi_{\tau}]\preceq\tau|\psi|_{\mathsf{2}}\mathsf{EnerPts}(\supp\psi)
and on the other hand we have:
\tau^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}|\psi(x_{i})|^{2}_{\mathsf{1}}\leq\tau^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1}}\mathsf{EnerPts}(\supp\psi)
, thus we can write:

\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\Phi_{\tau}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\Phi_{\tau}\#\mathbf{m}\right)=\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m}\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(\int\log\mathbf{m}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}-\int\log\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}_{\tau}\right)\\
+\left(\tau|\psi|_{\mathsf{2}}+\tau^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1}}\right)\mathsf{EnerPts}(\supp\psi)+\tau^{2}\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans},

and inserting this in (
E.56
) yields (
E.55
).
∎

Remark E.18
.

Each term in the right-hand side of (
E.57
) might be infinite when taken separately (because e.g.
\mathbf{m}
may have a singularity on
\partial\Lambda
and hence infinite entropy) but the difference makes sense as the two measures coincide outside
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
.

Free energy comparisons

As we have seen above, one can compare two partition functions using a “transportation” approach. On the other hand, we have the following.

Claim E.19

(Free energy comparison, the “direct” approach)
.

Assume that the support of
\tilde{\mathbf{m}}-\mathbf{m}
is contained in a square
\Omega
of sidelength
\hat{\ell}
included in
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
.

\left|\log\frac{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})}{\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})}\right|=\left(\frac{\beta}{4}-1\right)\left(\int\log\tilde{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{d}\tilde{\mathbf{m}}-\int\log\mathbf{m}\mathtt{d}\mathbf{m}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\hat{\ell}\log\hat{\ell}\right)

(E.58)

Proof of Claim
E.19
.

This is essentially the result of
[
Ser20
, Proposition 6.4]
, except that our reference measure does not necessarily have a
C^{1}
density near the edge of
\Lambda
. This is in fact not a problem as long as we are doing comparisons inside
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
(i.e. as long as the other measure coincides with
\mathbf{m}
outside
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
), but it requires an explanation.

The proof of
[
Ser20
, Proposition 6.4]
relies on two ingredients:

1.

[
Ser20
, Proposition 6.3]
(free energy expansion for general density in a rectangle). This we can import directly as it has nothing to do with our specific setup.

2.

[
Ser20
, Proposition 3.6]
(almost additivity of the free energy). It says that one can decompose
\mathrm{K}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}(\mathbf{m})
into two parts: inside/outside
\Omega
, with a small error. This is proven by two inequalities: one is easy and corresponds to the sub-additivity of Neumann energies whereas the other one uses the screening procedure and the local laws on (a neighborhood of)
\Omega
in order to control the screening error terms (see
[
Ser20
, Prop. 3.6]
). Since the screening procedure takes place in a neighborhood of
\Omega
, the possible singularities near
\partial\Lambda
are irrelevant. The only adaptation needed is to replace the local laws used in
[
Ser20
]
by ours (which is the reason why we “condition” on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
).

∎

Conclusion 1: smallness of
\mathsf{Ani}

We may now apply “Serfaty’s trick”. Comparing the statements of Claim
E.17
and Claim
E.19
we see that necessarily:

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\tau\mathsf{Ani}[\psi,\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m}]+\left(\tau|\psi|_{\mathsf{2}}+\tau^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1}}\right)\mathsf{EnerPts}(\supp\psi)+\tau^{2}\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}\right)\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\hat{\ell}\log\hat{\ell}\right).

Using the local laws and our assumptions (
E.50
) on
\psi
we may control the exponential moments of the error terms:

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\left(\tau|\psi|_{\mathsf{2}}+\tau^{2}|\psi|^{2}_{\mathsf{1}}\right)\mathsf{EnerPts}(\supp\psi)+\tau^{2}\mathrm{ErrEnerTrans}\right)\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\\
=\mathcal{O}\left(\tau\mathtt{C}_{\psi}\hat{\ell}^{-1}+\tau^{2}\mathtt{C}_{\psi}^{2}\hat{\ell}^{-2}+\mathtt{C}_{\psi}^{2}\tau^{2}\hat{\ell}^{-2}\log\hat{\ell}\right).

This is valid for all
\tau
smaller than
\frac{\hat{\ell}^{2}}{\mathtt{C}\mathtt{C}_{\psi}\log\hat{\ell}}
so that (
E.51
) are satisfied. Taking
\tau=\hat{\ell}^{3/2}
, we obtain the following statement:

Lemma E.20

(“The anisotropy is small”)
.

If
\psi
is a
C^{3}
vector field compactly supported on a disk of radius
\hat{\ell}
within
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
, and satisfying (
E.50
), then we have, for all
\hat{\ell}
large enough (depending on the constant
\mathtt{C}_{\psi}
)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\exp\left(\hat{\ell}^{3/2}\mathsf{Ani}(\psi,\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m})\right)\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq e^{\mathcal{O}(\hat{\ell}\log\hat{\ell})+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathtt{C}_{\psi}^{2}\hat{\ell}\log\hat{\ell}\right)},

(E.59)

with an implicit constant depending on
\beta
and the “local laws” constant
C

Local

. In particular, we have the following tail estimate on the distribution of
\mathsf{Ani}(\psi)
: for
\hat{\ell}
large enough,

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[|\mathsf{Ani}(\psi,\mathbf{X}_{\mathsf{n}},\mathbf{m})|\geq\frac{\log^{2}\hat{\ell}}{\hat{\ell}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\hat{\ell}\log^{2}\hat{\ell}\right).

(E.60)

Remark E.21
.

The same analysis holds for the full system, with no need for a conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}
and as soon as (
2.8
) is satisfied, so for a broader notion of “bulk”.

Conclusion 2: proof of Proposition
6.3

We may now conclude the proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Let us return to (
E.47
) and use the fact that by definition (see (
E.2
)):

\psi_{t}=t\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}(x)+\gamma_{t}(x),\quad\psi_{-t}=-t\mathtt{W}^{(\varepsilon,\ell)}(x)+\gamma_{-t}(x),

we obtain (for
\mathbf{X}
in
\mathsf{WellSpread}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}},\ell,K)
and thus with probability
1-\exp(-\ell^{2})
up to choosing
K
large enough as in Lemma
E.6
):

\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{t}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{-t}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})\right)-\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{Ani}[\mathbf{X},\gamma_{t}+\gamma_{-t}]+\mathcal{O}(K^{2}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}).

(E.61)

Let us introduce dyadic length scales
\ell_{i}:=2^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq\log(T/2)
and associated cut-off functions
\chi_{i}
. We decompose
\gamma_{t}
as:
\gamma_{t}=\sum_{i}\chi_{i}\gamma_{t}
. Using Proposition
E.1
and in particular (
E.4
) we see that the vector field
\tilde{\psi}^{(i)}:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon t^{2}}\chi_{i}\gamma_{t}
satisfies
|\tilde{\psi}^{(i)}|_{\mathsf{k}}\preceq\ell_{i}^{-\mathsf{k}-1}
. Using Lemma
E.20
, we know that for each
i
large enough:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[|\mathsf{Ani}(\tilde{\psi}^{(i)})|\geq\frac{\log^{2}\ell_{i}}{\ell_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\ell_{i}\log^{2}\ell_{i}\right).

Since this bound is only interesting (probabilistically speaking) for large enough
i
, we use it for
i\geq\log^{2}\ell
, in which case we have:

\sum_{i=\log^{2}\ell}^{\log(T/2)}\mathsf{Ani}(\tilde{\psi}^{(i)})\leq\sum_{i=\log^{2}\ell}^{\log(T/2)}\frac{\log^{2}\ell_{i}}{\ell_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon t^{2})

with probability
\geq 1-\sum_{i=\log^{2}\ell}^{\log(T/2)}\exp\left(-\ell_{i}\log^{2}\ell_{i}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\log^{2}\ell\right)
. The first contributions (for
0\leq i\leq\log^{2}\ell
) are controlled using the “rougher” control (
E.49
), we get:

\sum_{i=0}^{\log^{2}\ell}\mathsf{Ani}(\tilde{\psi}^{(i)})=\mathcal{O}\left(\log\ell^{2}\right).

By (
6.2
) we know that
\log\ell^{2}
is comparable to
\log\varepsilon
. In conclusion, we obtain:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(|\mathsf{Ani}[\mathbf{X},\gamma_{t}+\gamma_{-t}]|\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon\log\varepsilon t^{2}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\ell^{2}\right),

(E.62)

with a constant
\mathtt{C}
depending on
\beta
and the constant
C

Local

.

Combined with (
E.61
) which is valid under an event of comparable probability (see Lemma
E.6
), we conclude the proof of Proposition
6.3
.
∎

E.5
Effect on expectations: proof of Proposition
6.4

Proof of Proposition
6.4
.

For
\tau\in(0,1)
, let us introduce the event
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
as:

\mathcal{F}_{\tau}:=\left\{\mathrm{ErrAve}\leq\tau\right\}

(E.63)

(recall the definition (
6.3
) of
\mathrm{ErrAve}
). We split the proof into several steps.

The case of non-negative functions

In this paragraph, we make the additional assumption that
\mathsf{G}

is non-negative
. We can obviously decompose
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}]
as:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}\big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\mathtt{1}_{\overline{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}}\big|\mathcal{E}\right].

(E.64)

Claim E.22
.

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\Phi_{t}\cdot\mathbf{X})+\mathsf{G}(\Phi_{-t}\cdot\mathbf{X})\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]\times e^{\beta\tau}.

(E.65)

Proof of Claim
E.22
.

According to the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}
in Section
5.2
, the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}]
admits the following expression:

\frac{\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}(\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\zeta(x_{i})\right)}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}}{\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\zeta(x_{i})\right)}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}}.

Since the function
\zeta
vanishes identically on
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
and since our localized translations act as the identity outside
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
, the term
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\zeta(x_{i})
will not be affected by the operations below. For simplicity, we omit it altogether. Let us focus on the integral appearing in the numerator. By definition of
\mathrm{ErrAve}
and of
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
as in (
6.3
), (
E.63
), we may write:

\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}=\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{t}\cdot\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{-t}\cdot\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})\right)+\beta\mathrm{ErrAve}}\leq e^{\beta\tau}e^{-\beta\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{t}\cdot\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})+\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{-t}\cdot\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})\right)},

where we haved bounded the indicator function by
1
in the right-hand side. Using the convexity of
x\mapsto\exp(-\beta x)
and the fact that
\mathsf{G}
is assumed to be non-negative, we deduce that:

\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}(\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}\\
\leq\frac{1}{2}\left[\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{t}\cdot\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}+\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{t}\cdot\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}\right]\times e^{\beta\tau}.

By construction, the change of variable
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\mathsf{n}})\mapsto(\Phi_{t}(x_{1}),\dots,\Phi_{t}(x_{\mathsf{n}}))
, which maps
\mathbf{X}
to
\Phi_{t}\mathbf{X}
, has a Jacobian equal to
1
. We thus have, looking at the first integral on the right-hand side:

\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\Phi_{t}\cdot\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}=\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\Phi_{-t}\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\Phi_{-t}\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}.

Moreover, since
\Phi_{-t}\equiv\Id-t\vec{u}
on the disk
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/4)
(see Lemma
6.2
), since
\mathsf{G}
is assumed to be
\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/10)
-local, and since
|t|
is taken smaller than
\ell/10
, we have
\Phi_{-t}\left(\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/10)\right)\subset\mathfrak{D}(0,\ell/4)
and thus:

\mathsf{G}(\Phi_{-t}(\mathbf{X}))=\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u}).

On the other hand since by construction
\Phi_{t}
coincides with the identity map outside
\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
and since the event
\mathcal{E}
is assumed to be
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
-local, we have:

\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\Phi_{-t}(\mathbf{X}))=\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X}).

(E.66)

Hence we can ensure that:

\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\Phi_{-t}\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\Phi_{-t}\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}=\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X},

and similarly for the other term (reversing the roles of
-t
and
t
). In conclusion, we obtain:

\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}(\mathbf{X})\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}\leq\left[\int_{\Lambda^{\mathsf{n}}}\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{X})\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})\right)e^{-\beta\mathsf{F}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{m})}\mathtt{d}\mathrm{X}\right]\times e^{\beta\tau}.

Dividing back by the partition function, we obtain (
E.65
). On the other hand, we have by Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\mathtt{1}_{\overline{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}}}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}]\leq\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\left\{\mathrm{ErrAve}\geq\tau\right\}\Big|\mathcal{E}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

In summary, we have obtained
under the extra assumption that
\mathsf{G}
is non-negative
, and for all
\tau\in(0,1)
:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\Phi_{t}\mathbf{X})+\mathsf{G}(\Phi_{-t}\mathbf{X})\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]\times e^{\beta\tau}\\
+\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\left\{\mathrm{ErrAve}\geq\tau\right\}\Big|\mathcal{E}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(E.67)

∎

E.5.1
The general case.

We no longer assume that
\mathsf{G}\geq 0
. For
\sigma>0
, let us introduce the event
\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}:=\left\{\mathsf{G}+\sigma\geq 0\right\}
. We can write:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})+\sigma\big|\mathcal{E}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}]+\sigma=\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})+\sigma\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})+\sigma\right)\mathtt{1}_{\overline{\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}}}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}].

(E.68)

Since
\mathbf{X}\mapsto\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})+\sigma\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}}(\mathbf{X})
is non-negative by construction and has the same local character as
\mathsf{G}
, we may apply the conclusions of the previous paragraph and write:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})+\sigma\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}]\\
\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\sigma\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})+\sigma\right)\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})\big|\mathcal{E}\right]\times e^{\beta\tau}\\
+\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})+\sigma\right)^{2}\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left(\left\{\mathrm{ErrAve}\geq\tau\right\}\Big|\mathcal{E}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(E.69)

where in the last expectation term we have bounded an indicator function by
1
. We would like to get rid of the two remaining indicator functions in the right-hand side.

Using again Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality (and the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}
) we see that:

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\sigma\right)\mathtt{1}_{\overline{\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}}}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})\big|\mathcal{E}]\right|\leq 2\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[|\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})|\geq\sigma\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and similarly after replacing
t
by
-t
. Returning to (
E.68
), we thus obtain:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}]+\sigma\leq\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})\right)\big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\sigma\right)\times e^{\beta\tau}\\
+2e^{\beta\tau}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X}\pm\vec{u})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[|\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}\pm t\vec{u})|\geq\sigma\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
+\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathrm{ErrAve}\geq\tau\big|\mathcal{E}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We may substract
\sigma
on both sides, and observe that we have:

\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})\right)\big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\sigma\right)\times e^{\beta\tau}-\sigma=\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})\right)\big|\mathcal{E}\right]\\
+\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})\right)+\sigma\big|\mathcal{E}\right]\left(e^{\beta\tau}-1\right).

Using Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality one more time to bound the second line, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}[\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X})\big|\mathcal{E}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}+t\vec{u})+\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}-t\vec{u})\right)\big|\mathcal{E}\right]+2\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X}\pm t\vec{u})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(e^{\beta\tau}-1\right)\\
+2e^{\beta\tau}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X}\pm\vec{u})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]+\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[|\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{X}\pm t\vec{u})|\geq\sigma\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
+\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathsf{G}^{2}(\mathbf{X})\Big|\mathcal{E}\right]\times\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{n},\Lambda}^{\beta}\left[\mathrm{ErrAve}\geq\tau\big|\mathcal{E}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Replacing
\mathsf{G}
by
-\mathsf{G}
(which is possible as there is no more a sign constraint on
\mathsf{G}
), we obtain the converse inequality, which yields (
6.5
).
∎

Remark E.23
.

The identity (
E.66
) requires that
\mathcal{E}
be
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\mathrm{bulk}}
-local. One could try to “pass down” as much information as possible but if
\mathcal{E}
is “too rich” then it risks to be perturbed by our localized translation.
